range over the space of mean-zero and square integrable functions that satisfy the restrictions

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[s_{1}(Y\mid S,X)\mid W=1,G=1,S,X]=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[s_{1}(Y^{*}\mid S^{*},X)\mid S^{*}=s,X=x]

\displaystyle=0\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[s_{2}(S\mid X)\mid W=1,G=0,X]=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[s_{2}(S^{*}\mid X)\mid X=x]

\displaystyle=0\penalty\ \text{, and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[s_{3}(G,X)]

\displaystyle=0\penalty\ .

Pathwise Differentiability Conditions.

In the following calculations, it is often easier to work with the complete
data distribution. To that end, we note that for an arbitrary measurable
function
h
, we have that certain conditional means of the observed
distribution equal certain conditional means of the complete data
distribution:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[h(Y,S,X)\mid W=1,G=1,X=x,S=s]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[h(Y^{*},S,X)\mid G=1,X=x,S^{*}=s]\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[h(S,X)\mid G=0,W=1,X=x]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[h(S^{*},X)\mid G=0,X=x]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[h(S^{*},X)\mid G=1,X=x]\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[h(X)\mid G=1]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{h(X)\mid G=1}\right]\penalty\ ,

and, as a result, for
h
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}[h(Y,S,X)\mid W=1,G=1,X,S]\mid W=1,G=0,X]\mid G=1]

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{h(Y^{*},S^{*},X)\mid G=1}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{\frac{G}{\pi}h(Y^{*},S^{*},X)}\right]\penalty\ .

Note that
\theta_{1,1}
is such a functional with
h(y,s,x)=y
.

Observe that the parameter of interest can be written

\displaystyle\theta_{1,1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[Y^{*}\mid G=1]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\mid W=1,G=1,X,S]\mid W=1,G=0,X]\mid G=1]

\displaystyle=\iiint\frac{y}{\pi}p_{\star}(y\mid s,x)p_{\star}(s\mid x)p(x,G=1)\,\text{d}\lambda(y)\text{d}\lambda(s)\text{d}\lambda(x)\penalty\ .

The pathwise derivative of this parameter at 0 on
\mathcal{P}
is given by

\displaystyle\theta_{1,1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}(Y^{*}\mid S^{*},X)\mid G=1]

(A.3)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}(S^{*}\mid X)\mid G=1]+\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}(X,G=1)\mid G=1]\penalty\ .

Now, observe that

\displaystyle G\cdot\ell^{\prime}_{*}(X,G=1)

\displaystyle=G\cdot\ell^{\prime}_{*}(X,G)-G\cdot\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G=1)\penalty\ ,

and that

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G=1)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\ell^{\prime}_{*}(X,G)\mid G=1]\penalty\ .

Thus, each of the terms in (
A.3
) can be written in
terms of conditional scores by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}(Y^{*}\mid S^{*},X)\mid G=1]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\pi^{-1}GY^{*}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(Y^{*}\mid S^{*},X)]\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}(S^{*}\mid X)\mid G=1]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\pi^{-1}GY^{*}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S^{*}\mid X)]\penalty\ \text{, and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}(X,G=1)\mid G=1]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\pi^{-1}GY^{*}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(X,G=1)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\pi^{-1}GY^{*}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(X,G)]-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\pi^{-1}GY^{*}]\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G=1)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\pi^{-1}GY^{*}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(X,G)]-\theta_{1,1}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\ell^{\prime}_{*}(X,G)\mid G=1]\penalty\ ,

respectively.

An influence function for
\theta_{1,1}
is a mean-zero and square-integrable function
\tilde{\psi}_{1,1}(B_{1})
that satisfies the condition

(A.4)

\theta_{1,1}^{\prime}=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tilde{\psi}_{1,1}(B_{1})\ell^{\prime}(B_{1})]\penalty\ .

Hence, by (
A.2
) and (
A.3
), in order
to establish that a mean-zero and square-integrable function
\tilde{\psi}_{1,1}(B_{1})
is an influence function for
\theta_{1,1}
, it suffices to show that
the terms in the right-hand side of (
A.4
) match the
terms in the right-hand side of (
A.3
):

(A.5)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[GY^{*}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(Y^{*}\mid S^{*},X)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\tilde{\psi}_{1,1}(B_{1})GW\ell^{\prime}_{*}(Y\mid S,X)}\right]\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[GY^{*}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S^{*}\mid X)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Bigg[\tilde{\psi}_{1,1}(B_{1})\Bigg(W\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S\mid X)

(A.6)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad-G(1-W)\frac{\int\rho(s,X)p^{\prime}_{*}(s\mid X)\,\text{d}\lambda(s)}{1-\int\rho(s,X)p_{\star}(s\mid X)\,\text{d}\lambda(s)}\Bigg)\Bigg]\penalty\ ,

(A.7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tilde{\psi}_{1,1}(B_{1})\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[GY^{*}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X)]-\theta_{1,1}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X)\mid G=1]\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Bigg[\tilde{\psi}_{1,1}(B_{1})G\Bigg(W\frac{\rho^{\prime}(S,X)}{\rho(S,X)}

(A.8)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad-(1-W)\frac{\int\rho^{\prime}(s,X)p_{\star}(s\mid X)\text{d}\lambda(s)}{1-\int\rho(s,X)p_{\star}(s\mid X)\,\text{d}\lambda(s)}\Bigg)\Bigg]\penalty\ ,

(A.9)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\tilde{\psi}_{1,1}(B_{1})(1-G)\frac{W-\varrho(X)}{\varrho(X)(1-\varrho(X))}\varrho^{\prime}(X)}\right]\penalty\ .

Conjectured Efficient Influence Function.

We begin by considering an element of the tangent space. For the unspecified functions
a(s,x)
and
b(x)
, consider the choices

\displaystyle s_{1}(y,s,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{y-\mu(s,x)}{\pi\rho(s,x)}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle s_{2}(s,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varrho(x)}\frac{a(s,x)}{(1-\gamma(x))}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle s_{3}(g,x)

\displaystyle=g\cdot b(x),

\displaystyle s_{4}(s,x)

\displaystyle=-\rho(s,x)s_{2}(s,x)\penalty\ \text{, and}

(A.10)

\displaystyle s_{5}(x)

\displaystyle=0\penalty\ .

These choices correspond to the following
conjectured efficient influence function

\displaystyle\psi_{1,1}(b_{1})

\displaystyle=gw\cdot s_{1}(y,s,x)+w\cdot s_{2}(s,x)-gw\cdot s_{2}(s,x)+gb(x)

\displaystyle=gw\cdot s_{1}(y,s,x)+(1-g)w\cdot s_{2}(s,x)+g\cdot b(x)

(A.11)

\displaystyle=\frac{gw(y-\mu(s,x))}{\pi\rho(s,x)}+\frac{(1-g)w}{(1-\gamma(x))\varrho(x)}a(s,x)+g\cdot b(x)\penalty\ .

In particular,
s_{4}
is chosen so that certain terms cancel to construct
the term

(1-g)ws_{2}(s,x)
, which does not, prima facie, belong in the tangent
space (
A.2
).

In order to ensure that
\psi_{1,1}(b_{1})
belongs to the tangent space, we
require

(A.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[a(S,X)\mid W=1,G=0,X=x]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[a(S^{*},X)\mid X=x]=0\penalty\ \text{, and}

(A.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[G\cdot b(X)]

\displaystyle=0\penalty\ .

In the sequel, we verify that the choices

\displaystyle a(s,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma(x)}{\pi}(\mu(s,x)-\overline{\mu}(x))\penalty\ \text{, and}

\displaystyle b(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\overline{\mu}(x)-\theta_{1,1}}{\pi}

satisfy these conditions, and, moreover, give rise to the efficient influence function by satisfying
the conditions (
A.5
)–(
A.9
).

Verifying Pathwise Differentiability Conditions.

We begin by stating and proving a useful lemma for handling terms of the form
GWh(Y,S,X)
.

Lemma A.1.

Under the assumptions of
Theorem

3.1
, if
h(y,s,x)
is any
measurable function, then

\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\frac{GW}{\rho(S,X)}h(Y,S,X)}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{Gh(Y^{*},S^{*},X)}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{G\mathbb{E}[h(Y^{*},S^{*},X)\mid S^{*},X]}\right]\penalty\ .

Proof.

Observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\frac{GW}{\rho(S,X)}h(Y,S,X)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\pi\frac{W}{\rho(S,X)}h(Y,S,X)\mid G=1}\right]

\displaystyle=\pi\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{\frac{W}{\rho(S^{*},X)}h(Y^{*},S^{*},X)\mid G=1}\right]

\displaystyle=\pi\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\bigg[\rho^{-1}(S^{*},X)\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[W\mid S^{*},X,G=1]

(
2.4
)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[h(Y^{*},S^{*},X)\mid S^{*},X,G=1]\mid G=1\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\pi h(Y^{*},S^{*},X)\mid G=1]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[Gh(Y^{*},S^{*},X)]\penalty\ ,

giving the first equality. The second equality then follows from

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[Gh(Y^{*},S^{*},X)]

\displaystyle=\pi\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[h(Y^{*},S^{*},X)\mid S^{*},X,G=1]\mid G=1]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[G\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[h(Y^{*},S^{*},X)\mid S^{*},X,G=1]]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[G\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[h(Y^{*},S^{*},X)\mid S^{*},X]]\penalty\ ,

completing the proof.

It now suffices to verify the conditions (
A.5
)–(
A.9
) for
choices of
a(S,X)
and
b(X)
that satisfy (
A.12
) and (
A.13
).
The remainder of the proof verifies these conditions sequentially.

Condition (
A.5
):

Observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\psi_{1,1}(B_{1})GW\ell^{\prime}_{*}(Y\mid S,X)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\frac{GWY}{\pi\rho(S,X)}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(Y\mid S,X)}\right]

(
Lemma

A.1
)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{\pi^{-1}GY^{*}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(Y^{*}\mid S^{*},X)}\right]\penalty\ ,

as required.

Condition (
A.6
):

We derive the restrictions on
a(s,x)
implied by (
A.6
). Observe that the inner product from (
A.6
) can be written

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\psi_{1,1}(B_{1})\left({W\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S\mid X)-G(1-W)\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\rho(S,X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S\mid X)\mid X]}{1-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\rho(S,X)\mid X]}}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\frac{GW(Y-\mu(S,X))\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S\mid X)}{\pi\rho(S,X)}}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\frac{b(X)G(1-W)\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\rho(S,X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S\mid X)\mid X]}{1-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\rho(S,X)\mid X]}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\frac{(1-G)Wa(S,X)}{(1-\gamma(X))\varrho(X)}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S\mid X)}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{GWb(X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S\mid X)}\right]\penalty\ .

Note that the first term can be eliminated by
Lemma

A.1
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\frac{GW(Y-\mu(S,X))\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S\mid X)}{\pi\rho(S,X)}}\right]

\displaystyle=0\penalty\ .

Observe that

\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[G(1-W)\mid X]=P_{\star}(W\mid G=1,X)\gamma(X)=(1-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\rho(S,X)\mid X])\gamma(X),

and thus the second term is given
by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\frac{b(X)G(1-W)\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\rho(S^{*},X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S^{*}\mid X)\mid X]}{1-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\rho(S^{*},X)\mid X]}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{\gamma(X)b(X)\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\rho(S^{*},X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S^{*}\mid X)\mid X]}\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\gamma(X)b(X)\rho(S^{*},X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S^{*}\mid X)]\penalty\ .

Next, observe that

\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[(1-G)W\mid S^{*},X]=(1-\gamma(X))P_{\star}(W=1\mid G=0,S^{*},X)=(1-\gamma(X))\varrho(X),

and therefore the third term can be
written

\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\frac{(1-G)W}{(1-\gamma(X))\varrho(x)}a(S,X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S\mid X)}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{a(S^{*},X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S^{*}\mid X)}\right]\penalty\ .

The fourth term is given by

\mathbb{E}_{P}[GWb(X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S\mid X)]=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\gamma(X)q(S^{*},X)b(X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S\mid X)]\penalty\ ,

which cancels with the second term.

Thus, the condition (
A.6
) is equivalent to

(A.14)

\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S^{*}\mid X)a(S^{*},X)]=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{\pi^{-1}GY^{*}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S^{*}\mid X))}\right].

We proceed by verifying this condition for a choice of
a(S,X)
that satisfies (
A.12
), as required.
Consider the function

a(s,x)=\frac{\gamma(x)}{\pi}(\mu(s,x)-\overline{\mu}(x))\penalty\ .

Then
\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[a(S^{*},X)\mid X]=0
, which satisfies (
A.12
). Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[a(S^{*},X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S^{*}\mid X)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{\frac{\gamma(X)}{\pi}\mu(S^{*},X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S^{*}\mid X)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[G\mid S^{*},X]}{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S^{*}\mid X)\mid S^{*},X}\right]}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{\pi^{-1}GY^{*}\ell_{*}^{\prime}(S^{*}\mid X)}\right]\penalty\ ,

where the second to last equality follows by
2.3
, which verifies (
A.14
) and thereby (
A.6
).

Condition (
A.7
):

We derive the restrictions on
b(x)
implied by (
A.7
). Note
that the conjectured influence function (
A.11
) can be written as

\psi(b_{1})=gw\cdot\alpha(y,s,x)+(1-g)w\cdot\beta(s,x)+gb(x),

where

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[\alpha(Y,S,X)\mid S=s,X=x,G=1,W=1]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\beta(S,X)\mid G=0,W=1,X=x]=0\penalty\ .

We first show that the terms in (
A.7
) involving
\alpha
and
\beta
are zero.
Indeed, for terms involving
\alpha
, we have that

\mathbb{E}_{P}[GW\cdot\alpha(Y,S,X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X)]=\pi\mathbb{E}_{P}[\alpha(Y,S,X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X)\mid G=1,W=1]P(W=1\mid G=1)=0

since
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\alpha(Y,S,X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X;0)\mid G=1,W=1]=0
.
Analogously, we have that

\mathbb{E}_{P}[(1-G)W\beta(S,X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X;0)]=0.

Therefore, (
A.7
) is equivalent to

(A.15)

\frac{1}{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[GY^{*}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X)]-\theta_{1,1}\mathbb{E}[\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X)\mid G=1]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[Gb(X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X)].

We proceed by verifying this condition for a choice of
b(X)
.

Consider the function

b(x)=\frac{1}{\pi}(\overline{\mu}(x)-\theta_{1,1})\penalty\ ,

which satisfies (
A.13
).
We can compute

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[G\pi^{-1}\mu(X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{\frac{G}{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\mu(S^{*},X)\mid G=0,W=1,X]\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X;0)]}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{\frac{G}{\pi}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X;0)\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[Y\mid W=1,G=1,S^{*},X]\mid X]}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{\frac{\gamma(X)}{\pi}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X;0)\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{Y^{*}\mid S^{*},X}\right]\mid X}\right]}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{\frac{\gamma(X)}{\pi}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X;0)Y^{*}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[G\mid Y^{*},X]}{\pi}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X;0)Y^{*}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{\frac{G}{\pi}Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X;0)}\right],

and

-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[G\pi^{-1}\theta_{1,1}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X;0)]=-\theta_{1,1}\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\ell^{\prime}_{*}(1,X;0)\mid G=1]

which verifies (
A.15
) and thereby (
A.7
).

Condition (
A.8
):
Since the
first and second terms
\alpha(Y,S,X)
and
\beta(S,X)
in the conjectured efficient influence function (
A.11
) are mean zero conditional on
S^{*}
and
X
and
X
, respectively, the right-hand side of
(
A.8
) is equal to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{b(X)\left({GW\frac{\rho^{\prime}(S,X)}{\rho(S,X)}-G(1-W)\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\rho^{\prime}(S^{*},X)\mid X]}{1-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\rho(S^{*},X)\mid X]}}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{b(X)\gamma(X)\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{\rho(S^{*},X)\frac{\rho^{\prime}(S^{*},X)}{\rho(S^{*},X)}-(1-\rho(S^{*},X))\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\rho^{\prime}(S^{*},X)\mid X]}{1-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\rho(S^{*},X)\mid X]}\mid G=1,S^{*},X}\right]}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{b(X)\gamma(X)\left({\rho^{\prime}(S^{*},X)-(1-\rho(S^{*},X))\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\rho^{\prime}(S^{*},X)\mid X]}{1-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\rho(S^{*},X)\mid X]}}\right)}\right]=0

by iterated expectation conditional on
X
.

Condition (
A.9
):
The only
term on the right-hand side of (
A.9
) is

\mathbb{E}_{P}[(1-G)W\cdot\beta(S,X)\cdot(W-\rho(X))\delta(X)]

for some function
\delta(\cdot)
, where
(1-G)W\cdot\beta(S,X)

is the second term in the conjectured efficient influence function (
A.11
). We have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[(1-G)W\cdot\beta(S,X)\cdot(W-\varrho(X))\cdot\delta(X)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}[W\cdot\beta(S,X)\cdot(W-\varrho(X))\cdot\delta(X)\mid G=0,X](1-\gamma(X))]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\beta(S^{*},X)\varrho(X)(1-\varrho(X))\cdot\delta(X)]=0

by the condition
\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\beta(S^{*},X)\mid X]=0
, completing the proof.

A.4.
Proof of
Theorem

3.1
. Part 2

We maintain the notation of the proof of
Theorem

3.1
, Part 1.
In particular, we omit the
w
subscript in the nuisance function
\overline{\nu}_{w}(x)
and
let

A_{1}=(Y(1),S(1),W,G,X)=(Y^{*},S^{*},W,G,X)

and
B_{1}=(Y,S,W,G,X)
denote the complete and observed data, respectively. The efficient influence function we construct is

\displaystyle\xi_{1,1}(b)

\displaystyle=\frac{g}{\pi}\left(\frac{\gamma(x)}{\gamma(s,x)}\frac{1-\gamma(s,x)}{1-\gamma(x)}\frac{\varrho(s,x)(y-\nu(S,X))}{\varrho(x)}+(\overline{\nu}_{1}(x)-\theta_{1,1})\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1-g}{\pi}\frac{\gamma(x)}{1-\gamma(x)}\left(\frac{w(\nu(S,X)-\overline{\nu}_{1}(x))}{\varrho(x)}\right)\penalty\ .

Density Factorization.

By
2.1
,
2.6
,
2.3
, and
2.5
, the density
p
of the observed
data distribution admits the factorization

\displaystyle p(b_{1})=\,\,

\displaystyle p(x)\gamma(x)^{g}(1-\gamma(x))^{1-g}

\displaystyle p(y\mid G=1,s,x)^{g}p(s\mid G=1,x)^{g}

(A.16)

\displaystyle p(s\mid G=0,W=1,x)^{w(1-g)}(\varrho(x)^{w}(1-\varrho(x))^{1-w})^{1-g}\penalty\ .

Characterization of the Tangent Space.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be any regular parametric submodel of
\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}
indexed by
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}
, with densities

p_{\varepsilon}:=dP_{\varepsilon}/d\lambda

with
p_{0}=dP/d\lambda
and such that
2.1
,
2.6
,
2.3
, and
2.5
hold for each

P_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{P}
. By the factorization (
A.4
), we obtain the score

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}(b_{1})=\,\,

\displaystyle g\ell^{\prime}(y\mid s,x,G=1)+g\ell^{\prime}(s\mid x,G=1)+w(1-g)\ell^{\prime}(s\mid W=1,x,G=0)+\ell^{\prime}(x)

(A.17)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{g-\gamma(x)}{\gamma(x)(1-\gamma(x))}\gamma^{\prime}(x)+(1-g)\frac{w-\varrho(x)}{\varrho(x)(1-\varrho(x))}\varrho^{\prime}(x).

Thus, the tangent space
\mathcal{T}
is given by the mean-square closure of the linear
span of the functions

\displaystyle s(b_{1})=\,\,

\displaystyle g\cdot s_{1}(y\mid s,x,G=1)+g\cdot s_{2}(s\mid x,G=1)+w(1-g)\cdot s_{3}(s\mid W=1,x,G=0)+s_{4}(x)

(A.18)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{g-\gamma(x)}{\gamma(x)(1-\gamma(x))}s_{5}(x)+(1-g)\frac{w-\varrho(x)}{\varrho(x)(1-\varrho(x))}s_{6}(x)\penalty\ ,

where the functions
s_{1}
through
s_{6}
range over the space of mean-zero and square integrable
functions that satisfy the restrictions

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[s_{1}(Y\mid S,X,G=1)\mid S,X,G=1]

(A.19)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[s_{1}(Y\mid S,X,G=1)\mid S,X]\penalty\ ,

(A.20)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[s_{2}(S\mid X,G=1)\mid X,G=1]\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[s_{3}(S\mid W=1,X,G=0)\mid W=1,X,G=0]

(A.21)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[s_{3}(S^{*}\mid W=1,X,G=0)\mid X,G=0]\penalty\ ,

(A.22)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[s_{4}(X)]\penalty\ ,

and
s_{5},
and
s_{6}
are unconstrained, where (
A.19
) and (
A.21
) follow from
2.6
and
2.1
, respectively.

Pathwise Differentiability Representation.

Recall from the proof of
2.1
that, by
2.1
,
2.6
,
2.3
, and
2.5
, the target parameter
\theta_{1,1}
can be written as

\displaystyle\theta_{1,1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[Y^{*}\mid G=1]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\mid S,X,G=1]\mid W=1,X,G=0}\right]\mid G=1}\right]

\displaystyle=\iiint yp(y\mid s,x,G=1)p(s\mid x,W=1,G=0)p(x\mid G=1)\,d\lambda(y)\,\,d\lambda(s)\,\,d\lambda(x)\penalty\ .

The pathwise derivative of this parameter is given by

\displaystyle\theta_{1,1}^{\prime}=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}[YU(Y,S,X)\mid S,X,G=1]\mid X,W=1,G=0]\mid G=1]

with

\displaystyle U(y,s,x)

\displaystyle=\ell^{\prime}(y\mid s,x,G=1)+\ell^{\prime}(s\mid x,W=1,G=0)+\ell^{\prime}(x\mid G=1)

\displaystyle=\ell^{\prime}(y\mid s,x,G=1)+\ell^{\prime}(s\mid x,W=1,G=0)

\displaystyle+\ell^{\prime}(x)+\frac{\gamma^{\prime}(x)}{\gamma(x)}-\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}[\gamma^{\prime}(X)+\gamma(X)\ell^{\prime}(X)]}{\pi}\penalty\ ,

where final three terms follow from

\displaystyle p^{\prime}(x\mid G=1)=\ell^{\prime}(x\mid G=1)p(x\mid G=1)

and Bayes’ rule in the form of

\ell(x\mid G=1)=\log\gamma(x)+\ell(x)-\log\int\gamma(x)p(x)\,d\lambda(x)\penalty\ .

Observe that, using the definitions of
\nu(s,x)
and
\overline{\nu}(x)
, we may
further simplify the pathwise derivative to give

\displaystyle\theta_{1,1}^{\prime}=\,\,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\ell^{\prime}(Y\mid S,X,G=1)\mid S,X,G=1]\mid X,W=1,G=0]\mid G=1]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}[\nu(S,X)\ell^{\prime}(S\mid X,G=0,W=1)\mid X,W=1,G=0]\mid G=1]

(A.23)

\displaystyle+\pi^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\overline{\nu}(X)\left({\gamma^{\prime}(X)+\gamma(X)\ell^{\prime}(X)}\right)}\right]-\pi^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{P}[\gamma^{\prime}(X)+\gamma(X)\ell^{\prime}(X)]\theta_{1,1}.

An influence function for
\theta_{1,1}
is a mean-zero and square-integrable
function
\tilde{\xi}_{1,1}(B_{1})
that satisfies the condition

(A.24)

\theta^{\prime}_{1,1}=\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\xi}(B_{1})\ell^{\prime}(B_{1})]\penalty\ .

We make a conjecture for such a function and verify that it satisfies this condition and is
an element of the tangent space
\mathcal{T}
, establishing that it is the efficient influence
function.

Conjectured Efficient Influence Function.

We conjecture that the efficient influence function takes the form

(A.25)

\xi_{1,1}(b_{1})=g(y-\nu(s,x))\cdot f_{1}(s,x)+w(1-g)(\nu(s,x)-\overline{\nu}(x))\cdot f_{2}(x)+g\cdot f_{3}(x)\penalty\ ,

for functions
f_{1}(s,x)
,
f_{2}(x)
, and
f_{3}(x)
to be specified, where
f_{3}
will be chosen to satisfy

(A.26)

\mathbb{E}_{P}[f_{3}(X)\mid G=1]=0=\mathbb{E}_{P}[f_{3}(X)\gamma(X)]\penalty\ .

Observe that for this choice,
\xi_{1,1}(b)
will be an element of the
tangent space
\mathcal{T}
. This follows as

s_{1}(y\mid s,x,G=1)=(y-\nu(s,x))f_{1}(s,x)

satisfies (
A.19
), we make the choice

s_{2}(s\mid X,G=1)=0

satisfying (
A.20
),

s_{3}(s\mid W=1,x,G=0)=(\nu(s,x)-\overline{\nu}(x))f_{2}(x)

satisfies (
A.21
),
and that setting
s_{4}(x)=\gamma(x)f_{3}(x)
and
s_{5}(x)=\gamma(x)(1-\gamma(x))f_{3}(x)
yields

gf_{3}(x)=s_{4}(x)+\frac{g-\gamma(x)}{\gamma(x)(1-\gamma(x))}s_{5}(x)\penalty\ ,

satisfying (
A.22
), the mean zero conditions for
s_{4}
and
s_{5}
by (
A.26
), and implicitly satisfying (
A.20
) and the mean zero condition for
s_{6}
.

Verification of Pathwise Differentiability Representation.

In the sequel, we make choices of
f_{1}(s,x)
,
f_{2}(x)
, and
f_{3}(x)
such that the
pathwise derivative of
\theta_{1,1}
satisfies (
A.24
) and (
A.26
), completing the proof.

Our conjecture for
\xi_{1,1}(b_{1})
allows for some simplification of the right-hand side
of (
A.24
). Note that, for any measurable functions
h(S,X)
and
k(X)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[G(Y-\nu(S,X))f_{1}(S,X)h(S,X)]=0=\mathbb{E}_{P}[(1-G)W(\nu(S,X)-\overline{\nu}(X))f_{2}(X)k(X)]

by iterated expectations. Thus, by additionally applying the mean-zero property of the scores
\ell^{\prime}(Y\mid S,X,G=1)
,
\ell^{\prime}(S\mid X,G=1)
, we can simplify the pathwise differentiability condition to give

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\xi_{1,1}(B_{1})\ell^{\prime}(B_{1})}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{G(Y-\nu(S,X))f_{1}(S,X)\ell^{\prime}(Y\mid S,X,G=1)}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{W(1-G)(\nu(S,X)-\overline{\nu}(X))f_{2}(X)\ell^{\prime}(S\mid W=1,X,G=0)}\right]

(A.28)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\left({G\ell^{\prime}(X)+\gamma^{\prime}(X)}\right)f_{3}(X)}\right]\penalty\ .

Therefore, we obtain a set of sufficient conditions for (
A.24
)
by matching each term in (
A.4
) with (
A.23
), giving

(A.29)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{G(Y-\nu(S,X))f_{1}(S,X)\ell^{\prime}(Y\mid S,X,G=1)}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\ell^{\prime}(Y\mid S,X,G=1)\mid S,X,G=1]\mid X,W=1,G=0]\mid G=1]

(A.30)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{W(1-G)(\nu(S,X)-\overline{\nu}(X))f_{2}(X)\ell^{\prime}(S\mid W=1,G=0,X)}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mu(S,X)\ell^{\prime}(S\mid X,W=1,G=0)\mid X,W=1,G=0]\mid G=1]

(A.31)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\left({G\ell^{\prime}(X)+\gamma^{\prime}(X)}\right)f_{3}(X)}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\pi^{-1}\overline{\nu}(X)\left({\gamma^{\prime}(X)+\ell^{\prime}(X)\gamma(X)}\right)}\right]-\pi^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{P}[\gamma^{\prime}(X)+\gamma(X)\ell^{\prime}(X)]\theta_{1,1}.

We complete the proof by considering each condition sequentially, making
appropriate choices of
f_{1}(s,x)
,
f_{2}(x)
, and
f_{3}(x)
, where we note
that each function appears in only one term.

Condition (
A.29
):

Evaluating the left-hand side of (
A.29
) we find that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{G(Y-\nu(S,X))f_{1}(S,X)\ell^{\prime}(Y\mid S,X,G=1)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[GYf_{1}(S,X)\ell^{\prime}(Y\mid S,X,G=1)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\gamma(X)\mathbb{E}_{P}[f_{1}(S,X)\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\ell^{\prime}(Y\mid S,X,G=1)\mid S,X,G=1]\mid X,G=1]].

Thus, if we choose

f_{1}(s,x)=\frac{p(s\mid x,W=1,G=0)}{\gamma(x)p(s\mid x,G=1)}\frac{p(x\mid G=1)}{p(x)}

to change the measure of the two outer expectations, (
A.29
) is satisfied. Bayes’ rule calculations
show that

f_{1}(s,x)=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{\varrho(s,x)}{\varrho(x)}\frac{1-\gamma(s,x)}{\gamma(s,x)}\frac{\gamma(x)}{1-\gamma(x)}\penalty\ .

Condition (
A.30
):

Observe that, for any measurable function
h(s,x)
, we have that

\mathbb{E}_{P}[W(1-G)h(S,X)]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\varrho(X)(1-\gamma(X))\mathbb{E}_{P}[h(S,X)\mid X,W=1,G=0]].

Also, recall that since
\ell^{\prime}(S\mid X,W=1,G=0)
is a conditional
score, it is conditionally orthogonal to any function of
X
; that is, for any measurable function
h(x)
,

\mathbb{E}_{P}[h(X)\ell^{\prime}(S\mid X,W=1,G=0)\mid X,W=1,G=0]=0\penalty\ .

With the above two observations in mind, evaluating the left-hand side of
(
A.30
), we find that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{W(1-G)(\nu(S,X)-\overline{\nu}(X))f_{2}(X)\ell^{\prime}(S\mid W=1,G=0,X)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\varrho(x)(1-\gamma(X))\mathbb{E}_{P}[(\nu(S,X)-\overline{\nu}(X))f_{2}(X)\ell^{\prime}(S\mid W=1,G=0,X)]}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[\varrho(x)(1-\gamma(X))f_{2}(X)\mathbb{E}_{P}[\nu(S,X)\ell^{\prime}(S\mid X,W=1,G=0)\mid X,W=1,G=0]\big]\penalty\ .

Thus, again, if we choose

f_{2}(x)=\frac{p(x\mid G=1)}{p(x)\varrho(x)(1-\gamma(x))}=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{1}{\varrho(x)}\frac{\gamma(x)}{1-\gamma(x)}\penalty\ ,

again to change the measure of the outer expectation, then (
A.30
) is satisfied.

Condition (
A.31
):

Lastly, consider the choice

f_{3}(x)=\frac{\overline{\nu}(X)-\theta_{1,1}}{\pi}\penalty\ ,

which satisfies (
A.26
). In this case, we find
that the left-hand side of (
A.31
) is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\left({G\ell^{\prime}(X)+\gamma^{\prime}(X)}\right)f_{3}(X)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\frac{\gamma(X)\ell^{\prime}(X)+\gamma^{\prime}(X)}{\pi}\overline{\nu}(X)}\right]-\frac{1}{\pi}\mathbb{E}[\gamma(X)\ell^{\prime}(X)+\gamma^{\prime}(X)]\theta_{1,1},

which is exactly the right-hand side of (
A.31
).

Collecting the above choices for
f_{1}(s,x)
,
f_{2}(x)
, and
f_{3}(x)
gives the efficient influence function

\displaystyle\xi_{1,1}(b)

\displaystyle=\frac{g}{\pi}\left(\frac{\gamma(x)}{\gamma(s,x)}\frac{1-\gamma(s,x)}{1-\gamma(x)}\frac{\varrho(s,x)(y-\nu(S,X))}{\varrho(x)}+(\mu_{1}(x)-\theta_{1,1})\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1-g}{\pi}\frac{\gamma(x)}{1-\gamma(x)}\left(\frac{w(\nu(S,X)-\mu_{1}(x))}{\varrho(x)}\right)\penalty\ ,

as required.

A.5.
Proof of Theorem
3.2
. Part 1

We maintain the notation from the proof of
Theorem

3.1
. Let
\tilde{\psi}_{1,1}(b_{1})
be an influence function for
\theta_{1,1}
. It is necessarily that case that

\tilde{\psi}_{1,1}(b_{1})=\psi_{1,1}(b_{1})+f(b_{1})\penalty\ ,

where
\psi_{1,1}(b_{1})
is the efficient influence function in derived in the proof of
Theorem

3.1
and
f(b_{1})
is mean-zero and orthogonal to the tangent space
\mathcal{T}
, also defined in the proof of
Theorem

3.1
. Moreover, we may
write

\displaystyle f(b_{1})

\displaystyle=gwf_{1}(y,s,x)+(1-g)wf_{2}(s,x)+g(1-w)f_{3}(x)

(A.32)

\displaystyle+(1-g)(1-w)f_{4}(x)+A\penalty\ ,

where
A
is a constant making
f
mean-zero.

The orthogonality between
f(b_{1})
and the tangent space
\mathcal{T}
implies the following set of conditions that are analogous to the conditions (
A.5
) through (
A.9
) defined in the proof of
Theorem

3.1
:

(A.33)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{f(B_{1})GW\ell^{\prime}_{*}(Y\mid S,X)}\right]\penalty\ ,

(A.34)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{f(B_{1})\left({W\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S\mid X)-G(1-W)\frac{\int\rho(s,X)p^{\prime}_{*}(s\mid X)\,d\lambda(s)}{1-\int\rho(s,X)p_{\star}(s\mid X)\,d\lambda(s)}}\right)}\right]\penalty\ ,

(A.35)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[f(B_{1})\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X)]\penalty\ ,

(A.36)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{f(B_{1})G\left({W\frac{\rho^{\prime}(S,X)}{\rho(S,X)}-(1-W)\frac{\int\rho^{\prime}(s,X)p_{\star}(s\mid X)d\nu_{S}(s)}{1-\int\rho(s,X)p_{\star}(s\mid X)\,d\nu_{S}(s)}}\right)}\right]\penalty\ ,

(A.37)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{f(B_{1})(1-G)\frac{W-\varrho(X)}{\varrho(X)(1-\varrho(X))}\varrho^{\prime}(X)}\right]\penalty\ .

In the sequel, we characterize
f
by deriving restrictions from each of these conditions for particular choices of the scores
\ell_{*}^{\prime}(\cdot)
,
\varrho^{\prime}(x)
, and
\rho^{\prime}(s,x)
.

Combining (
A.33
) and (
A.32
) yields

\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{GWf_{1}(Y,S,X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(Y\mid S,X)}\right]=0\penalty\ .

As
\ell^{\prime}_{*}(Y\mid S,X)
ranges over all conditionally mean-zero, square integrable functions, we may pick

\ell^{\prime}_{*}(Y\mid S,X)=f_{1}(Y,S,X)-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f_{1}(Y,S,X)\mid G=1,W=1,S,X],

from which we find that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{GWf_{1}(Y,S,X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(Y\mid S,X)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{GWf_{1}^{2}(Y,S,X)-f_{1}(Y,S,X)\mathbb{E}_{P}[GWf_{1}(Y,S,X)\mid G=1,W=1,S,X]}\right]\penalty\ ,

and so
f_{1}(Y,S,X)=f_{1}(S,X)
does not depend on
Y
.

Next, observe that plugging (
A.32
) into (
A.36
), yields

0=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{\gamma(X)\left({f_{1}(S^{*},X)\rho^{\prime}(S^{*},X)-f_{3}(X)\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\rho^{\prime}(S^{*},X)\mid X]}\right)}\right]

after simplification via law of iterated expectations. Choosing

\rho^{\prime}(S^{*},X)=\gamma(X)^{-1}\left({f_{1}(S^{*},X)-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[f_{1}(S^{*},X)\mid X]}\right)

establishes that

0=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[f_{1}^{2}(S^{*},X)-f_{1}(S^{*},X)\mathbb{E}[f_{1}(S^{*},X)\mid X]]\penalty\ ,

and so
f_{1}(S,X)=f_{1}(X)
does not depend on
S
. This then implies that
f_{1}(x)=f_{3}(x)
by law of iterated expectations.

Now, plugging (
A.32
) into (
A.34
)
yields

0=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{(1-\gamma(X))\varrho(X)f_{2}(S^{*},X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S^{*}\mid X)}\right]\penalty\ ,

again after simplification via law of iterated expectations. As we may choose

\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S^{*}\mid X)=f_{2}(S^{*},X)-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[f_{2}(S^{*},X)\mid X]\penalty\ ,

we find similarly that
f_{2}
is a function only of
X
.

Plugging (
A.32
) into (
A.37
), gives the condition

0=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{(1-\gamma(X))(f_{2}(X)-f_{4}(X))\varrho^{\prime}(X)}\right]

where picking
\varrho^{\prime}(X)=f_{2}(X)-f_{4}(X)
implies that
f_{4}(x)=f_{2}(x)
.

Lastly, plugging (
A.32
) into (
A.35
), we find

0=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X)(Gf_{3}(X)+(1-G)f_{2}(X))}\right].

We then find that that

\mathbb{E}_{P}[\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X)]=0=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\gamma(X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(1,X)+(1-\gamma(X))\ell^{\prime}_{*}(0,X)]\penalty\ ,

and so
f_{1}(x)=f_{2}(x)=f_{3}(x)=f_{4}(x)=C
almost everywhere, for some constant
C
. Hence,
f(b)=0
almost everywhere
and the space of influence functions is a singleton.

A.6.
Proof of
Theorem

3.2
. Part 2

We demonstrate that
\xi_{1,1}
is the unique influence function for

\theta_{1,1}
. An analogous argument verifies the statement for
\theta_{0,1}
, and therefore for

\tau_{1}
. We maintain the notation from the proof of
Theorem

3.1
, Part 2.

Let
\tilde{\xi}(b_{1})
be an influence function for
\theta_{1,1}
. It is necessarily the case that

\tilde{\xi}_{1,1}(b_{1})=\xi_{1,1}(b_{1})+f(b_{1})\penalty\ ,

where
\xi_{1,1}(b_{1})
is the efficient influence function derived in
Theorem

3.1
, Part 2,
and
f(b_{1})
is mean-zero and orthogonal to the tangent space
\mathcal{T}
, defined in the proof of
Theorem

3.1
, Part 2. We may without loss of generality write

f(b)=g\cdot f_{1}(y,s,x)+(1-g)w\cdot f_{2}(s,x)+(1-g)(1-w)\cdot f_{3}(x)+A\penalty\ ,

where
A
is some constant making
f
mean-zero.

The orthogonality between
f
and the tangent space
\mathcal{T}
implies that

(A.38)

\mathbb{E}_{P}[f(B_{1})\ell^{\prime}(B_{1})]=0

for any
\ell^{\prime}(\cdot)
corresponding to a score of a parametric submodel.

We first argue that
f_{1}(y,s,x)=f_{1}(x)
does not depend on
y
or
s
.
Consider all free components in the score (
A.17
), and set
everything except for
\ell^{\prime}(y\mid s,x,G=1)
to zero. Then the orthogonality
condition (
A.38
) becomes

\mathbb{E}_{P}[Gf_{1}(Y,S,X)\ell^{\prime}(Y\mid S,X,G=1)]=0\penalty\ .

Picking
\ell^{\prime}(Y\mid S,X,G=1)=f_{1}(Y,S,X)-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f_{1}(Y)\mid S,X,G=1]

implies that
\mathbb{E}_{P}[Gf_{1}(Y,S,X)\ell^{\prime}(Y\mid S,X,G=1)]>0
unless
f_{1}(Y,S,X)=\mathbb{E}[f_{1}(Y)\mid S,X,G=1]
almost surely. Therefore
f_{1}(Y,S,X)
does not
depend on
Y
.
Similarly, if we set every free component in (
A.17
)
except for
\ell^{\prime}(S\mid X,G=1)
to zero, then choosing
\ell^{\prime}(S\mid X,G=1)=f_{1}(S,X)-\mathbb{E}[f(S,X)\mid X,G=1]
again implies that
f_{1}
only depends on
X
.
A similar argument with the score component
\ell^{\prime}(S\mid W=1,X,G=0)
shows
that
f_{2}(s,x)=f_{2}(x)
depends only on
x
. Therefore,
f(b)
must take the form

f(b)=g\cdot f_{1}(x)+(1-g)w\cdot f_{2}(x)+(1-g)(1-w)\cdot f_{3}(x).

The orthogonality condition (
A.38
) then takes the form

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[f(B)\ell^{\prime}(X)]+\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{f(B)\frac{G-\gamma(X)}{\gamma(X)(1-\gamma(X))}\gamma^{\prime}(X)}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{(1-G)(Wf_{2}(X)+(1-W)f_{3}(X))\frac{W-\varrho(x)}{\varrho(x)(1-\varrho(x))}\varrho^{\prime}(x)}\right]

for any valid choices of
\ell^{\prime}(x),\gamma^{\prime}(x),\rho^{\prime}(x)
, as the other terms
are zero by the mean-zero conditions on the conditional scores.

Observe that the third term can be simplified as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{(1-G)(Wf_{2}(X)+(1-W)f_{3}(X))\frac{W-\varrho(x)}{\varrho(x)(1-\varrho(x))}\varrho^{\prime}(x)}\right]

(A.39)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\frac{f_{2}(X)-f_{3}(X)}{1-\pi}\varrho^{\prime}(x)\mid G=0}\right].

Picking
\varrho^{\prime}(x)=f_{2}(x)-f_{3}(x),\ell^{\prime}(x)=\gamma^{\prime}(x)=0
implies that
f_{2}(x)=f_{3}(x)
.
Thus
f(b)=gf_{1}(x)+(1-g)f_{2}(x)
. Working with the second term in (
A.39
) (i.e. setting
\ell^{\prime}(x)=0=\rho^{\prime}(x)
and considering choices of
\gamma^{\prime}(x)
) yields that

\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{f(B)\frac{G-\gamma(X)}{\gamma(X)(1-\gamma(X))}\gamma^{\prime}(X)}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[(f_{1}(X)-f_{2}(X))\gamma^{\prime}(X)]

Picking
\gamma^{\prime}(x)=f_{1}(x)-f_{2}(x)
shows that
f_{1}(x)=f_{2}(x)
. Therefore, from the first term in (
A.39
), we
find that for all
\ell^{\prime}
s.t.
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\ell^{\prime}(X)]=0
,

\mathbb{E}_{P}[f(B_{1})\ell^{\prime}(X)]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[f_{1}(X)\ell^{\prime}(X)]=0\implies f_{1}(X)=0.

implies that
f_{1}(X)=0
almost everywhere. Hence,
f(b_{1})=0
almost everywhere, completing the proof.

A.7.
Proof of
Theorem

4.1

In this section, we provide proofs for both
Theorem

4.1
and for the analogous result for the statistical surrogacy model, stated as follows.

Theorem A.2.

Let
\mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}
be the set of all probability distributions

P
for
\{B_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
that satisfy the Statistical Surrogacy Model stated in
2.2

in addition to
4.1
. If
4.2
holds for
\mathcal{P}
, then

(A.40)

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\hat{\tau}_{1,\mathsf{DML}}-\tau_{1})\overset{d}{\to}\mathcal{N}(0,V_{1}^{\star\star})

uniformly over
P\in\mathcal{P}
, where
\hat{\tau}_{1,\mathsf{DML}}
is defined in
4.1
,
V_{1}^{\star\star}
is defined in
3.1
, and
\overset{d}{\to}
denotes convergence in distribution. Moreover, we have that

(A.41)

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{1}^{\star\star}=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i\in I_{\ell}}\left(\xi_{1}(B_{i},\hat{\tau}_{1,\mathsf{DML}},\hat{\varphi}(I_{\ell}^{c}))\right)^{2}\overset{p}{\to}V_{1}^{\star\star}

uniformly over
P\in\mathcal{P}
, where
\overset{p}{\to}
denotes convergence in probability. As a result, we obtain the uniform asymptotic validity of the confidence intervals

(A.42)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\Big|P\left(\tau_{1}\in\left[\hat{\tau}_{1,\mathsf{DML}}\pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\sqrt{\hat{V}_{1}^{\star\star}/n}\right]\right)-(1-\alpha)\Big|=0\penalty\ ,

where
z_{1-\alpha/2}
is the
1-\alpha/2
quantile of the standard normal distribution.

To ease notation for the purposed of this proof, we change the notation introduced in

3.1
. In particular, partition the collection of nuisance functions
\eta
appearing as an argument
in
\psi_{1}(B,\tau_{1},\eta)
into long-term outcome means and propensity scores by
\eta=(\omega,\kappa)
, where we include the parameter
\pi
in the propensity scores
\kappa
to ease notation. Similarly
partition the collection of nuisance function
\varphi
appearing as an argument
in
\xi_{1}(B,\tau_{1},\varphi)
into
\varphi=(\vartheta,\zeta)
.

Observe that the efficient influence functions
\psi_{1}(b,\tau_{1},\eta)
and
\xi_{1}(b,\tau_{1},\varphi)
can be decomposed linearly into

\psi_{1}(b,\tau_{1},\eta)=\psi_{1}^{\alpha}(b,\eta)\cdot\tau_{1}+\psi_{1}^{\beta}(b,\eta)\quad\text{and}\quad\xi_{1}(b,\tau_{1},\varphi=\xi_{1}^{\alpha}(b,\varphi)\cdot\tau_{1}+\xi_{1}^{\beta}(b,\varphi)

where

\displaystyle\psi_{1}^{\alpha}(b,\eta)

\displaystyle=\xi_{1}^{a}(b,\varphi)=-\frac{g}{\pi}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\psi_{1}^{\beta}(b,\eta)

\displaystyle=\frac{g}{\pi}\left(\frac{w(y-\mu_{1}(s,x))}{\rho_{1}(s,x)}-\frac{(1-w)(y-\mu_{0}(s,x))}{\rho_{0}(s,x)}+(\overline{\mu}_{1}(x)-\overline{\mu}_{0}(x))\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1-g}{\pi}\left(\frac{\gamma(x)}{1-\gamma(x)}\left(\frac{w(\mu_{1}(s,x)-\overline{\mu}_{1}(x))}{\varrho(x)}-\frac{(1-w)(\mu_{0}(s,x)-\overline{\mu}_{0}(x))}{1-\varrho(x)}\right)\right),\text{ and}

\displaystyle\xi_{1}^{\beta}(b,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\frac{g}{\pi}\left(\frac{\gamma(x)}{\gamma(s,x)}\frac{1-\gamma(s,x)}{1-\gamma(x)}\frac{(\varrho(s,x)-\varrho(x))(y-\nu(s,x))}{\varrho(x)(1-\varrho(x)}+(\overline{\nu}_{1}(x)-\overline{\nu}_{0}(x))\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{1-g}{\pi}\left(\frac{\gamma(x)}{1-\gamma(x)}\left(\frac{w(\nu(s,x)-\overline{\nu}_{1}(x))}{\varrho(x)}-\frac{(1-w)(\nu(s,x)-\overline{\nu}_{0}(x))}{1-\varrho(x)}\right)\right).

Consequently, it will suffice to verify the conditions of Theorem 3.1 of
Chernozhukov et al., 2018
. In particular, it will suffice to verify Assumption 3.1 and Assumption 3.2 therein.

Verification of Assumption 3.1 of
Chernozhukov et al., 2018
.

Both
\psi_{1}(b,\tau_{1},\eta)
and
\xi_{1}(b,\tau_{1},\varphi)
are mean-zero, satisfying condition (a). By the above decomposition, both
\psi_{1}(b,\tau_{1},\eta)
and
\xi_{1}(b,\tau_{1},\varphi)
are linear in
\tau_{1}
satisfying condition (b). It is clear that the mappings
\eta\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\psi_{1}(b,\tau_{1},\eta)\right]
and
\varphi\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\xi_{1}(b,\tau_{1},\varphi)\right]
are twice continuously Gateaux differentiable, as they are both smooth functions by the strict-overlap
2.2
, satisfying condition (c). Both
\psi_{1}(b,\tau_{1},\eta)
and
\xi_{1}(b,\tau_{1},\varphi)
satisfy the “Neyman orthogonality” condition of
Chernozhukov et al., 2018
by the orthogonality conditions (
A.4
) and (
A.24
), satisfying condition (d). Finally, as

\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[-\frac{g}{\pi}\right]=-1\penalty\ ,

the singular values of
\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\psi_{1}(b,\tau_{1},\eta)\right]
and
\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\xi_{1}(b,\tau_{1},\varphi))\right]
are bounded away from zero, satisfying condition (e).

Verification of Assumption 3.2 of
Chernozhukov et al., 2018
.

We note that
4.2
can be restated as: For all

P\in\mathcal{P}
, with probability at least
1-\Delta_{n}
, the nuisance
estimator
\hat{\eta}_{n}
(resp.
\hat{\varphi}_{n}
) belongs to a

realization set

\mathcal{R}_{P,n}
. The realization set
\mathcal{R}_{P,n}
contains nuisance parameter values
\tilde{\eta}=(\tilde{\omega},\tilde{\kappa})
such that

(1)

\|\tilde{\eta}-\eta\|_{P,q}\leq C
,

(2)

\|\tilde{\eta}-\eta\|_{P,2}\leq\delta_{n}
,
\|\tilde{\kappa}-1/2\|_{P,\infty}\leq 1/2-\epsilon
, and

(3)

\|\tilde{\omega}-\omega\|_{P,2}\cdot\|\tilde{\kappa}-\kappa\|_{P,2}\leq\delta_{n}/n^{1/2}

Define the realization set
\mathcal{R}_{P,n}
analogously for the
Statistical Surrogacy Model, where the nuisance function
\varphi
replaces
\eta
.

Condition (a).
The nuisance parameter estimators are elements of their respective realization sets with probability
1-\Delta_{N}
by
4.2
, verifying condition (a).

Condition (b).
To verify condition (b), in the Latent Unconfounded Treatment Model, we must demonstrate that

\displaystyle\sup_{\eta\in\mathcal{R}_{P,n}}\left(\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\|\psi_{1}^{\alpha}\left(B;\eta\right)\|^{q}\right]\right)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime},\text{ and}

\displaystyle\sup_{\eta\in\mathcal{R}_{P,n}}\left(\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\|\psi_{1}\left(B;\tau_{1},\eta\right)\|^{q}\right]\right)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}

for some
C^{\prime}
. Analogous conditions suffice for the Statistical Surrogacy Model. Verification of the first
inequality is immediate, as

\sup_{\eta\in\mathcal{R}_{P,n}}\left(\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\|\psi_{1}^{\alpha}\left(B;\eta\right)\|^{q}\right]\right)^{1/q}=1\quad\text{and}\quad\sup_{\varphi\in\mathcal{R}_{P,n}}\left(\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\|\xi_{1}^{a}\left(B;\varphi\right)\|^{q}\right]\right)^{1/q}=1\penalty\ .

For the Latent Unconfounded Treatment Model,
by the equalities

\mu_{w}\left(s,x\right)=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[Y\left(w\right)\mid S\left(w\right)=s,X=x\right]\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{\mu}_{w}\left(X\right)=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[Y\left(w\right)\mid X=x\right]\penalty\ ,

we have that

\displaystyle\|\mu_{w}\left(S,X\right)\|_{P,q}\vee\|\overline{\mu}_{w}\left(X\right)\|_{P,q}

\displaystyle\leq\|Y\left(w\right)\|_{P,q}\leq C

by Jensen’s inequality and
4.1
, where
\vee
denotes the
join binary operation. Thus, for any
\tilde{\eta}\in\mathcal{R}_{P,n}
, we
have that

\displaystyle\|\psi_{1}\left(B;\tau_{1},\tilde{\eta}\right)\|_{P,q}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}(\|\tilde{\mu}_{1}\left(S,X\right)\|_{P,q}+\|\tilde{\mu}_{0}\left(S,X\right)\|_{P,q}

\displaystyle\quad+\|\tilde{\overline{\mu}}_{1}\left(X\right)\|_{P,q}+\|\tilde{\overline{\mu}}_{0}\left(X\right)\|_{P,q}+\|Y\|_{P,q}+|\tau_{1}|)

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}(\|\mu_{1}\left(S,X\right)-\tilde{\mu}_{1}\left(S,X\right)\|_{P,q}+\|\tilde{\mu}_{0}\left(S,X\right)-\tilde{\mu}_{0}\left(S,X\right)\|_{P,q}

\displaystyle\quad+\|\mu_{1}\left(S,X\right)\|_{P,q}+\|\mu_{0}\left(S,X\right)\|_{P,q}+\|\overline{\mu}_{1}\left(X\right)-\tilde{\overline{\mu}}_{1}\left(X\right)\|_{P,q}

\displaystyle\quad+\|\overline{\mu}_{1}\left(X\right)-\tilde{\overline{\mu}}_{0}\left(X\right)\|_{P,q}+\|\tilde{\overline{\mu}}_{1}\left(X\right)\|_{P,q}+\|\tilde{\overline{\mu}}_{0}\left(X\right)\|_{P,q}

\displaystyle\quad+\|Y\|_{P,q}+|\tau_{1}|)\leq C_{2},

where the constants
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
depend only on
C

and
\epsilon
, by the
2.2
, the triangle inequality, and

4.2
.

For the Statistical Surrogacy Model,
by the equalities

\nu\left(s,x\right)=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[Y\mid S=x,X=x,G=1\right]\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{\nu}_{w}\left(X\right)=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[Y\left(w\right)\mid X=x,W=w,G=0\right]

and the bounds

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[Y\mid S=x,X=x\right]\|_{P,q}

\displaystyle\geq\epsilon^{1/q}\|\nu\left(s,x\right)\|_{P,q},\text{ and}

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[Y\left(w\right)\mid X=x\right]\|_{P,q}\geq\epsilon^{1/q}\|\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[Y\left(w\right)\mid X=x,W=w\right]\|_{P,q}

\displaystyle\geq\epsilon^{2/q}\|\overline{\nu}_{w}\left(X\right)\|_{P,q}\penalty\ ,

again we have that

\displaystyle\|\nu\left(S,X\right)\|_{P,q}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{-1/q}\|Y\|_{P,q}\leq 2\epsilon^{-1/q}\left(1-\epsilon\right)C\quad\text{and}\quad\|\overline{\nu}_{w}\left(X\right)\|_{P,q}\leq\epsilon^{-2/q}C

by Jensen’s inequality,
2.2
, and
4.1
. Thus,
for any
\tilde{\varphi}\in\mathcal{R}_{P,n}
, we have by an analogous
argument that

\displaystyle\|\xi_{1}\left(B;\tau_{1},\tilde{\varphi}\right)\|_{P,q}

\displaystyle\leq C_{4},

where
C_{4}
depends only on
C
,
\epsilon
, and
q
, by Assumption 3.1, the triangle inequality, and
4.2
.

Condition (c).

To verify condition (c), in the Latent Unconfounded Treatment Model, we must demonstrate that

(A.43)

\displaystyle\sup_{\tilde{\eta}\in\mathcal{R}_{P,n}}\|E_{P}\left[\psi_{1}^{\alpha}\left(B;\tilde{\eta}\right)-\psi_{1}^{\alpha}\left(B;\eta\right)\right]\|_{P,2}

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{\prime}_{n},

(A.44)

\displaystyle\sup_{\tilde{\eta}\in\mathcal{R}_{P,n}}\left(E_{P}\left[\psi_{1}\left(B;\tau_{1},\tilde{\eta}\right)-\psi_{1}\left(B;\tau_{1},\eta\right)\right]^{2}\|^{1/2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{\prime}_{n},\text{ and}

(A.45)

\displaystyle\sup_{h_{0}\in\left(0,1\right),\tilde{\eta}\in\mathcal{R}_{P,n}}\bigg\lVert\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial h^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\psi_{1}\left(B;\tau_{1},\eta+h\left(\tilde{\eta}-\eta\right)\right)\right]|_{h=h_{0}}\bigg\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{\prime}_{n}/\sqrt{n}\penalty\ ,

for some sequence of positive constants
\{\delta^{\prime}_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
converging to zero. Analogous conditions suffice for the Statistical Surrogacy Model. Verification of (
A.43
)
is immediate as

\|E_{P}\left[\psi_{1}^{\alpha}\left(B;\tilde{\eta}\right)-\psi_{1}^{\alpha}\left(B;\eta_{0}\right)\right]\|_{P,2}=\|\pi^{-1}-\tilde{\pi}^{-1}\|_{P,2}\leq\epsilon^{-2}\delta_{n}

and

\|E_{P}\left[\psi_{1}^{a}\left(B;\tilde{\eta}\right)-\psi_{1}^{a}\left(B;\eta_{0}\right)\right]\|=\|\pi^{-1}-\tilde{\pi}^{-1}\|_{P,2}\leq\epsilon^{-2}\delta_{n}

for any
\tilde{\eta}
and
\tilde{\varphi}
in their respective
realization sets.

For the Latent Unconfounded Treatment Model,
observe that for any
\tilde{\eta}\in\mathcal{R}_{P,n}
, the triangle
inequality gives the bound

\|\psi_{1}\left(B;\tau_{1},\tilde{\eta}\right)-\psi_{1}\left(B;\tau_{1},\eta_{0}\right)\|_{P,2}\leq\mathcal{I}_{1,1}+\mathcal{I}_{1,0}+\mathcal{I}_{2}+\mathcal{I}_{3}+\mathcal{I}_{4,1}+\mathcal{I}_{4,0}

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1,1}

\displaystyle=\bigg\lVert\frac{G}{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\frac{W\left(Y-\tilde{\mu}_{1}\left(S,X\right)\right)}{\tilde{\rho}_{1}\left(S,X\right)}\right)-\frac{G}{\pi}\left(\frac{W\left(Y-\mu_{1}\left(S,X\right)\right)}{\rho_{1}\left(S,X\right)}\right)\bigg\rVert_{P,2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1,0}

\displaystyle=\bigg\lVert\frac{G}{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\frac{\left(1-W\right)\left(Y-\tilde{\mu}_{0}\left(S,X\right)\right)}{\tilde{\rho}_{0}\left(S,X\right)}\right)-\frac{g}{\pi}\left(\frac{\left(1-W\right)\left(Y-\mu_{0}\left(S,X\right)\right)}{\rho_{0}\left(S,X\right)}\right)\bigg\rVert_{P,2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}

\displaystyle=\bigg\lVert\frac{G}{\tilde{\pi}}\tilde{\overline{\mu}}_{1}\left(X\right)-\frac{G}{\pi}\overline{\mu}_{1}\left(x\right)\bigg\|_{P,2}+\bigg\|\frac{G}{\tilde{\pi}}\tilde{\overline{\mu}}_{0}\left(x\right)-\frac{G}{\pi}\overline{\mu}_{0}\left(x\right)\bigg\rVert_{P,2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{3}

\displaystyle=\bigg\lVert\left(\pi^{-1}-\tilde{\pi}^{-1}\right)G\tau_{1}\bigg\rVert_{P,2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{4,1}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\frac{G}{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\frac{\tilde{\gamma}\left(X\right)}{1-\tilde{\gamma}\left(X\right)}\frac{W\left(\tilde{\mu}_{1}\left(S,X\right)-\tilde{\overline{\mu}}_{1}\left(X\right)\right)}{\tilde{\varrho}\left(X\right)}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{G}{\pi}\left(\frac{\gamma\left(X\right)}{1-\gamma\left(X\right)}\frac{W\left(\mu_{1}\left(S,X\right)-\overline{\mu}_{1}\left(X\right)\right)}{\varrho\left(X\right)}\right)\bigg\|_{P,2},\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{4,0}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\frac{G}{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\frac{\tilde{\gamma}\left(X\right)}{1-\tilde{\gamma}\left(X\right)}\frac{\left(1-W\right)\left(\tilde{\mu}_{0}\left(S,X\right)-\tilde{\overline{\mu}}_{0}\left(X\right)\right)}{\tilde{\varrho}\left(X\right)}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{G}{\pi}\left(\frac{\gamma\left(X\right)}{1-\gamma\left(X\right)}\frac{\left(1-W\right)\left(\mu_{0}\left(S,X\right)-\overline{\mu}_{0}\left(X\right)\right)}{1-\tilde{\varrho}\left(X\right)}\right)\bigg\|_{P,2}\penalty\ .

Observe that

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1,1}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{-2}\bigg\lVert\pi\left(\frac{W\left(Y-\tilde{\mu}_{1}\left(S,X\right)\right)}{\tilde{\rho}_{1}\left(S,X\right)}\right)-\tilde{\pi}\left(\frac{W\left(Y-\mu_{1}\left(S,X\right)\right)}{\rho_{1}\left(S,X\right)}\right)\bigg\rVert_{P,2}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{-2}\bigg\lVert\frac{W\left(Y-\tilde{\mu}_{1}\left(S,X\right)\right)}{\tilde{\rho}_{1}\left(S,X\right)}-\frac{W\left(Y-\mu_{1}\left(S,X\right)\right)}{\rho_{1}\left(S,X\right)}\bigg\rVert_{P,2}+\bigg\lVert\left(\pi-\tilde{\pi}\right)\left(\frac{W\left(Y-\mu_{1}\left(S,X\right)\right)}{\rho_{1}\left(S,X\right)}\right)\bigg\rVert_{P,2}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{-4}\left(\|\left(\rho_{1}\left(S,X\right)-\tilde{\rho}_{1}\left(S,X\right)\right)\left(Y-\mu_{1}\left(S,X\right)\right)\|_{P,2}+\|\rho_{1}\left(S,X\right)\left(\mu_{1}\left(S,X\right)-\tilde{\mu}_{1}\left(S,X\right)\right)\|_{P,2}\right)

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{-3}\bigg\lVert\left(\pi-\tilde{\pi}\right)\left(Y-\mu_{1}\left(S,X\right)\right)\bigg\rVert_{P,2}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{-4}\left(\sqrt{C}\|\rho_{1}\left(S,X\right)-\tilde{\rho}_{1}\left(S,X\right)\|_{P,2}+\|\mu_{1}\left(S,X\right)-\tilde{\mu}_{1}\left(S,X\right)\|_{P,2}\right)+\epsilon^{-3}\sqrt{C}\|\pi-\tilde{\pi}\|_{P,2}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{-3}\left(\epsilon^{-1}\left(\sqrt{C}+1\right)+\sqrt{C}\right)\delta_{n},

where the third inequality follows from
\mathbb{E}\left[\sigma^{2}\left(S,X\right)\mid X\right]\leq C

and
\rho_{1}\left(S,X\right)\leq 1
. An analogous argument verifies
that
\mathcal{I}_{1,0}\leq\epsilon^{-3}\left(\epsilon^{-1}\left(\sqrt{C}+1\right)+\sqrt{C}\right)\delta_{n}
.
Likewise, we can see that

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{-2}\left(\|\tilde{\overline{\mu}}_{1}\left(X\right)-\overline{\mu}_{1}\left(x\right)\|_{P,2}+\|\tilde{\overline{\mu}}_{0}\left(X\right)-\overline{\mu}_{0}\left(X\right)\|_{P,2}\right)\leq 2\epsilon^{-2}\delta_{n}

and that

\mathcal{I}_{3}\leq\epsilon^{-2}\|\left(\tilde{\pi}-\pi\right)\tau_{1}\|_{P,2}\leq\epsilon^{-2}2C\delta_{n}.

Finally, if we define
\upsilon\left(X\right)
by

\frac{1}{\upsilon\left(X\right)}=\frac{\gamma\left(X\right)}{\pi\left(1-\gamma\left(X\right)\right)\varrho\left(X\right)},

and observe that
\upsilon\left(X\right)\geq\epsilon^{3}/\left(1-\epsilon\right)=\epsilon_{\star}
,
we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{4,1}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{\star}^{-2}\|\upsilon\left(X\right)\left(\tilde{\mu}_{1}\left(S,X\right)-\tilde{\overline{\mu}}_{1}\left(X\right)\right)-\tilde{\upsilon}\left(X\right)\left(\mu_{1}\left(S,X\right)-\overline{\mu}_{1}\left(X\right)\right)\|_{P,2}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{\star}^{-2}\|\upsilon\left(X\right)\left(\tilde{\mu}_{1}\left(S,X\right)-\mu_{1}\left(S,X\right)+\mu_{1}\left(S,X\right)-\overline{\mu}_{1}\left(X\right)+\overline{\mu}_{1}\left(X\right)-\tilde{\overline{\mu}}_{1}\left(X\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\tilde{\upsilon}\left(X\right)\left(\mu_{1}\left(S,X\right)-\overline{\mu}_{1}\left(X\right)\right)\|_{P,2}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{\star}^{-4}\Big(\|\upsilon\left(X\right)\left(\tilde{\mu}_{1}\left(S,X\right)-\mu_{1}\left(S,X\right)\right)\|_{P,2}+\|\left(\upsilon\left(X\right)-\tilde{\upsilon}\left(X\right)\right)\left(\mu_{1}\left(S,X\right)-\overline{\mu}_{1}\left(X\right)\right)\|_{P,2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\|\upsilon\left(X\right)\left(\overline{\mu}_{1}\left(X\right)-\tilde{\overline{\mu}}_{1}\left(X\right)\right)\|_{P,2}\Big)\leq C_{5}\delta_{n}\penalty\ ,

where
C_{5}
depends only on
\epsilon
and
C
, with an analogous
argument giving
\mathcal{I}_{4,1}\leq C_{5}\delta_{N}
, verifying
(
A.44
) for the Latent Unconfounded Treatment Model.

In turn, for the Statistical Surrogacy Model, observe that for any
\tilde{\varphi}\in\mathcal{R}_{P,n}
,
the triangle inequality gives the bound

\|\xi_{1}\left(B;\tau_{1},\tilde{\varphi}\right)-\xi_{1}\left(B;\tau_{1},\varphi_{0}\right)\|_{P,2}\leq\mathcal{J}_{1,1}+\mathcal{J}_{1,0}+\mathcal{J}_{2}+\mathcal{J}_{3}+\mathcal{J}_{4,1}+\mathcal{J}_{4,0}

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{1,1}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\frac{G}{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\frac{\tilde{\gamma}\left(X\right)}{\tilde{\gamma}\left(S,X\right)}\frac{1-\tilde{\gamma}\left(X\right)}{1-\tilde{\gamma}\left(S,X\right)}\frac{\tilde{\varrho}\left(S,X\right)\left(Y-\tilde{\nu}\left(S,X\right)\right)}{\tilde{\varrho}\left(X\right)}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{G}{\pi}\left(\frac{\gamma\left(X\right)}{\gamma\left(S,X\right)}\frac{1-\gamma\left(X\right)}{1-\gamma\left(S,X\right)}\frac{\varrho\left(S,X\right)\left(Y-\nu\left(S,X\right)\right)}{\varrho\left(X\right)}\right)\bigg\|_{P,2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{1,0}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\frac{G}{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\frac{\tilde{\gamma}\left(X\right)}{\tilde{\gamma}\left(S,X\right)}\frac{1-\tilde{\gamma}\left(X\right)}{1-\tilde{\gamma}\left(S,X\right)}\frac{\left(1-\tilde{\varrho}\left(S,X\right)\right)\left(Y-\tilde{\nu}\left(S,X\right)\right)}{1-\tilde{\varrho}\left(X\right)}\right),

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{G}{\pi}\left(\frac{\gamma\left(X\right)}{\gamma\left(S,X\right)}\frac{1-\gamma\left(X\right)}{1-\gamma\left(S,X\right)}\frac{\left(1-\varrho\left(S,X\right)\right)\left(y-\nu\left(S,X\right)\right)}{1-\varrho\left(X\right)}\right)\bigg\|_{P,2}

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\frac{G}{\tilde{\pi}}\tilde{\overline{\nu}}_{1}\left(X\right)-\frac{G}{\pi}\overline{\nu}_{1}\left(x\right)\bigg\|_{P,2}+\bigg\|\frac{G}{\tilde{\pi}}\tilde{\overline{\nu}}_{0}\left(x\right)-\frac{G}{\pi}\overline{\nu}_{0}\left(x\right)\bigg\|_{P,2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{3}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\left(\pi^{-1}-\tilde{\pi}^{-1}\right)G\tau_{1}\bigg\|_{P,2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{4,1}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\frac{G}{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\frac{W\left(\tilde{\nu}_{1}\left(S,X\right)-\tilde{\overline{\nu}}_{1}\left(X\right)\right)}{\tilde{\varrho}\left(X\right)}\right)-\frac{G}{\pi}\left(\frac{W\left(\nu_{1}\left(S,X\right)-\overline{\nu}_{1}\left(X\right)\right)}{\varrho\left(X\right)}\right)\bigg\|_{P,2},\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{4,0}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\frac{G}{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\frac{\left(1-W\right)\left(\tilde{\nu}_{0}\left(S,X\right)-\tilde{\overline{\nu}}_{0}\left(X\right)\right)}{\tilde{\varrho}\left(X\right)}\right)-\frac{G}{\pi}\left(\frac{\left(1-W\right)\left(\nu_{0}\left(S,X\right)-\overline{\nu}_{0}\left(X\right)\right)}{1-\tilde{\varrho}\left(X\right)}\right)\bigg\|_{P,2}.

Bounds for each term follow similar arguments to their analogues for
the case where treatment is observed in the observational sample. We omit their explicit derivation to avoid repetition, concluding the verification of (
A.44
).

The expressions for the first and second Gateaux derivatives

\frac{\partial}{\partial h}\mathbb{E}_{p}\left[\psi_{1}\left(B;\tau_{1},\eta+h\left(\tilde{\eta}-\eta\right)\right)\right]\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial h^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{p}\left[\psi_{1}\left(B;\tau_{1},\eta+h\left(\tilde{\eta}-\eta\right)\right)\right]

as well as

\frac{\partial}{\partial h}\mathbb{E}_{p}\left[\xi_{1}\left(B;\tau_{1},\varphi+h\left(\tilde{\varphi}-\varphi\right)\right)\right]\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial h^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{p}\left[\xi_{1}\left(B;\tau_{1},\varphi+h\left(\tilde{\varphi}-\varphi\right)\right)\right]

are very tedious. In particular, the second Gateaux derivatives each
take more than a page to display in the present formatting. They are
available upon request, but are omitted for the sake of clarity. The
second Gateaux derivatives can be written as a sum of several terms,
each upper bounded, by Hölder’s inequality, by the product between the
L_{2}\left(P\right)
norms
of the differences of components of
\tilde{\omega}-\omega
and

\tilde{\kappa}-\kappa
, if treatment is observed in the observational
sample, and
\tilde{\vartheta}-\vartheta
and
\tilde{\zeta}-\zeta
,
if treatment is not observed in the observational sample. Thus, by
4.2
, (
A.45
) is satisfied in each case, completing the verification of condition (c). We refer the reader to the final passage in the proof of Theorem 5.1 of
Chernozhukov et al., 2018
for an example of an argument with an identical structure for the substantially simpler context of the average treatment effect
under ignorability.

Condition (d).

Finally, to verify condition (d), we must demonstrate that
V_{1}^{\star}

and
V_{1}^{\star\star}
are non degenerate. Observe that

\displaystyle V_{1}^{\star}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\frac{\gamma\left(X\right)}{\pi}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}\left(S,X\right)}{\rho_{1}\left(S,X\right)}+\frac{\sigma_{0}^{2}\left(S,X\right)}{\rho_{0}\left(S,X\right)}+\left(\overline{\mu}_{1}\left(X\right)+\overline{\mu}_{0}\left(X\right)-\tau_{1}\right)^{2}+\Gamma_{0}\left(S,X\right)+\Gamma_{1}\left(S,X\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\epsilon}{\pi\left(1-\epsilon\right)}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\sigma_{1}^{2}\left(S,X\right)+\sigma_{0}^{2}\left(S,X\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{\pi\left(1-\epsilon\right)^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\left(\mu_{1}\left(S,X\right)-\overline{\mu}_{1}\left(X\right)\right)^{2}+\left(\mu_{0}\left(S,X\right)-\overline{\mu}_{0}\left(X\right)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\geq 2\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\pi\left(1-\epsilon\right)}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{\pi\left(1-\epsilon\right)^{2}}\right)c,

as required. An analogous argument holds for
V_{1}^{\star\star}
, completing the proof.

A.8.
Proof of
Theorem

4.2

We begin by giving an explicit construction for the estimator

\hat{\theta}_{1,1}(g_{\mathsf{w}})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{G_{i}}{\hat{\pi}_{n}}\frac{W_{i}Y_{i}}{\hat{\rho}_{1n}(S_{i},X_{i})}

for the parameter
\theta_{1,1}=\mathbb{E}[Y(1)\mid G=1]
. We then state sufficient conditions for its asymptotic linearity and Gaussianity. The construction and sufficient conditions for the corresponding estimator of the parameter
\theta_{1,0}=\mathbb{E}[Y(0)\mid G=1]
are analogous. To ease notation, we drop the subscript
\mathsf{w}
from the parameter
\zeta_{\mathsf{w}}
.
We will use the standard empirical process notation
\mathbb{P}_{n}f=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(B_{i})
,
\mathbb{P}f=\mathbb{E}_{P}[f(B_{i})]
, and
\mathbb{G}_{n}=\sqrt{n}(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})
, where expectations are taken over
B_{i}
and not over
f
if
f
is random. Our assumptions and argument closely follow
Chen and Liao, 2015
.

Observe that

\rho_{1}(s,x)=\frac{\zeta_{1}(s,x)}{1-\zeta_{1}(s,x)}\zeta_{2}(x)\frac{1-\zeta_{3}(x)}{\zeta_{3}(x)}\penalty\ ,

where

\displaystyle\zeta_{1}(s,x)

\displaystyle=P(G=1\mid S=s,W=1,X=x),

\displaystyle\zeta_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=P(W=1\mid X=x,G=1),\quad\text{and}\quad\zeta_{3}(x)=P(G=1\mid W=1,X=x).

The parameter
\zeta=(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\zeta_{3})
can be estimated with the sieve extremum estimator

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}_{n}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{h\in\mathcal{H}_{n}}\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{i}(G_{i}-h_{1}(S_{i},X_{i}))^{2}+G_{i}(W_{i}-h_{2}(X_{i}))^{2}+W_{i}(G_{i}-h_{3}(X_{i}))^{2}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{h\in\mathcal{H}_{n}}\mathbb{P}_{n}L(\cdot,h)\penalty\ ,

where
\mathcal{H}_{n}
is a sieve approximation of the parameter space for

\zeta\in\mathcal{H}
. The estimator

\hat{\rho}_{1n}
is then derived from plugging
\hat{\zeta}_{n}
into (
A.8
). The parametric nuisance parameter
\pi
can be estimated by its sample
analogue
\hat{\pi}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}G_{i}.

We begin by imposing a set of regularity conditions on the data generating distribution
P
, the estimator
\hat{\zeta}_{n}
, and the parameter space
\mathcal{H}
.

Assumption A.1.

Assume that

(1)

The parameter
\theta_{1,1}\in\mathrm{int}(\Theta)\subset\mathbb{R}
, where
\Theta
is compact.

(2)

The nuisance parameters
\pi
and
\zeta
are bounded between
\varepsilon
and
1-\varepsilon
,
\lambda
-almost surely, for some fixed constant
0<\varepsilon<1/2
.

(3)

The long-term outcome
Y_{i}
has a finite fourth
moment, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}_{P}[|Y_{i}|^{4}]<\infty
.

(4)

The estimator
\hat{\zeta}_{n}
is
n^{-1/4}
-consistent in
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
, where
\|\zeta\|_{\infty}=\max_{j}\|\zeta_{j}\|_{\infty}
, in the sense that there exists a sequence
0\leq\delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/2})
such that

\delta_{n}^{-1}\lVert\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta\rVert^{2}_{\infty}\overset{p}{\to}0

and entries of
\hat{\zeta}_{n}(\cdot)
belong to
[\epsilon,1-\epsilon]
,
\lambda
-almost surely.

(5)

The parameter space
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\times\mathcal{H}_{2}\times\mathcal{H}_{3}
is a product of Donsker classes.

Next, we introduce more structure on the sieve space

\mathcal{H}_{n}
. We assume that
\mathcal{H}_{n}
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
in
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty}
as
n\to\infty
. Define

\displaystyle\bm{\Gamma}[v]

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\zeta}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\frac{GWY}{\pi\rho_{1}}}\right][v]

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\frac{GWY}{\pi\frac{\zeta_{1}+tv_{1}}{1-\zeta_{1}-tv_{1}}(\zeta_{2}+tv_{2})\frac{1-\zeta_{3}-tv_{3}}{\zeta_{3}+tv_{3}}}}\right]\bigg\rvert_{t=0}

(A.47)

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\frac{GWY}{\pi\rho_{1}(S,X)}\left\{{\frac{1}{\zeta_{1}(1-\zeta_{1})}v_{1}+\frac{1}{\zeta_{2}}v_{2}+\frac{1}{\zeta_{3}(1-\zeta_{3})}v_{3}}\right\}}\right]

as the pathwise derivative of
\theta_{1,1}
in
\zeta
in the direction of
v=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})
.
Similarly, define the inner product

\langle v,u\rangle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[W(v_{1}u_{1}+v_{3}u_{3})+Gv_{2}u_{2}]=\frac{\partial{{}^{2}}}{\partial{t_{1}\partial t_{2}}}\mathbb{E}_{P}[L(B,\zeta+t_{1}v+t_{2}u)]\bigg\rvert_{t_{1}=t_{2}=0}

as the cross-derivative of the first-step criterion function
L
and let
\lVert\cdot\rVert
denote the norm induced by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
.
Define

\mathcal{V}=\mathrm{CL}\left({\left\{{h-\zeta=(h_{1}-\zeta_{1},h_{2}-\zeta_{2},h_{3}-\zeta_{3}):h\in\mathcal{H}}\right\}}\right)

as the
closed linear span of the re-centered parameter space under
\lVert\cdot\rVert
.
19
19

19

\mathrm{CL}
takes the
closed linear span under
\lVert\cdot\rVert
.

Note that
\bm{\Gamma}:\mathcal{V}\to\mathbb{R}
is a bounded linear
functional on
\mathcal{V}
.
Define
v^{*}\in\mathcal{V}
as the Riesz representer of
\bm{\Gamma}
in
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
:
That is, for all
w\in\mathcal{V}
, we have that

\langle v^{*},w\rangle=\Gamma[w].

Similarly, let
\zeta_{n}=\argmin_{h\in\mathcal{H}_{n}}\lVert h-\zeta\rVert
be the
projection of
\zeta
to the sieve space in
\lVert\cdot\rVert
. Define

\mathcal{V}_{n}=\mathrm{CL}\left({\left\{{h-\zeta_{n}:h\in\mathcal{H}_{n}}\right\}}\right)

as the re-centered sieve space. Lastly, let
v^{*}_{n}
be the Riesz representer in
\mathcal{V}_{n}
of

\bm{\Gamma}[\cdot]
in
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. We refer to
v^{*}
as the Riesz representer and

v^{*}_{n}
as the sieve Riesz representer. Note that these objects are defined under the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
, which is different from how Riesz representers are defined in our
efficiency calculations.

With this notation in place, we impose the following conditions.

Assumption A.2.

(1)

The rate condition
\lVert\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta\rVert\lVert v_{n}^{*}-v^{*}\rVert=o_{p}(n^{-1/2})
is satisfied.

(2)

Recall
\delta_{n}
from
A.1
(4). Assume that the function classes

\mathcal{F}_{n}=\left\{{h-\zeta:h\in\mathcal{H}_{n},\lVert h-\zeta\rVert_{\infty}<\delta_{n}^{1/2}}\right\}

have finite uniform entropy
integrals
J(1,\mathcal{F}_{n})
. The entropy integral
J(1,\mathcal{F}_{n})
is defined as

J(1,\mathcal{F}_{n})=\sup_{Q}\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{1+\log N\left({\epsilon\delta_{n}^{1/2},\mathcal{F}_{n},L_{2}(Q)}\right)}\,d\epsilon<C<\infty,

where
Q
ranges over all discrete distributions,
N(\epsilon\delta_{n}^{1/2},\mathcal{F},L_{2}(Q))
is
a covering number in
L_{2}(Q)
-norm. We choose the envelope function
F
for
\mathcal{F}_{n}
as the constant
function
F=\delta_{n}^{1/2}
.

A.1
and
A.2
are sufficient for the asymptotic linearity and Gaussianity of
\hat{\theta}_{1,1}(g_{\mathsf{w}})
. The following assumption collects these conditions for reference in the main text.

Assumption A.3.

A.1
and
A.2
, in addition to their analogues that pertain to the corresponding weighted estimator of
\theta_{1,0}=\mathbb{E}[Y(0)\mid G=1]
, hold.

Under
A.3
, we can readily verify Theorem
4.2
, stated in the
main text using Corollary
A.1
stated in
Section

A.8.1
.

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.

By an application of
A.1
(and its analogue for
\mathbb{E}[Y(0)\mid G=1]
), we have
that

\sqrt{n}(\hat{\tau}_{1}(g_{\mathsf{w}})-\tau_{1})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{1}(B_{i},\tau_{1},\eta)+o_{p}(1).

where
\psi_{1}(\cdot)
is the efficient influence function for
\tau_{1}
under the Latent Unconfounded Treatment Model, derived in
Theorem

3.1
. As we have assumed that
\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y_{i}^{4}]<\infty
and by
and strict overlap, i.e.,
2.2
, we can readily verify that

\mathbb{E}_{P}[|\psi_{1}(B_{i},\tau_{1},\eta)|^{2+\epsilon}]<\infty

for some
\epsilon>0
. As a result, by the Lyapunov central limit theorem,

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{1}(B_{i},\tau_{1},\eta)\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}(0,V_{1}^{\star}),

by the definition of
V_{1}^{\star}
given in
3.1
. The proof is complete with an application of the continuous mapping theorem and Slutsky’s theorem.

A.8.1.
Asymptotic linearity

Define

\Delta_{i}[v]=-\frac{\partial{L(B_{i},\zeta)}}{\partial{\zeta}}[v]=W_{i}(G_{i}-\zeta_{1})v_{1}+G_{i}(W_{i}-\zeta_{2})v_{2}+W_{i}(G_{i}-\zeta_{3})v_{3}.

as the negative pathwise derivative of the first-stage loss function with respect to
\zeta
in the direction of
v
, evaluated at the true nuisance
\zeta
. For a test value
\tilde{\zeta}
of the nuisance parameter
\zeta
,
let an infeasible moment condition be

\tilde{g}_{1}(B_{i},\theta_{1,1},\chi)=\frac{G_{i}W_{i}Y_{i}}{\pi\tilde{\rho}_{1}(S,X)}-\theta_{1,1},

where
\tilde{\rho}_{1}
is derived from plugging
\chi
into (
A.8
).
\tilde{g}_{1}
is a moment condition that assumes knowledge of the true
\pi
.

Let
\tilde{\theta}_{1,1}
be the
Z
estimator based on the moment condition

(A.51)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{n}\tilde{g}_{1}(\cdot,\tilde{\theta}_{1,1},\hat{\zeta}_{n})=0.

The following theorem applies Theorem 2.1 from
Chen and Liao, 2015
to the moment
condition (
A.51
). The result is key in that it shows that the influence of the sieve
estimation of
\zeta
on
\theta_{1n}
is asymptotically linear and is given by
\Delta_{i}[v^{*}]
.

Theorem A.3.

Under
A.1
and
A.2
, we have that

\sqrt{n}(\tilde{\theta}_{1,1}-\theta_{1,1})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{g}_{1}(B_{i},\theta,\zeta)+\Delta_{i}[v^{*}]+o_{p}(1).

Corollary A.1.

Under
A.1
and
A.2
, we have that

(A.52)

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{1,1}-\theta_{1,1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[{\tilde{g}_{1}(B_{i},\theta,\zeta)+\Delta_{i}[v^{*}]+\theta_{1,1}-\frac{G_{i}\theta_{1,1}}{\pi}}\right]+o_{p}(1)

(A.53)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{1,1}(B_{i})+o_{p}(1)\penalty\ ,

where
\psi_{1,1}(\cdot)
is the efficient influence function for
\theta_{1,1}
under the Latent Unconfounded Treatment Model, derived in the the proof of
Theorem

3.1
.

Proof of
A.1
.

Note that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{1,1}-\theta_{1,1})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}\left({\frac{\pi}{\hat{\pi}_{n}}-1}\right)\theta_{1,1}+\sqrt{n}(\tilde{\theta}_{1,1}-\theta_{1,1})+\sqrt{n}(\tilde{\theta}_{1,1}-\theta_{1,1})\left({\frac{\pi}{\hat{\pi}_{n}}-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[{\tilde{g}_{1}(B_{i},\theta_{1,1},\zeta)+\Delta_{i}[v^{*}]+\theta_{1,1}-\frac{G_{i}\theta_{1,1}}{\pi}}\right]+o_{p}(1)\penalty\ ,

where the second equality follows from
Lemma

A.6
, stated in
Section

A.8.3
, and

Theorem

A.3
. This verifies (
A.52
). The equality (
A.53
) follows from

Lemma

A.5
, stated in
Section

A.8.2
.

Towards verifying
Theorem

A.3
, we state the assumptions and main result of
Chen and Liao, 2015
. For that purpose, let
g(\cdot,\theta,\zeta)
be a generic moment
condition, with true parameter values
(\theta,\zeta)
and generic parameter values
(\vartheta,h)
. Let
L(\cdot,\cdot)
a generic first-stage loss function. Define the
generic counterparts of other objects analogously, relative to
g
and to
L
. Let

\bm{\Gamma}_{1}(\vartheta)
be the derivative of
\mathbb{E}[g(\cdot,\theta,\zeta)]
in
\theta
,
evaluated at
\vartheta
. Let
\bm{\Gamma}_{2}(\vartheta)[v]
be the pathwise derivative of
\mathbb{E}[g(\cdot,\theta,\zeta)]
in
\zeta
, evaluated at
\vartheta
and
\zeta
, in the direction

v
.
Chen and Liao, 2015
maintain the following high-level assumptions.

Assumption A.4
(Assumption A.1 in
Chen and Liao, 2015
)
.

Assume that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\lVert v^{*}_{n}\rVert<\infty
.
Additionally assume that the estimator
\hat{\zeta}_{n}
satisfies:

(1)

\lvert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{i}[v^{*}_{n}]-\langle v^{*}_{n},\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta\rangle\rvert=o_{p}(n^{-1/2})

(2)

\lVert\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta\rVert\cdot\lVert v^{*}_{n}-v^{*}\rVert=o_{p}(n^{-1/2})

Assumption A.5
(Assumption A.2 in
Chen and Liao, 2015
)
.

Assume
\theta
is in the interior of
\Theta
and
\tilde{\theta}\overset{p}{\to}\theta
. Assume
additionally that

(1)

The derivative

\bm{\Gamma}_{1}(\vartheta)=\frac{\partial{\mathbb{E}_{P}[g(B_{i},\vartheta,\zeta)]}}{\partial{\vartheta}}

exists in a neighborhood of

\theta
and is continuous at
\theta
.

(2)

The pathwise derivative

\bm{\Gamma}_{2}(\vartheta)[v]=\frac{\partial{\mathbb{E}_{P}[g(B_{i},\vartheta,\zeta)]}}{\partial{\zeta}}[v]

exists in all directions
v
and satisfies

\lvert\bm{\Gamma}_{2}(\vartheta)[v]-\bm{\Gamma}_{2}(\theta)[v]\rvert\leq|\vartheta-\theta|\cdot o(1).

(3)

We have

\lvert\mathbb{P}_{n}g(\cdot,\vartheta,\hat{\zeta}_{n})-\mathbb{P}g(\cdot,\vartheta,\zeta)-\bm{\Gamma}_{2}(\vartheta)[\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta]\rvert=o_{p}(n^{-1/2}).

for all
\vartheta=\theta+o(1)

(4)

For all sequences
0<\kappa_{n}=o(1)
,

\sup_{\lvert\vartheta-\theta\rvert<\kappa_{n},\lVert h-\zeta\rVert_{\infty}<\kappa_{n}}\lvert\mathbb{G}_{n}[g(\cdot,\vartheta,h)-g(\cdot,\theta,\zeta)]\rvert=o_{p}(1).

Theorem A.4
(Theorem 2.1 in
Chen and Liao, 2015
)
.

Let
\tilde{\theta}_{n}
be the estimator based on
g
: That is,

\mathbb{P}_{n}g(\cdot,\tilde{\theta}_{n},\hat{\zeta}_{n})=0.

Under
A.4
and
A.5
,

\sqrt{n}(\tilde{\theta}_{n}-\theta)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(B_{i},\theta,\zeta)+\Delta_{i}[v^{*}].

Proof intuition for
Theorem

A.4
.

The influence of the first-step estimation is of the form

\sqrt{n}\bm{\Gamma}_{2}(\theta)[\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta]=\sqrt{n}\langle\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta,v^{*}_{k}\rangle,

for which we use
A.5
. The linearity of
\langle\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta,v^{*}_{k}\rangle
is
assumed in
A.4
(1). As
n\to\infty
,
v_{k}^{*}\to v^{*}
, and
A.4
(2) controls the corresponding difference
between
\langle v_{n}^{*},\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta\rangle
and
\langle v^{*},\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta\rangle
.

Proof of
Theorem

A.3
.

It suffices to show that, in our setting, with
g
chosen as
\tilde{g}
,
A.4
and
A.5
follow from
A.1
and
A.2
.
A.4
(1) follows from
Lemma

A.7
, stated in
Section

A.8.3
.

A.4
(2) is assumed as
A.2
(1). Lastly, note that
\mathcal{V}_{n}
consist of
functions that are uniformly bounded, since entries of
\zeta
are functions taking values
in
[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]
. Thus,
\lim_{n\to\infty}\lVert v_{n}^{*}\rVert\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\lVert v_{n}^{*}\rVert_{\infty}<\infty
. This verifies
A.4
.

Next, the interiority and consistency in
A.5
are assumed by
A.1
(1) and shown in
Lemma

A.6
. Our moment function is
(
A.8.1
), whose derivatives are particularly simple.
The derivative
\bm{\Gamma}_{1}(\vartheta)=-1
is a constant, and thus
A.5
(1) is
satisfied. The derivative
\bm{\Gamma}_{2}(\vartheta)[v]
in our setting does not depend on

\vartheta
, and so
A.5
(2) is satisfied.

Since
\bm{\Gamma}_{2}
does not depend on
\vartheta
and neither does
\mathbb{P}_{n}g(\cdot,\vartheta,\hat{\zeta}_{n})-\mathbb{P}g(\cdot,\vartheta,\zeta)
, we can simplify
A.5
(3)
into

\lvert\mathbb{P}\tilde{g}_{1}(\cdot,\theta_{1,1},\hat{\zeta}_{n})-\mathbb{P}\tilde{g}_{1}(\cdot,\theta_{1,1},\zeta)-\bm{\Gamma}[\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta]\rvert=o_{p}(n^{-1/2})

Consider the functional

t\mapsto\mathbb{P}\tilde{g}_{1}(\cdot,\theta,\zeta+t(\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta)).

By Taylor’s theorem, there exists a
\tilde{t}\in(0,1)
such that

\mathbb{P}\tilde{g}_{1}(\cdot,\theta_{1,1},\hat{\zeta}_{n})-\mathbb{P}\tilde{g}_{1}(\cdot,\theta_{1,1},\zeta)-\bm{\Gamma}[\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta]=\frac{\partial{{}^{2}\mathbb{P}\tilde{g}_{1}(\cdot,\theta,\zeta+t(\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta))}}{\partial{t^{2}}}\bigg\rvert_{t=\tilde{t}}.

It is tedious, but not difficult, to see that the second derivative term is quadratic in
the deviation
\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta
, and therefore satisfies

\bigg\lvert\frac{\partial{{}^{2}\mathbb{P}\tilde{g}_{1}(\cdot,\theta_{1,1},\zeta+t(\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta))}}{\partial{t^{2}}}\bigg\rvert_{t=\tilde{t}}\bigg\rvert\leq C(\epsilon)\mathbb{E}|Y|\cdot\lVert\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta\rVert_{\infty}^{2}=o_{p}(n^{-1/2}).

for some
C(\epsilon)
that depends on
\epsilon
. Thus, it suffices to assume

A.1
(2,4).

Lastly, the stochastic equicontinuity condition
A.5
(4) is implied by the
condition that

\mathcal{G}=\left\{{\tilde{g}_{1}(\cdot,\vartheta,h)-g(\cdot,\theta_{1,1},\zeta):\vartheta\in\Theta,h\in\mathcal{H}}\right\}

is Donsker. Note that by
A.1
(2) and the fact
that
\Theta
is compact,
g(\cdot,\vartheta,h)-g(\cdot,\theta_{1,1},\zeta)
is a composition of Lipschitz functions (i.e. addition, multiplication,
and division) of
\vartheta,h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}
. Therefore, since Lipschitz functions of
Donsker classes are Donsker (Example 19.20 in
Van der Vaart, 1998
), it suffices to
assume that
\mathcal{H}
is a product of Donsker classes (
A.1
(5)).

A.8.2.
The influence function for
\hat{\theta}_{1,1}
is the efficient influence function

In this section, we show that the influence function expression given in (
A.52
) matches the efficient influence function for
\theta_{1,1}
, derived in the the proof of
Theorem

3.1
, yielding (
A.53
).

Lemma A.5.

Under
A.1
and
A.2
, we have that

\psi_{1,1}(B_{i})=\tilde{g}_{1}(B_{i},\theta_{1,1},\zeta)+\Delta_{i}[v^{*}]+\theta_{1,1}-\frac{G_{i}\theta_{1,1}}{\pi}.

Proof.

By inspection of
\psi_{1,1}(B_{i})
, it will suffice to show that

-\Delta_{i}[v^{*}]=\frac{G_{i}W_{i}\mu_{1}(S_{i},X_{i})}{\pi\rho_{1}(S_{i},X_{i})}-(1-G_{i})W_{i}\frac{1}{\pi\varrho(X_{i})}\frac{\gamma(X_{i})}{1-\gamma(X_{i})}(\mu_{1}(S_{i},X_{i})-\overline{\mu}_{1}(X_{i}))-\frac{G_{i}}{\pi}\overline{\mu}_{1}(X).

It is not difficult to verify that the choices

\displaystyle-v_{1}^{*}(S,X)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\zeta_{1}(S,X)\pi}\frac{\gamma(X)}{1-\gamma(X)}\frac{1}{\varrho(S,X)}\mu_{1}(S,X)=\frac{\mu(S,X)}{\pi\rho_{1}(S,X)}\frac{1}{1-\zeta_{1}(S,X)}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle-v_{2}^{*}(X)

\displaystyle=\frac{\overline{\mu}_{1}(X)}{\zeta_{2}(X)\pi}\penalty\ ,\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle-v_{3}^{*}(X)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\varrho(X)}\frac{\gamma(X)}{1-\gamma(X)}\frac{1}{\zeta_{3}(X)\pi}\overline{\mu}_{1}(X)\penalty\ ,

ensure that (
A.8.2
) is satisfied by (
A.8.1
). These terms can be derived by
inspecting terms in (
A.8.2
)
that are of the form
Wf(X),Wf(S,X),Gf(X)
, and matching them with corresponding
expressions in (
A.8.1
).

The following identity
(
A.8.2
) is useful for this verification. Note that, by Bayes rule,

\zeta_{3}(x)=\frac{\gamma(x)\zeta_{2}(x)}{\gamma(x)\zeta_{2}(x)+(1-\gamma(x))\varrho(x)}\implies\frac{\zeta_{3}(x)}{1-\zeta_{3}(x)}=\frac{\gamma(x)\zeta_{2}(x)}{(1-\gamma(x))\varrho(x)}

and so it is convenient to note that

\rho_{1}(s,x)\frac{1-\zeta_{1}(s,x)}{\zeta_{1}(s,x)}=\zeta_{2}(x)\frac{1-\zeta_{3}(x)}{\zeta_{3}(x)}=\varrho(x)\frac{1-\gamma(x)}{\gamma(x)}.

Lastly, we need to verify that this
v^{*}
is actually the Riesz representer for
\bm{\Gamma}[\cdot]
. To do so, we need to check that for any
u=(u_{1}(s,x),u_{2}(x),u_{3}(x))
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[W(v_{1}^{*}u_{1}+v_{3}^{*}u_{3})+Gv_{2}^{*}u_{2}]

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\frac{GWY}{\pi\rho_{1}(S,X)}\left({\frac{u_{1}(S,X)}{\zeta_{1}(S,X)(1-\zeta_{1}(S,X))}+\frac{u_{2}(X)}{\zeta_{2}(X)}+\frac{v_{3}(X)}{\zeta_{3}(X)(1-\zeta_{3}(X))}}\right)}\right]\penalty\ .

The remainder of the proof verifies that

\mathbb{E}[Wv_{1}^{*}u_{1}]=-\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\frac{GWY}{\pi\rho_{1}(S,X)}\frac{u_{1}(S,X)}{\zeta_{1}(S,X)(1-\zeta_{1}(S,X))}.}\right]

The other two terms can be verified analogously.

We first analyze the left-hand side. By law of total probability, we can break the left-hand side into

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{Wv_{1}^{*}u_{1}}\right]

\displaystyle=-\pi\mathbb{E}_{P}[Wv_{1}^{*}u_{1}\mid G=1]-(1-\pi)\mathbb{E}_{P}[Wv_{1}^{*}u_{1}\mid G=0]

\displaystyle\text{ where }-\pi\mathbb{E}_{P}[Wv_{1}^{*}u_{1}\mid G=1]

\displaystyle=\pi\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}[\rho_{1}(S(1),X)v_{1}^{*}(S(1),X)u_{1}(S(1),X)\mid G=1]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\left[{\frac{\mu(S(1),X)u_{1}(S(1),X)}{(1-\zeta_{1})}\mid G=1}\right]

and where

\displaystyle-(1-\pi)\mathbb{E}_{P}[Wv_{1}^{*}u_{1}\mid G=0]

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\pi}{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\left[{\varrho(X)\frac{\mu(S(1),X)u_{1}(S(1),X)}{(1-\zeta_{1}(S(1),X))\rho_{1}(S(1),X)}\mid G=0}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\left[{\frac{1-\pi}{\pi}\frac{\gamma(X)}{1-\gamma(X)}\frac{1}{\zeta_{1}(S(1),X)}\mu_{1}(S(1),X)u_{1}(S(1),X)\mid G=0}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\left[{\frac{1}{\zeta_{1}(S(1),X)}\mu_{1}(S(1),X)u_{1}(S(1),X)\mid G=1}\right]

where the last step follows from the observation that

p(s(1),x\mid G=1)=\frac{\gamma(x)}{1-\gamma(x)}\frac{1-\pi}{\pi}p(x\mid G=0)p(s(1)\mid x).

We can also compute from the right-hand side that

\mathbb{E}\left[{\frac{GWY}{\pi\rho_{1}}\frac{1}{\zeta_{1}(1-\zeta_{1})}u_{1}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{\frac{\mu(S(1),X)u_{1}(S(1),X)}{\zeta_{1}(1-\zeta_{1})}\mid G=1}\right]\penalty\ .

Therefore, the terms involving
u_{1}
in
\bm{\Gamma}[u]
and
\langle v^{*},u\rangle
do equal.

A.8.3.
Auxiliary Lemmas

Lemma A.6.

Under
A.1
(1–3),

\sqrt{n}(\hat{\pi}_{n}/\pi-1)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left({1-\frac{G_{i}}{\pi}}\right)+o_{p}(1)

and
\hat{\theta}_{1,1}-\theta=o_{p}(1).

Proof.

The normality of
\hat{\pi}_{n}/\pi
follows from the delta method. The consistency of

\hat{\theta}_{1n}
follows from the sup-norm consistency of
\hat{\zeta}_{n}
. Both claims
rely on
A.1
(2) to enforce continuity.

Lemma A.7.

Under
A.1
and
A.2
, the condition
A.4
(1) is satisfied.

Proof.

Define
\epsilon_{n}=\pm\delta_{n}
.
For some
h-\zeta\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
, defined in

A.2
