(2), let
\tilde{h}=h+\epsilon_{n}v_{n}^{*}
. We first consider the quantity

\sup_{h-\zeta\in\mathcal{F}_{n}}\mathbb{G}_{n}\left[{L(\cdot,\tilde{h})-L(\cdot,h)+\Delta_{i}[\epsilon_{n}v_{n}^{*}]}\right].

Note that we can compute

\displaystyle L(\cdot,\tilde{h})-L(\cdot,h)+\Delta_{i}[\epsilon_{n}v_{n}^{*}]

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{n}\left({W(h_{1}-\zeta_{1})v_{n1}^{*}+G(h_{2}-\zeta_{2})v_{n2}^{*}+W(h_{3}-\zeta_{3})v_{n3}^{*}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{n}^{2}({v_{n1}^{*}}^{2}+{v_{n2}^{*}}^{2}+{v_{n3}^{*}}^{2})

\displaystyle\equiv\epsilon_{n}R_{2}(\cdot,h,v_{n}^{*})+\epsilon_{n}^{2}R_{3}(\cdot,v_{n}^{*})

Note that
R_{3}
does not depend on
h
, and hence

\sup_{h-\zeta\in\mathcal{F}_{n}}\mathbb{G}_{n}R_{3}(\cdot,v_{n}^{*})\leq\lvert\mathbb{G}_{n}R_{3}(\cdot,v_{n}^{*})\rvert=\sqrt{n}O_{p}\left({\mathbb{E}\lVert v_{n}^{*}\rVert_{2}^{2}}\right)=O_{p}(\sqrt{n})

where we note that
\mathbb{E}\lVert v_{n}^{*}\rVert_{2}^{2}<\infty
since

\infty>\lVert v_{n}^{*}\rVert^{2}=\mathbb{E}\left[{P(W=1\mid S,X)({v_{n1}^{*}}^{2}+{v_{n3}^{*}}^{2})+P(G=1\mid X){v_{n2}^{*}}^{2}}\right]\geq\varepsilon\mathbb{E}[{v_{n1}^{*}}^{2}+{v_{n2}^{*}}^{2}+{v_{n3}^{*}}^{2}].

To bound
R_{2}
, observe that

\displaystyle\lVert R_{2}(\cdot,h_{1},v_{n}^{*})-R_{2}(\cdot,h_{2},v_{n}^{*})\rVert_{L_{2}(Q)}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lVert v_{n}^{*}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\lVert h_{1}-h_{2}\rVert_{L_{2}(Q)}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lVert h_{1}-h_{2}\rVert_{L_{2}(Q)}^{2}

where the implicit constant does not depend on
Q
.
Observe too that for
h-\zeta\in\mathcal{F}_{n}

|R_{2}(\cdot,h,v_{n}^{*})|\lesssim\delta_{n}^{1/2}

uniformly, and thus
C\delta_{n}^{1/2}
serves as an envelope function for
\mathcal{R}_{n}
, defined below.
Hence, letting

\mathcal{R}_{n}=\left\{{R_{2}(\cdot,h,v_{n}^{*}):h-\zeta\in\mathcal{F}_{n}}\right\},

we have that

\sqrt{1+\log N\left({C_{1}\epsilon\delta_{n}^{1/2},\mathcal{R}_{n},L_{2}(Q)}\right)}\leq C_{2}\sqrt{1+\log N\left({\epsilon\delta_{n}^{1/2},\mathcal{F}_{n},L_{2}(Q)}\right)}<C<\infty.

Hence, by Theorem 2.14.1 in
van der Vaart and Wellner, 1996
,

\mathbb{E}\sup_{h-\zeta\in\mathcal{F}_{n}}\lvert\mathbb{G}_{n}R_{2}(\cdot,h,v_{n}^{*})\rvert\lesssim\delta_{n}^{1/2}

This implies that

\eqref{eq:emp_proc_term_sieve}=O_{p}(\delta_{n}^{3/2}+\delta_{n}^{2}\sqrt{n}).

Now, consider some estimator
\hat{\zeta}
and the event that
\lVert\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta\rVert_{\infty}<\delta_{n}
. On this event, which occurs with probability tending to 1,
\lVert\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta\rVert\leq C\delta_{n}
. Let
\tilde{\zeta}_{n}=\hat{\zeta}_{n}+\epsilon_{n}v_{n}^{*}
. Let
A_{n}=\mathbbm{1}\left({\lVert\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta\rVert_{\infty}<\delta_{n}}\right)
.

Since
\hat{\zeta}_{n}

minimizes the empirical criterion

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq A_{n}\sqrt{n}\mathbb{P}_{n}[L(\cdot,\tilde{\zeta}_{n})-L(\cdot,\hat{\zeta}_{n})]

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}A_{n}\mathbb{P}\left[{L(\cdot,\tilde{\zeta}_{n})-L(\cdot,\hat{\zeta}_{n})}\right]+A_{n}\mathbb{G}_{n}\left[{L(\cdot,\tilde{\zeta}_{n})-L(\cdot,\hat{\zeta}_{n})}\right]

(Note that
\mathbb{P}[L(\cdot,h)-L(\cdot,\zeta)]=\frac{1}{2}\lVert h-\zeta\rVert^{2}
by definition of
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}\frac{A_{n}}{2}\left({\lVert\tilde{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta\rVert^{2}-\lVert\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta\rVert^{2}}\right)+A_{n}\mathbb{G}_{n}\left[{L(\cdot,\tilde{\zeta}_{n})-L(\cdot,\hat{\zeta}_{n})}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{n}A_{n}\langle\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta,\epsilon_{n}v_{n}^{*}\rangle+\sqrt{n}\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{n}^{2}+A_{n}\mathbb{G}_{n}\left[{L(\cdot,\tilde{\zeta}_{n})-L(\cdot,\hat{\zeta}_{n})}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{n}A_{n}\langle\epsilon_{n}v_{n}^{*},\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta\rangle+\mathbb{G}_{n}[-\Delta_{i}[\epsilon_{n}v_{n}^{*}]]+O_{p}(\delta_{n}^{3/2}+\delta_{n}^{2}\sqrt{n}),

where the last step follows from our bound on (
A.8.3
).

Finally, this implies that

\mathbb{G}_{n}[\Delta_{i}[\epsilon_{n}v_{n}^{*}]]\leq\sqrt{n}A_{n}\langle\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta,\epsilon_{n}v_{n}^{*}\rangle+O_{p}(\delta_{n}^{3/2}+\delta_{n}^{2}\sqrt{n}).

When
\epsilon_{n}=\delta_{n}
, (
A.8.3
) implies

\mathbb{G}_{n}\Delta_{i}[v_{n}^{*}]\leq\sqrt{n}A_{n}\langle\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta,v_{n}^{*}\rangle+O_{p}(\delta_{n}^{1/2}+\delta_{n}\sqrt{n})=\sqrt{n}A_{n}\langle\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta,v_{n}^{*}\rangle+o_{p}(1)

When
\epsilon_{n}=-\delta_{n}
, (
A.8.3
) implies

\sqrt{n}A_{n}\langle\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta,v_{n}^{*}\rangle\leq\mathbb{G}_{n}\Delta_{i}[v_{n}^{*}]+o_{p}(1).

Thus, taken together

|\sqrt{n}A_{n}\langle\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta,v_{n}^{*}\rangle-\mathbb{G}_{n}\Delta_{i}[v_{n}^{*}]|=o_{p}(1)

Note that (i)
\mathbb{P}\Delta_{i}[v]=0
by the first-order condition of the first-step problem and (ii)
\sqrt{n}A_{n}\langle\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta,v_{n}^{*}\rangle=\sqrt{n}\langle\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta,v_{n}^{*}\rangle+o_{p}(1)
.
As a result, we can rewrite the above display as

\lvert\langle\hat{\zeta}_{n}-\zeta,v_{n}^{*}\rangle-\mathbb{P}_{n}\Delta_{i}[v_{n}^{*}]\rvert=o_{p}(n^{-1/2})\penalty\ ,

completing the proof.

Appendix B
Long-Term Treatment Effect for the Experimental Population

In this section, we develop results analogous to those presented in the main text for long-term average treatment effect for the experimental population, given by

(B.1)

\tau_{0}=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[Y_{i}(1)-Y_{i}(0)\mid G_{i}=0\right]\penalty\ .

This estimand was considered in
Athey et al., 2020b
for the Statistical Surrogacy Model. The efficient influence functions and efficiency bounds we state in this section for that context correct those given in Theorem 1 and Theorem 3 of the February, 2020 draft of that paper.

B.1.
Identification

The identifying assumptions for
\tau_{0}
are slightly less restrictive than for
\tau_{1}
. In particular, in the case that treatment is not measured in the observational data set, it is unnecessary to impose the restriction
2.3
. Thus, the set of assumptions that compose the Statistical Surrogacy Model will not include
2.3
in this section.

Proposition B.1.

(1)

(
Athey et al., 2020a
)
Under the Latent Unconfounded Treatment Model,
\tau_{0}
is point identified.

(2)

(
Athey et al., 2020b
)
Under the Statistical Surrogacy Model,
\tau_{0}
is point identified.

Proof.

The result follows almost immediately from inspection of the proof of
2.1
by considering the parameter

\theta_{0,1}=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[Y(1)\mid G=0]\penalty\ .

In the Latent Unconfounded Treatment Model, the only difference will occur in the final step where it is apparent that

\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\mu_{1}(X)\mid G=0]

is identified. In the Statistical Surrogacy Model, the only difference is that
2.3
need not be invoked in order to condition on
G=0
, where again the expectation of
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mu(S,X)\mid X,W=1,G=0]
conditional on
G=0
remains identified.

B.2.
Semiparametric Efficiency

Next, we state a theorem analogous to
Theorem

3.1
for the case where the estimand of interest is the average long-term treatment effect
\tau_{0}
in the experimental population.

Theorem B.2.

(1)

Under the Latent Unconfounded Treatment Model, where treatment is observed in the observational data set, the efficient influence function for the parameter
\tau_{1}
is given by

(B.2)

\displaystyle\psi_{0}(b,\tau_{0},\eta)

\displaystyle=\frac{g}{1-\pi}\left(\frac{1-\gamma(x)}{\gamma(x)}\left(\frac{w(y-\mu_{1}(s,x))}{\rho_{1}(s,x)}-\frac{(1-w)(y-\mu_{0}(s,x))}{\rho_{0}(s,x)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1-g}{1-\pi}\left(\frac{w(\mu_{1}(s,x)-\overline{\mu}_{1}(x))}{\varrho(x)}-\frac{(1-w)(\mu_{0}(s,x)-\overline{\mu}_{0}(x))}{1-\varrho(x)}+(\overline{\mu}_{1}(x)-\overline{\mu}_{0}(x))-\tau_{0}\right),

where
\eta=(\omega,\kappa)
collects nuisance functions with

\omega=\left\{\mu_{w},\overline{\mu}_{w}\right\}_{w\in\{0,1\}}\quad\text{and}\quad\kappa=\{\{\rho_{w}\}_{w\in\{0,1\}},\varrho(\cdot),\gamma(\cdot),\pi\}\penalty\ .

collecting long-term outcome means and propensity scores, respectively.

(2)

Under the Statistical Surrogacy Model, where treatment is not observed in the observational data set, the efficient influence function
for the parameter
\tau_{1}
is given by

(B.3)

\displaystyle\xi_{0}(b,\tau_{0},\varphi)

\displaystyle=\frac{g}{1-\pi}\left(\frac{1-\gamma(s,x)}{\gamma(s,x)}\frac{(\varrho(s,x)-\varrho(x))(y-\nu(s,x))}{\varrho(x)(1-\varrho(x)}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1-g}{1-\pi}\left(\frac{w(\nu(s,x)-\overline{\nu}_{1}(x))}{\rho(x)}-\frac{(1-w)(\nu(s,x)-\overline{\nu}_{0}(x))}{1-\rho(x)}+(\overline{\nu}_{1}(x)-\overline{\nu}_{0}(x))-\tau_{0}\right)\penalty\ ,

where
\varphi=(\vartheta,\zeta)
collects nuisance functions with

\vartheta=\left\{\nu,\{\overline{\nu}_{w}\}_{w\in\{0,1\}}\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad\zeta=\{\varrho(\cdot,\cdot),\varrho(\cdot),\gamma(\cdot,\cdot),\gamma(\cdot),\pi\}\penalty\ .

collecting long-term outcome means and propensity scores, respectively.

Corollary B.1.

Define the functionals

\displaystyle\Gamma_{w,0}(s,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\mu_{w}(s,x)-\overline{\mu}_{w}(x)\right)^{2}}{\varrho(x)^{w}(1-\varrho(x))^{1-w}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Lambda_{w,0}(s,x)=\frac{\left(\nu(s,x)-\overline{\nu}_{w}(x)\right)^{2}}{\varrho(x)^{w}(1-\varrho(x))^{1-w}}\penalty\ .

(1)

Under the Latent Unconfounded Treatment Model, the semiparametric efficiency bound for
\tau_{0}
is given by

\displaystyle V_{0}^{\star}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Bigg[\frac{1-\gamma(X)}{(1-\pi)^{2}}\Bigg(\frac{1-\gamma(X)}{\gamma(X)}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}(S,X)}{\rho_{1}(S,X)}+\frac{\sigma_{0}^{2}(S,X)}{\rho_{0}(S,X)}\right)

(B.4)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+(\overline{\mu}_{1}(X)-\overline{\mu}_{0}(X)-\tau_{0})^{2}+\Gamma_{0,0}(S,X)+\Gamma_{1,0}(S,X)\Bigg)\Bigg]\penalty\ .

(2)

Under the Statistical Surrogacy Model, the semiparametric efficiency bound for
\tau_{0}
is given by

\displaystyle V_{0}^{\star\star}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Bigg[\frac{\gamma(X)}{(1-\pi)^{2}}\Bigg(\left(\frac{1-\gamma(S,X)}{\gamma(S,X)}\frac{\varrho(S,X)-\varrho(X)}{\varrho(X)(1-\varrho(X))}\right)^{2}\sigma^{2}(S,X)\Bigg)

(B.5)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{1-\gamma(X)}{(1-\pi)^{2}}\Bigg((\overline{\nu}_{1}(X)-\overline{\nu}_{0}(X)-\tau_{0})^{2}+\Lambda_{0,0}(S,X)+\Lambda_{1,0}(S,X)\Bigg)\Bigg]\penalty\ .

Proof.

We maintain the same notation and follow the same structure as the proof of

Theorem

3.1
, emphasizing differences without repeating shared steps in the argument.

We begin by proving Part 1. The analogue of (
A.3
) for
\theta_{1,0}
is

(B.6)

\displaystyle\theta_{1,0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}(Y^{*}\mid S^{*},X)\mid G=0]

(B.7)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}(S^{*}\mid X)\mid G=0]+\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}(X\mid G=0)\mid G=0]\penalty\ ,

with each term now conditioned on
G=0
and
\ell^{\prime}(X\mid G=0)
replacing
\ell^{\prime}(X\mid G=1)
. Note also that

\ell^{\prime}_{*}(x\mid G=0)=\ell^{\prime}_{*}(x,G=0)-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\ell^{\prime}_{*}(X,G=0)\mid G=0]\penalty\ .

For a candidate influence function
\tilde{\psi}_{1,0}(b_{1})
, the analogues of the pathwise differentiability conditions
(
A.5
) through (
A.9
) are

(B.8)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\pi}\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[(1-G)Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(Y^{*}\mid S^{*},X)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\tilde{\psi}_{1,0}(B_{1})GW\ell^{\prime}_{*}(Y\mid S,X)}\right]

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\pi}\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[(1-G)Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S^{*}\mid X)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Bigg[\tilde{\psi}_{1,0}(B_{1})\Bigg(W\ell^{\prime}_{*}(S\mid X)

(B.9)

\displaystyle\quad-G(1-W)\frac{\int\rho(s,X)p^{\prime}_{*}(s\mid X)\,d\lambda(s)}{1-\int\rho(s,X)p_{\star}(s\mid X)\,d\lambda(s)}\Bigg)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tilde{\psi}_{1,0}(B_{1})\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G,X)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\pi}\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[(1-G)Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G=0,X)]

(B.10)

\displaystyle\quad-\theta_{1,0}\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G=0,X)\mid G=0\right]

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Bigg[\tilde{\psi}_{1,0}(B_{1})G\Bigg(W\frac{\rho^{\prime}(S,X)}{\rho(S,X)}

(B.11)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-(1-W)\frac{\int\rho^{\prime}(s,X)p_{\star}(s\mid X)d\lambda(s)}{1-\int\rho(s,X)p_{\star}(s\mid X)\,d\lambda(s)}\Bigg)\Bigg]

(B.12)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\tilde{\psi}_{1,0}(B_{1})(1-G)\frac{W-\varrho(X)}{\varrho(X)(1-\varrho(X))}\varrho^{\prime}(X)}\right]

where the only differences relative to (
A.5
) through (
A.9
) are the replacement of
G
with
1-G
and
\pi
with

1-\pi
in
(
B.8
) through (
B.10
) on the sides of the
equalities that correspond to
\theta^{\prime}_{1,0}
. Since the density of the data doesn’t
change with the estimand, the sides of the equalities that correspond to orthogonality condition

\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\tilde{\psi}_{1,0}(B_{1})\ell^{\prime}(B_{1})\right]

remains
unchanged relative to (
A.5
) through (
A.9
).

We make the same choices
s_{4}(s,x)=-\rho(s,x)s_{2}(s,x)
and
s_{5}(x)=0
as in
(
A.10
), resulting in an conjectured efficient influence function of the form

\psi_{1,0}(b_{1})=gw\cdot s_{1}(y,s,x)+(1-g)w\cdot s_{2}(s,x)+s_{3}(g,x)

We make the conjecture that

\displaystyle s_{1}(y,s,x)

\displaystyle=f_{1}(x)\cdot\frac{y-\mu(s,x)}{\rho(s,x)}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle s_{2}(s,x)=\frac{\mu(s,x)-\overline{\mu}(x)}{(1-\pi)\varrho(x)}\penalty\ ,\quad\text{and}\quad

(B.13)

\displaystyle s_{3}(g,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-g}{1-\pi}(\overline{\mu}(x)-\theta_{1,0})\penalty\ ,

where

f_{1}(x)=\frac{1}{1-\pi}\frac{1-\gamma(x)}{\gamma(x)}\penalty\ .

These choices satisfy the conditional mean-zero conditions
for scores.

We verify the conditions (
B.8
) through (
B.12
) sequentially, completing the proof. To verify condition (
B.8
), we note that by the mean-zero property of the conditional scores, the right-hand side of (
B.8
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{f_{1}(X)\frac{GWY^{*}}{\rho(s,x)}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(Y^{*}\mid S^{*},X)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\pi f_{1}(X)Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(Y^{*}\mid S^{*},X)\mid G=1]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{\frac{1-\gamma}{\gamma}\frac{\pi}{1-\pi}Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}(Y^{*}\mid S^{*},X)\mid G=1}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}(Y^{*}\mid S^{*},X)\mid G=0}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\pi}\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[(1-G)Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}_{*}(Y^{*}\mid S^{*},X)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\tilde{\psi}_{1,0}(B_{1})GW\ell^{\prime}_{*}(Y\mid S,X)}\right]\penalty\ .

where the third equality follows from the importance sampling argument

p_{\star}(y,s,x\mid G=1)=p_{\star}(y,s\mid x)p_{\star}(x\mid G=1)=p_{\star}(y,s\mid x)p_{\star}(x\mid G=0)\frac{1-\gamma}{\gamma}\frac{\pi}{1-\pi}\penalty\ .

To verify condition (
B.9
), we note that by the mean-zero property of the conditional scores, the right-hand side of (
B.9
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{\frac{1-G}{1-\pi}W\frac{\mu(S,X)}{\varrho(x)}\ell^{\prime}(S^{*}\mid X)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\mu(S,X)\ell^{\prime}(S^{*}\mid X)\mid G=0]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}(S^{*}\mid X)\mid G=0]\penalty\ ,

which is equal to the left-hand side of (
B.9
).

To verify condition (
B.10
), we note again that by the mean-zero properties of the conditional
scores, the left-hand side of (
B.10
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{\frac{1-G}{1-\pi}(\overline{\mu}(X)-\theta_{1,0})\ell^{\prime}(G=0,X)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[{(\overline{\mu}(X)-\theta_{1,0})\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G=0,X)\mid G=0}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\overline{\mu}(X)\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G=0,X)\mid G=0]

\displaystyle-\theta_{1,0}\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}[\ell^{\prime}_{*}(G=0,X)\mid G=0]

which is equal to the right-hand side of (
B.10
).

Finally, condition (
B.11
) holds by mean-zero properties of
s_{1}(y,s,x)
and
condition (
B.12
) holds by mean-zero properties of
s_{2}(y,s,x)
and the
fact that
\mathbb{E}_{P}[W-\varrho(x)\mid X,G=0]=0
, completing the argument.

Next, we prove Part 2. The analogue of the pathwise derivative (
A.23
) for
\theta_{1,0}
is now

\displaystyle\theta_{1,0}^{\prime}=\,\,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\ell^{\prime}(Y\mid S,X,G=1)\mid S,X,G=1]\mid X,W=1,G=0]\mid G=0]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}[\nu(S,X)\ell^{\prime}(S\mid X,G=0,W=1)\mid X,W=1,G=0]\mid G=0]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[{(1-\pi)^{-1}\overline{\nu}(X)\left({-\gamma^{\prime}(X)+\ell^{\prime}(X)(1-\gamma(X))}\right)}\right]

(B.14)

\displaystyle+(1-\pi)^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{P}[\gamma^{\prime}(X)+\gamma(X)\ell^{\prime}(X)]\theta_{1,0}.

The conjectured efficient influence function is given by

\xi_{1,0}(b_{1})=g(y-\nu(s,x))\cdot f_{1}(s,x)+(1-g)w(\nu(s,x)-\overline{\nu}(x))\cdot f_{2}(x)+(1-g)\cdot f_{3}(x)

with
f_{1}(s,x)
,
f_{2}(x)
, and
f_{3}(x)
to be specified, where
f_{3}(x)
is chosen such that

\mathbb{E}[f_{3}(X)\mid G=0]=0\penalty\ .

An argument very similar to that given in
Section

A.4
demonstrates that

\xi_{1,0}(b_{1})
is in the tangent space.

Again, following a similar argument, we may verify that the choices

\displaystyle f_{1}(s,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varrho(s,x)}{(1-\pi)\varrho(x)}\frac{1-\gamma(s,x)}{\gamma(s,x)}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle f_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1-\pi)\varrho(x)}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle f_{3}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu(x)-\theta_{1,0}}{1-\pi}\penalty\ ,\text{and}

satisfy the pathwise differentiability conditions. The conditions for

f_{1}(s,x)
and
f_{2}(x)
are such that multiplication of the density ratio

\frac{p(x\mid G=0)}{p(x\mid G=1)}=\frac{\pi}{1-\pi}\frac{1-\gamma(x)}{\gamma(x)}

with the choices for
f_{1}(s,x)
and
f_{2}(x)
given in
Section

A.4
yields the corresponding choices here.

B.3.
Estimation

In this section, we define a semiparametric estimator of the long-term treatment effect
\tau_{0}
in the experimental population. This estimator is analogous to the estimator formulated in
Section

4.1.1
. Again, we use the “Double/Debiased Machine Learning” (DML) construction developed in
Chernozhukov et al., 2018
.

Definition B.1

(DML Estimators)
.

Let
\hat{\eta}(I)
and
\hat{\varphi}(I)
denote generic estimates of
\eta
and
\varphi
based on the data
\{B_{i}\}_{i\in I}
for some subset
I\in[n]
. Let
\{I_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{k}
denote a random
k
-fold partition of
[n]
such that the size of each fold is
m=n/k
. The estimator
\hat{\tau}_{0}
is defined as the solution to

\frac{1}{k}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i\in I_{\ell}}\psi_{0}(B_{i},\hat{\tau}_{0},\hat{\eta}(I_{\ell}^{c}))=0\quad\text{or}\quad\frac{1}{k}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i\in I_{\ell}}\xi_{0}(B_{i},\hat{\tau}_{0},\hat{\varphi}(I_{\ell}^{c}))=0

for the Latent Unconfounded Treatment and Statistical Surrogacy Models, respectively.

Theorem

B.3
demonstrates that
\hat{\tau}_{0}
is semiparametrically efficient for
\tau_{0}
under the Latent Unconfounded Treatment Model.

Theorem B.3
(DML Estimation and Inference)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}
be the set of all probability distributions

P
for
\{B_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
that satisfy the Latent Unconfounded Treatment Model stated in
2.1

in addition to
4.1
. If
4.2
holds for every
\mathcal{P}
, then

(B.15)

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\hat{\tau}_{0}-\tau_{0})\overset{d}{\to}\mathcal{N}(0,V_{0}^{\star})

uniformly over
P\in\mathcal{P}
, where
\hat{\tau}_{0}
is defined in
B.1
,
V_{0}^{*}
is defined in
B.1
, and
\overset{d}{\to}
denotes convergence in distribution. Moreover, we have that

(B.16)

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{0}^{*}=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i\in I_{\ell}}\left(\psi_{0}(B_{i},\hat{\tau}_{0},\hat{\eta}(I_{\ell}^{c}))\right)^{2}\overset{p}{\to}V_{0}^{\star}

uniformly over
P\in\mathcal{P}
, where
\overset{p}{\to}
denotes convergence in probability, and as a result we obtain the uniform asymptotic validity of the confidence intervals

(B.17)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\Big|P\left(\tau_{0}\in\left[\hat{\tau}_{1}\pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\sqrt{\hat{V}_{0}^{\star}/n}\right]\right)-(1-\alpha)\Big|=0\penalty\ ,

where
z_{1-\alpha/2}
is the
1-\alpha/2
quantile of the standard normal distribution.

Theorem B.4
(DML Estimation and Inference)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}
be the set of all probability distributions

P
for
\{B_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
that satisfy the Statistical Surrogacy Model stated in
2.2

in addition to
4.1
. If
4.2
holds for every
\mathcal{P}
, then

(B.18)

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\hat{\tau}_{0}-\tau_{0})\overset{d}{\to}\mathcal{N}(0,V_{0}^{\star\star})

uniformly over
P\in\mathcal{P}
, where
\hat{\tau}_{0}
is defined in
B.1
,
V_{0}^{\star\star}
is defined in
B.1
, and
\overset{d}{\to}
denotes convergence in distribution. Moreover, we have that

(B.19)

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{0}^{\star\star}=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i\in I_{\ell}}\left(\xi_{0}(B_{i},\hat{\tau}_{0},\hat{\varphi}(I_{\ell}^{c}))\right)^{2}\overset{p}{\to}V_{0}^{\star\star}

uniformly over
P\in\mathcal{P}
, where
\overset{p}{\to}
denotes convergence in probability, and as a result we obtain the uniform asymptotic validity of the confidence intervals

(B.20)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\Big|P\left(\tau_{0}\in\left[\hat{\tau}_{1}\pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\sqrt{\hat{V}_{0}^{\star\star}/n}\right]\right)-(1-\alpha)\Big|=0\penalty\ ,

where
z_{1-\alpha/2}
is the
1-\alpha/2
quantile of the standard normal distribution.

Proof of
Theorem

B.3
and
Theorem

B.4
.

The proof of
Theorem

B.3
is very similar to the proof of
Theorem

4.1
, being obtained by verifying the conditions of Theorem 3.1 of
Chernozhukov et al., 2018
. The arguments in support of the verification of Assumption 3.1 are identical, with
1-g
replacing
g
in the statement supporting condition (e). Similarly, the arguments supporting the verification of Assumption 3.2 are very similar, where the constants premultiplying the various bounds derived in support of conditions (b), (c), and (d) will be slightly different, but will be obtained with the same arguments, which we omit to avoid repetition. The second Gateaux derivatives of the efficient influence functions have the same structure, and so the third inequality in condition (c) will again follow from Hölder’s inequality and
4.2
.

Appendix C
Additional Results

C.1.
Consistency Double Robustness

Recall that, in
Remark

3.2
, we divide the nuisance parameters
\eta
or
\varphi
into
(\omega,\kappa,\pi)
, where
\omega
consists of a set of outcome regression nuisance parameters, and
\kappa
consists of a set of propensity score-type nuisance parameters. We note that the mean-zero properties of the efficient influence function is preserved as long as one of
\omega,\kappa
is set at its respective true value. In this section, we show a consistency analogue of this double robustness result.

To economize on notation, we note that the solutions
\hat{\tau}_{1n}
to both
\psi_{1}=0
and
\xi_{1}=0
take the form

\hat{\tau}_{1n}=\tau_{1n}(\hat{\omega}_{n},\hat{\kappa}_{n})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(B_{i};\hat{\omega}_{n}(B_{i}),\hat{\kappa}_{n}(B_{i}),\hat{\pi}_{n})

when
\hat{\pi}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}G_{i}
is used. Here,
\hat{\omega}_{n}(B_{i}),\hat{\kappa}_{n}(B_{i})
evaluates the nuisance parameters at
B_{i}
.
Define
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty}
entrywise as
\lVert\theta\rVert_{\infty}=\max_{j}\lVert\theta_{j}\rVert_{\infty}
for a vector of nuisance parameters
\theta
.

Theorem C.1.

Let
\hat{\tau}_{1n}
be as in (
C.1
), derived either from either
g
equal to
\psi_{1}
or
\xi_{1}
under either the Latent Unconfoundedness or the Statistical Surrogacy Models. Assume that
\hat{\pi}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}G_{i}
. Assume additionally that (i) entries in
\kappa
are bounded uniformly between
[1-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]
for some
\varepsilon>0
, (ii) entries in
\omega
are bounded uniformly by some finite
C>0
, (iii) entries in
\hat{\kappa}_{n}
are bounded uniformly between
[1-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]
almost surely, (iv) entries in
\hat{\omega}_{n}
are bounded uniformly by
C
almost surely, (v) all nuisance parameters, except for
\hat{\pi}_{n}
, are estimated on a hold-out sample, and (vi)
\mathbb{E}_{P}|Y|^{2}<\infty
. Then:

(1)

If
\lVert\hat{\omega}_{n}-\omega\rVert_{\infty}\overset{p}{\to}0
, then
\hat{\tau}_{1n}\overset{p}{\to}\tau_{1}
.

(2)

If
\lVert\hat{\kappa}_{n}-\kappa\rVert_{\infty}\overset{p}{\to}0
, then
\hat{\tau}_{1n}\overset{p}{\to}\tau_{1}
.

Proof.

Let
A_{n}=\mathbbm{1}(\hat{\pi}_{n}\in[\varepsilon/2,1-\varepsilon/2])
be an event that occurs with probability tending to one. For either (1) or (2), let
\eta_{C}
collect the consistently estimated nuisance parameters, and let
\eta_{I}
collect the rest. Let
\hat{\eta}_{Cn}
,
\hat{\eta}_{In}
be their estimated analogues. Note that
\pi
is always in
\eta_{C}
.

Note that under either model, by Taylor’s theorem, on the event
A_{n}
,

g(B_{i};\hat{\omega}_{n}(B_{i}),\hat{\kappa}_{n}(B_{i}))=g(B_{i};\eta_{C}(B_{i}),\hat{\eta}_{In}(B_{i}))+\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\eta_{Ci}}}g(B_{i};\eta_{Ci},\hat{\eta}_{In}(B_{i}))\bigg\rvert_{\eta_{Ci}=\tilde{\eta}_{Ci}}(\hat{\eta}_{Cn}(B_{i})-\eta_{C}(B_{i}))

where
\tilde{\eta}_{Ci}
lies somewhere on the line segment connecting
\hat{\eta}_{Cn}(B_{i})
and
\eta_{C}(B_{i})
. Hence, by inspecting the derivative of
g
, we find that, for some constant
M
that depends on
(C,\varepsilon)
,

\displaystyle\bigg\lvert\hat{\tau}_{1n}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(B_{i};\eta_{C}(B_{i}),\hat{\eta}_{In}(B_{i}))\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle=A_{n}\bigg\lvert\hat{\tau}_{1n}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(B_{i};\eta_{C}(B_{i}),\hat{\eta}_{In}(B_{i}))\bigg\rvert+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\hat{\eta}_{Cn}-\eta_{C}\rVert_{\infty}\frac{A_{n}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigg\lvert\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\eta_{Ci}}}g(B_{i};\eta_{Ci},\hat{\eta}_{In}(B_{i}))\bigg\rvert_{\eta_{Ci}=\tilde{\eta}_{Ci}}\bigg\rvert+o_{p}(1)

((i)–(iv))

\displaystyle\leq M(C,\varepsilon)\lVert\hat{\eta}_{Cn}-\eta_{C}\rVert_{\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|Y_{i}|+o_{p}(1)

(vi)

\displaystyle=o_{p}(1).

It suffices to then show that

\bigg\lvert\tau_{1}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(B_{i};\eta_{C}(B_{i}),\hat{\eta}_{In}(B_{i}))\bigg\rvert=o_{p}(1).

Note that, conditional on
\hat{\eta}_{In}
,
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(B_{i};\eta_{C}(B_{i}),\hat{\eta}_{In}(B_{i}))
is an average of i.i.d. random variables with mean
\tau_{1}
, by the double robust property and (v). By the law of large numbers applied to triangular arrays, which uses (i)–(iv) and (vi), for almost every sequence
\hat{\eta}_{In}
, for every
\epsilon>0
,

P\left({\bigg\lvert\tau_{1}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(B_{i};\eta_{C}(B_{i}),\hat{\eta}_{In}(B_{i}))\bigg\rvert>\epsilon\mid\hat{\eta}_{In}}\right)\to 0.

Thus, by the dominated convergence theorem applied to left-hand side of the previous display, treating it as a random variable indexed by
n
,

\displaystyle P\left({\bigg\lvert\tau_{1}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(B_{i};\eta_{C}(B_{i}),\hat{\eta}_{In}(B_{i}))\bigg\rvert>\epsilon}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{P\left({\bigg\lvert\tau_{1}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(B_{i};\eta_{C}(B_{i}),\hat{\eta}_{In}(B_{i}))\bigg\rvert>\epsilon\mid\hat{\eta}_{In}}\right)}\right]\to 0\penalty\ ,

as required.

C.2.
Known Nuisance Functions

In this section, we assess how the efficient influence functions derived in
Theorem

3.1
change if different components of the nuisance functions
\eta
and
\varphi
are known. We consider only estimation of the long-term treatment effect in the observational sample
\tau_{1}
. The case of the treatment effect in the experimental sample
\tau_{1}
is analogous.

First, we consider the Latent Unconfounded Treatment Model. We show that the efficient influence function, and thereby, the semiparametric efficiency bound, is unchanged if the propensity score in the experimental sample
\varrho(x)
is known. This result echoes an analogous result for estimation of the average treatment effects under ignorability given in
Hahn, 1998
. On the other hand, if the probability of being included in observational sample
\gamma(x)
is known, then there is a change in the efficient influence function. In particular, the term
(g/\pi)((\overline{\mu}_{1}(x)-\overline{\mu}_{0}(x))-\tau_{1})
in
\psi_{1}(b,\tau_{1},\eta)
is replaced by
(\gamma(x)/\pi)((\overline{\mu}_{1}(x)-\overline{\mu}_{0}(x))-\tau_{1})
. Proofs for these results are given in
Sections

C.2.1
and
C.2.2
, respectively.

Theorem C.2.

Consider the Latent Unconfounded Treatment Model, given in
2.1
.

(i)
If the propensity score in the experimental sample
\varrho(x)
is known, then the efficient influence function for the parameter
\tau_{1}
is unchanged.

(ii)
If the probability of being included in observational sample
\gamma(x)
is known, then the efficient influence function for the parameter
\tau_{1}
is given by

\displaystyle\check{\psi}_{1}(b,\tau_{1},\eta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\Bigg(g\left(\frac{w(y-\mu_{1}(s,x))}{\rho_{1}(s,x)}-\frac{(1-w)(y-\mu_{0}(s,x))}{\rho_{0}(s,x)}\right)+\gamma(x)\left((\overline{\mu}_{1}(x)-\overline{\mu}_{0}(x))-\tau_{1}\right)

(C.2)

\displaystyle+\frac{(1-g)\gamma(x)}{1-\gamma(x)}\left(\frac{w(\mu_{1}(s,x)-\overline{\mu}_{1}(x))}{\varrho(x)}-\frac{(1-w)(\mu_{0}(s,x)-\overline{\mu}_{0}(x))}{1-\varrho(x)}\right)\Bigg),

where, again, the parameter
\eta
collects the nuisance functions appearing in (
C.2
).

Next, we provide analogous results for the Statistical Surrogacy Model. Here, however, the efficient influence function is additionally invariant to knowledge of the distribution in the observational sample of the short-term outcomes conditional on the covariates, i.e. the law
S\mid X,G=1
. Proofs are given in
Sections

C.2.3
,
C.2.4
, and
C.2.5
.

Theorem C.3.

Consider the Statistical Surrogacy Model, given in
2.2
.

(i)
If the propensity score in the experimental sample
\varrho(x)
is known, then the efficient influence function for the parameter
\tau_{1}
is unchanged.

(ii)
If the distribution of the short-term outcomes, conditional on the pretreatment covariates, is known in observational dataset, then the efficient influence function for the parameter
\tau_{1}
is unchanged.

(iii)
If the probability of being included in observational sample
\gamma(x)
is known, then the efficient influence function for the parameter
\tau_{1}
is given by

\displaystyle\check{\xi}_{1}(b,\tau_{1},\eta)

\displaystyle=\frac{g}{\pi}\left(\frac{\gamma(x)}{\gamma(s,x)}\frac{1-\gamma(s,x)}{1-\gamma(x)}\frac{(\varrho(s,x)-\varrho(x))(y-\nu(s,x))}{\varrho(x)(1-\varrho(x)}\right)+\frac{\gamma(x)}{\pi}\left((\overline{\nu}_{1}(x)-\overline{\nu}_{0}(x))-\tau_{1}\right)

(C.3)

\displaystyle+\frac{1-g}{\pi}\left(\frac{\gamma(x)}{1-\gamma(x)}\left(\frac{w(\nu(s,x)-\overline{\nu}_{1}(x))}{\varrho(x)}-\frac{(1-w)(\nu(s,x)-\overline{\nu}_{0}(x))}{1-\varrho(x)}\right)\right)\penalty\ ,

where, again, the parameter
\varphi
collects the nuisance functions appearing in (
C.3
).

C.2.1.
Proof of
Theorem

C.2
, Part (i)

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a regular parametric submodel of
\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}
,
indexed by
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}
and such that
2.1
,
2.3
, and
2.4
hold for each
P_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{P}
. Additionally,
suppose that

p_{\varepsilon}\left(W=1\mid x,G=0\right)=\varrho\left(x\right)

for all
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}
and some fixed function
\varrho\left(x\right)
.

In this case, we have the score

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}\left(b_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=wg\cdot\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(y\mid x,s\right)+w\cdot\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(s\mid x\right)+\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(g,x\right)

\displaystyle-g\left(1-w\right)\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\rho\left(S^{*},X\right)\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(S^{*}\mid X\right)\mid G=0,W=1,X=x\right]}{1-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\rho\left(S^{*},X\right)\mid G=0,W=1,X=x\right]}\right)

\displaystyle+g\left(w\cdot\frac{\rho^{\prime}\left(s,x\right)}{\rho\left(s,x\right)}-\left(1-w\right)\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\rho^{\prime}\left(S^{*},X\right)\mid G=0,W=1,X=x\right]}{1-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\rho\left(S^{*},X\right)\mid G=0,W=1,X=x\right]}\right)

and so the tangent space
\mathcal{T}
is given by the mean-square
closure of the linear space of the functions

\displaystyle s\left(b_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=wg\cdot s_{1}\left(y\mid x,s\right)+w\cdot s_{2}\left(s\mid x\right)+s_{3}\left(g,x\right)

\displaystyle-g\left(1-w\right)\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\rho\left(S^{*},X\right)s_{2}\left(S^{*}\mid X\right)\mid G=0,W=1,X=x\right]}{1-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\rho\left(S^{*},X\right)\mid G=0,W=1,X=x\right]}\right)

\displaystyle+g\left(w\cdot\frac{s_{4}\left(s,x\right)}{\rho\left(s,x\right)}-\left(1-w\right)\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[s_{4}\left(S^{*},X\right)\mid G=0,W=1,X=x\right]}{1-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\rho\left(S^{*},X\right)\mid G=0,W=1,X=x\right]}\right)

where the functions
s_{1}
through
s_{4}
range over the space
of mean-zero and square integrable functions that additionally satisfy
the restrictions

(C.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[s_{1}\left(Y\mid X,S\right)\mid W=1,G=1,S,X\right]

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{and}

(C.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[s_{2}\left(S\mid X\right)\mid W=1,G=0,X\right]

\displaystyle=0.

Recall the integral representation

(C.6)

\theta_{1,1}=\int\int\int\frac{y}{\pi}p_{\star}\left(y\mid s,x\right)p_{\star}\left(s\mid x\right)p\left(x,G=1\right)\text{d}\lambda\left(y\right)\text{d}\lambda\left(s\right)\text{d}\lambda\left(x\right).

Observe that
p\left(w=1\mid x,G=0\right)
does not appear in (
C.6
).
Thus, the pathwise derivative of
\theta_{1,1}
at
0
on the submodel
of
\mathcal{P}
is again given by (
A.3
). Thus, it will suffice to
choose functions
s_{1}\left(\cdot\right)
through
s_{4}\left(\cdot\right)

satisfying the above conditions and whose resultant score function
satisfies (
A.5
) through (
A.8
).

Consider the choices

\displaystyle s_{1}\left(y,s,x\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{y-\mu\left(s,x\right)}{\pi\rho\left(s,x\right)},

\displaystyle s_{2}\left(y,s\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{1}{\varrho\left(x\right)}\frac{\gamma\left(x\right)}{1-\gamma\left(x\right)}\left(\mu\left(s,x\right)-\overline{\mu}\left(x\right)\right),

\displaystyle s_{3}\left(g,x\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{g}{\pi}\left(\overline{\mu}\left(x\right)-\theta_{1,1}\right)\text{, and}

\displaystyle s_{4}\left(y,s\right)

\displaystyle=-\rho\left(s,x\right)s_{2}\left(s,x\right),

which again yield the conjectured influence function

\psi_{1,1}\left(b_{1}\right)=\frac{gw\left(y-\overline{\mu}\left(s,x\right)\right)}{\pi\rho\left(s,x\right)}+\frac{\left(1-g\right)w}{\left(1-\gamma\left(x\right)\right)\varrho\left(x\right)}\frac{\gamma\left(x\right)}{\pi}\left(\mu\left(s,x\right)-\overline{\mu}\left(x\right)\right)+\frac{g}{\pi}\left(\overline{\mu}\left(x\right)-\theta_{1,1}\right).

Each of these choices are mean-zero and square integrable, and satisfy
the conditions (
C.4
) and (
C.5
),
as before. The arguments verifying the conditions (
A.5
) through (
A.8
)
given in proof of
Theorem

3.1
, Part 1, completeing the proof.

C.2.2.
Proof of
Theorem

C.2
, Part (ii)

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a regular parametric submodel of
\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}
,
indexed by
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}
and such that
2.1
,
2.3
, and
2.4
hold for each
P_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{P}
. Additionally,
suppose that

p_{\varepsilon}\left(G=1\mid X=x\right)=\gamma\left(x\right)

for all
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}
and some fixed function
\gamma\left(x\right)
.

In this case, we have the score

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}\left(b_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=wg\cdot\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(y\mid x,s\right)+w\cdot\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(s\mid x\right)+\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle-g\left(1-w\right)\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\rho\left(S^{*},X\right)\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(S^{*}\mid X\right)\mid G=0,W=1,X=x\right]}{1-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\rho\left(S^{*},X\right)\mid G=0,W=1,X=x\right]}\right)

\displaystyle+g\left(w\cdot\frac{\rho^{\prime}\left(s,x\right)}{\rho\left(s,x\right)}-\left(1-w\right)\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\rho^{\prime}\left(S^{*},X\right)\mid G=0,W=1,X=x\right]}{1-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\rho\left(S^{*},X\right)\mid G=0,W=1,X=x\right]}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(1-g\right)\varrho^{\prime}\left(x\right)\left(\frac{w-\varrho\left(x\right)}{\varrho\left(x\right)\left(1-\varrho\left(x\right)\right)}\right)

and so the tangent space
\mathcal{T}
is given by the mean-square
closure of the linear space of the functions

\displaystyle s\left(b_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=wg\cdot s_{1}\left(y\mid x,s\right)+w\cdot s_{2}\left(s\mid x\right)+s_{3}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle-g\left(1-w\right)\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\rho\left(S^{*},X\right)s_{2}\left(S^{*}\mid X\right)\mid G=0,W=1,X=x\right]}{1-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\rho\left(S^{*},X\right)\mid G=0,W=1,X=x\right]}\right)

\displaystyle+g\left(w\cdot\frac{s_{4}\left(s,x\right)}{\rho\left(s,x\right)}-\left(1-w\right)\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[s_{4}\left(S^{*},X\right)\mid G=0,W=1,X=x\right]}{1-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\rho\left(S^{*},X\right)\mid G=0,W=1,X=x\right]}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(1-g\right)\left(s_{5}\left(x\right)\cdot\frac{w-\varrho\left(x\right)}{\varrho\left(x\right)\left(1-\varrho\left(x\right)\right)}\right)

where the functions
s_{1}
through
s_{5}
range over the space
of mean-zero and square integrable functions that additionally satisfy
the restrictions (
C.4
) and (
C.5
).
By the integral representation (
C.6
) and the fact
that

p_{\varepsilon}\left(x,G=1\right)=\gamma\left(x\right)p_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)

for all
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}
, the pathwise derivative of
\theta_{1,1}

at
0
on
\mathcal{P}
is given by

\displaystyle\theta_{1,1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}\left(Y^{*}\mid S^{*},X\right)\mid G=1\right]

(C.7)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}\left(S^{*}\mid X\right)\mid G=1\right]+\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}\left(X\right)\mid G=1\right].

Each of the terms in (
C.7
) can be written
in terms of conditional scores by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}\left(Y^{*}\mid S^{*},X\right)\mid G=1\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\pi^{-1}GY^{*}\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(Y^{*}\mid S^{*},X\right)\right],

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[Y^{*}\ell^{\prime}\left(S^{*}\mid X\right)\mid G=1\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\pi^{-1}GY^{*}\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(S^{*}\mid X\right)\right],\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[Y^{*}\gamma\left(X\right)\ell^{\prime}\left(X\right)\mid G=1\right].

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\pi^{-1}GY^{*}\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(X\right)\right],

respectively. Thus, in order to establish that a mean-zero and square-integrable
function
\tilde{\psi}_{1,1}\left(B_{1}\right)
is an influence function
for
\theta_{1,1}
it suffices to verify the conditions (
A.5
) through (
A.9
), with (
A.7
) replaced by

(C.8)

\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\pi^{-1}\gamma^{2}\left(X\right)Y^{*}\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(X\right)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\tilde{\psi}_{1,1}\left(B_{1}\right)\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(X\right)\right].

Consider the choices

\displaystyle s_{1}\left(y,s,x\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{y-\mu\left(s,x\right)}{\pi\rho\left(s,x\right)},

\displaystyle s_{2}\left(y,s\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{1}{\varrho\left(x\right)}\frac{\gamma\left(x\right)}{1-\gamma\left(x\right)}\left(\mu\left(s,x\right)-\overline{\mu}\left(x\right)\right),

\displaystyle s_{3}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma\left(x\right)}{\pi}\overline{\mu}\left(x\right)-\theta_{1,1}

\displaystyle s_{4}\left(y,s\right)

\displaystyle=-\rho\left(s,x\right)s_{2}\left(s,x\right),\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle s_{5}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=0,

which yield the conjectured influence function

\displaystyle\check{\psi}_{1,1}\left(b_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{gw\left(y-\mu\left(s,x\right)\right)}{\pi\rho\left(s,x\right)}+\frac{\left(1-g\right)w}{\pi\varrho\left(x\right)}\frac{\gamma\left(x\right)}{1-\gamma\left(x\right)}\left(\mu\left(s,x\right)-\overline{\mu}\left(x\right)\right)+\frac{\gamma\left(x\right)}{\pi}\left(\overline{\mu}\left(x\right)-\theta_{1,1}\right).

These choices are again mean-zero and square integrable, and satisfy
the conditions (
C.4
) and (
C.5
).
Thus, it suffices to check that the conditions (
A.5
), (
A.6
), (
A.8
), (
A.9
), and (
C.8
) continue to hold.

The conditions (
A.5
), (
A.6
), and (
A.8
) continue to hold through iterated
expectation conditional on
X
by the fact the only term changed
relative to
\psi_{1,1}\left(b_{1}\right)
is
s_{3}\left(x\right)
.
The condition (
A.9
) continues to hold as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\frac{1-G}{\pi}\left(\gamma\left(X\right)\overline{\mu}\left(X\right)-\theta_{1,1}\right)\frac{W-\varrho\left(X\right)}{\varrho\left(X\right)\left(1-\varrho\left(X\right)\right)}\varrho^{\prime}\left(X\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\frac{\left(1-G\right)}{\pi}\left(\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[G\mid X\right]\gamma\left(X\right)\overline{\mu}\left(X\right)-\theta_{1,1}\right)\frac{W-\varrho\left(X\right)}{\varrho\left(X\right)\left(1-\varrho\left(X\right)\right)}\varrho^{\prime}\left(X\right)\right]

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\frac{\theta_{1,1}\left(1-G\right)}{\pi}\frac{\theta_{1,1}\left(W-\varrho\left(X\right)\right)}{\varrho\left(X\right)\left(1-\varrho\left(X\right)\right)}\varrho^{\prime}\left(X\right)\right]=0.

Thus, it remains to verify the new condition (
C.8
).
This condition reduces to

\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\pi^{-1}GY^{*}\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(X\right)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\frac{1}{\pi}\gamma\left(X\right)\overline{\mu}\left(X\right)\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(X\right)\right],

as before. Observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\pi^{-1}\gamma\left(X\right)\overline{\mu}\left(X\right)\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(X\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\pi^{-1}\gamma\left(X\right)\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\mu\left(S,X\right)\mid G=0,W=1,X\right]\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(X\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\pi^{-1}\gamma\left(X\right)\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\left[\mu\left(S^{*},X\right)\mid X\right]\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(X\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\pi^{-1}\gamma\left(X\right)\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\left[Y^{*}\mid W=1,G=1,S,X\right]\mid X\right]\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(X\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\pi^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[G\mid X,Y^{*}\right]Y^{*}\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(X\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\star}}\left[\pi^{-1}GY^{*}\ell_{*}^{\prime}\left(X\right)\right]

where the second to last equality follows from Assumption 2.3, completing
the proof.

C.2.3.
Proof of
Theorem

C.3
, Part (i)

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a regular parametric submodel of
\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}
,
indexed by
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}
and such that
2.1
,

,

, and

.

for all
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}
and some fixed function
\varrho\left(x\right)
.

In this case, we have the score

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}\left(b_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=g\cdot\ell^{\prime}\left(y\mid x,s,G=1\right)+g\cdot\ell^{\prime}\left(s\mid x,G=1\right)+w\left(1-g\right)\cdot\ell^{\prime}\left(s\mid W=1,x,G=0\right)

\displaystyle+\ell^{\prime}\left(x\right)+\frac{g-\gamma\left(x\right)}{\gamma\left(x\right)\left(1-\gamma\left(x\right)\right)}\gamma^{\prime}\left(x\right)

and so the tangent space
\mathcal{T}
is given by the mean-square
closure of the linear space of the functions

\displaystyle s\left(b_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=g\cdot s_{1}\left(y\mid x,s,G=1\right)+g\cdot s_{2}\left(s\mid x,G=1\right)+w\left(1-g\right)s_{3}\left(s\mid W=1,x,G=0\right)

\displaystyle+s_{4}\left(x\right)+\frac{g-\gamma\left(x\right)}{\gamma\left(x\right)\left(1-\gamma\left(x\right)\right)}s_{5}\left(x\right)

where the functions
s_{1}
through
s_{5}
range over the space
of mean-zero and square integrable functions that additionally satisfy

(C.9)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[s_{1}\left(Y\mid X,S,G=1\right)\mid S,X,G=1\right]

\displaystyle=0,

(C.10)

\displaystyle=0,\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[s_{3}\left(S\mid X,W=1G=0\right)\mid X,W=1G=0\right]

\displaystyle=0.

Consider the choices

(C.12)

\displaystyle s_{1}\left(y\mid s,x,G=1\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{\varrho\left(s,x\right)}{\varrho\left(x\right)}\frac{1-\gamma\left(s,x\right)}{\gamma\left(s,x\right)}\frac{\gamma\left(x\right)}{1-\gamma\left(x\right)}\left(y-\nu\left(s,x\right)\right),

(C.13)

\displaystyle s_{2}\left(s\mid X,G=1\right)

\displaystyle=0,

(C.14)

\displaystyle s_{3}\left(s\mid x,W=1,G=1\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{1}{\varrho\left(x\right)}\frac{\gamma\left(x\right)}{1-\gamma\left(x\right)}\left(\nu\left(s,x\right)-\overline{\nu}\left(x\right)\right),

(C.15)

\displaystyle s_{4}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=\gamma\left(x\right)\left(\frac{\overline{\nu}\left(x\right)-\theta_{1,1}}{\pi}\right),\quad\text{and}

(C.16)

\displaystyle s_{5}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=\gamma\left(x\right)\left(1-\gamma\left(x\right)\right)\left(\frac{\overline{\nu}\left(x\right)-\theta_{1,1}}{\pi}\right).

These choices are again mean-zero and square integrable, and satisfy
the conditions (
C.9
), (
C.10
),
and (
C.11
).

Recall the integral representation

(C.17)

\theta_{1,1}=\int\int\int yp\left(y\mid s,x,G=1\right)p\left(s\mid x,W=1,G=0\right)p\left(x\mid G=1\right)\text{d$\lambda\left(x\right)\text{d}\lambda\left(s\right)\text{d}\lambda\left(x\right).$}

does not appear in (
C.17
).
Thus, the pathwise derivative of
\theta_{1,1}
at
0
on the submodel
of
\mathcal{P}
is again given by (
A.23
). Thus, it will suffice
to verify the conditions

(C.18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[Gs_{1}\left(Y\mid S,X,G=1\right)\ell^{\prime}\left(Y\mid X,S,G=1\right)\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[Y\ell^{\prime}\left(Y\mid S,X,G=1\right)\mid S,X,G=1\right]\mid X,W=1,G=0\right]\mid G=1\right]

(C.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[W\left(1-G\right)s_{3}\left(S\mid X,W=1,G=1\right)\ell^{\prime}\left(y\mid x,s,G=1\right)\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\nu\left(S,X\right)\ell^{\prime}\left(S\mid X,W=1,G=0\right)\mid X,W=1,G=0\right]\mid G=1\right]

(C.20)

\displaystyle\quad\quad=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\overline{\nu}\left(X\right)\left(\gamma^{\prime}\left(X\right)+\ell^{\prime}\left(X\right)\gamma\left(X\right)\right)\right]-\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\gamma^{\prime}\left(X\right)+\ell^{\prime}\left(X\right)\gamma\left(X\right)\right]\theta_{1,1}

These are identical to the conditions (

), (

), and (
