which yield the conjectured influence function

\displaystyle\check{\xi}_{1,1}\left(b_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{g}{\pi}\frac{\varrho\left(s,x\right)}{\varrho\left(x\right)}\frac{1-\gamma\left(s,x\right)}{\gamma\left(s,x\right)}\frac{\gamma\left(x\right)}{1-\gamma\left(x\right)}\left(y-\nu\left(s,x\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{\gamma\left(x\right)}{1-\gamma\left(x\right)}\frac{w\left(\nu\left(s,x\right)-\overline{\nu}\left(x\right)\right)}{\varrho\left(x\right)}+\frac{\gamma\left(x\right)\overline{\nu}\left(x\right)}{\pi}-\theta_{1,1}.

These choices are again mean-zero and square integrable, and satisfy
the conditions (
C.9
) and (
C.11
).

Observe that

p_{\varepsilon}\left(x\mid G=1\right)=\frac{\gamma\left(x\right)p_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)}{\pi}.

Thus, by the integral representation (
C.17
)
the pathwise derivative of
\theta_{1,1}
at
0
on the submodel
of
\mathcal{P}
is given by

\theta_{1,1}^{\prime}=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[YU\left(Y,S,X\right)\mid S,X,G=1\right]\mid X,W=1,G=0\right]\mid G=1\right],

where

U\left(y,s,x\right)=\ell^{\prime}\left(y\mid s,x,G=1\right)+\ell^{\prime}\left(s\mid x,W=1,G=0\right)+\ell^{\prime}\left(x\right).

Thus, we have that

\displaystyle\theta_{1,1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[Y\ell^{\prime}\left(Y\mid S,X,G=1\right)\mid S,X,G=1\right]\mid X,W=1,G=0\right]\mid G=1\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\nu\left(S.X\right)\ell^{\prime}\left(S\mid X,W=1,G=0\right)\mid X,W=1,G=0\right]\mid G=1\right]

\displaystyle+\pi^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\overline{\nu}\left(X\right)\gamma\left(X\right)\ell^{\prime}\left(X\right)\right].

Note that the first two lines in this expression are unchanged relative
to (
A.23
). By the choices of
s_{1}\left(\cdot\right)
through
s_{6}\left(\cdot\right)

given above, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\check{\xi}_{1,1}\left(B_{1}\right)\ell^{\prime}\left(B_{1}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[Gs_{1}\left(Y\mid S,X,G=1\right)\ell^{\prime}\left(Y\mid X,S,G=1\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[W\left(1-G\right)s_{3}\left(S\mid X,W=1,G=1\right)\ell^{\prime}\left(Y\mid X,S,G=1\right)\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[s_{4}\left(X\right)\ell^{\prime}\left(X\right)\right].

The equality

\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\check{\xi}_{1,1}\left(B_{1}\right)\ell^{\prime}\left(B_{1}\right)\right]=\theta_{1,1}^{\prime},

follows from the fact that (
C.18
) and (
C.19
)
hold, as
s_{1}\left(\cdot\right)
and
s_{3}\left(\cdot\right)

are unchanged relative to the proof of
Theorem

3.1
, Part 2, and

\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[s_{4}\left(X\right)\ell^{\prime}\left(X\right)\right]=\pi^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\overline{\nu}\left(X\right)\gamma\left(X\right)\ell^{\prime}\left(X\right)\right]

holds by definition, completing the proof.

C.3.
Nested Condition Expectation Estimation

In this section, we derive explicit rates of convergence for estimators of the nested condition expectation functions
\overline{\mu}_{w}(x)
and
\overline{\nu}_{w}(x)
, defined in
Equations

3.2
and
3.4
, based on linear sieves. To simplify exposition, we focus attention on the conditional expectation functions

\displaystyle\mu\left(s,x\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[Y\mid S=s,X=x,G=1\right]\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{\mu}\left(x\right)=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\mu\left(S,x\right)\mid X=x,G=0\right]\penalty\ ,

and let
Z_{i}=(S_{i},X_{i})
collect the short-term outcomes and pretreatment covariates.

C.3.1.
Estimation

We consider the following simple linear sieve estimators for
\mu(\cdot,\cdot)
and
\overline{\mu}(\cdot)
. For functions
h
and
\overline{h}
in
L_{2}\left(P_{Z}\right)
and
L_{2}\left(P_{X}\right)
, define the loss functions

(C.21)

L\left(h,A\right)=G\left(Y-h\left(Z\right)\right)^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{L}\left(h,A\right)=\left(1-G\right)\left(\mu\left(X,S\right)-\overline{h}\left(X\right)\right)^{2}

as well as the population risk functions

(C.22)

R_{P}\left(h\right)=\mathbb{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\left[L\left(h,A\right)\right]\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{R}_{P}\left(h\right)=\mathbb{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\left[\overline{L}\left(h,A\right)\right].

It is clear that the functions
\mu\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)
and
\overline{\mu}\left(\cdot\right)
minimize the risks (
C.22
) over
L^{2}\left(P_{Z}\right)
and
L^{2}\left(P_{X}\right)
.

Throughout, we assume that the functions
\mu(\cdot,\cdot)
and
\overline{\mu}(\cdot)
are
elements of the linear subspaces
\Theta\subseteq L_{2}\left(P_{Z}\right)

and
\overline{\Theta}\subseteq L_{2}\left(P_{X}\right)
, respectively.
Define the sequences of finite-dimensional parameter spaces
\Theta_{1}\subseteq\Theta_{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\Theta

and
\overline{\Theta}_{1}\subseteq\overline{\Theta}_{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\overline{\Theta}
.
Let
\Pi_{m}:\Theta\to\Theta_{m}
denote the
L_{2}\left(P_{Z}\right)

projection of
\Theta
onto
\Theta_{m}
and let
\overline{\Pi}_{m}:\overline{\Theta}\to\overline{\Theta}_{m}

denote the
L_{2}\left(P_{X}\right)
projection of
\overline{\Theta}

onto
\overline{\Theta}_{m}
.

Define the empirical criterion and associated sieve extremum estimator

(C.23)

R_{n}\left(h\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}G_{i}\left(Y-h\left(Z_{i}\right)\right)^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{\mu}_{m,n}=\underset{h\in\Theta_{m}}{\arg\min\text{ }}R_{n}\left(h\right).

Proceeding analogously, define the infeasible empirical criterion
and associated sieve extremum estimator

(C.24)

\overline{R}_{n}\left(\overline{h}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}G_{i}\left(\mu\left(Z_{i}\right)-\overline{h}\left(X_{i}\right)\right)^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{m,n}^{*}=\underset{h\in\Theta_{m}}{\arg\min\text{ }}\overline{R}_{n}\left(\overline{h}\right)

in addition to the feasible empirical criterion and associated sieve
extremum estimator

(C.25)

\overline{R}_{m,n}\left(\overline{h}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(1-G_{i}\right)\left(\hat{\mu}_{m,n}\left(Z_{i}\right)-\overline{h}\left(X_{i}\right)\right)^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{m,n}=\underset{h\in\overline{\Theta}_{m}}{\arg\min\text{ }}\overline{R}_{m,n}\left(\overline{h}\right).

C.3.2.
Consistency

The following assumptions are sufficient to show that the estimators
\hat{\mu}_{m,n}
and
\quad\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{m,n}
are consistent in the norm
\|\cdot\|_{P,2}
for their respective estimands.

Assumption C.1.

(i)
The variables
Z_{i}
have support
\mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{S\times\mathcal{X}},

where
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}

are compact.

(ii)
The sieve spaces
\Theta_{m}
and
\overline{\Theta}_{m}

satisfy

\|\Pi_{m}\left(\mu\right)-\mu\|_{P_{Z},2}\to 0\quad\text{and}\quad\|\overline{\Pi}_{m}\left(\overline{\mu}\right)-\overline{\mu}\|_{P_{X},2}\to 0

as
n\to\infty
.

Consistency follows from
Lemmas

C.7
and
C.8
, which are stated and proved in
Section

C.3.4
.

Theorem C.4.

Under
C.1
, the estimators
\hat{\mu}_{m,n}
and
\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{m,n}
satisfy

\|\hat{\mu}_{m,n}-\mu\|_{P,2}\overset{p}{\to}0\quad\text{and}\quad\|\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{m,n}-\overline{\mu}\|_{P,2}\overset{p}{\to}0,

respectively, as
n,m\to\infty
.

Proof.

By strict convexity, the population risk function
R_{P}\left(h\right)

has a unique minimizer over the convex subspace
\Theta_{m}
, denoted
by
\mu_{m}^{*}
. We have that
\|\hat{\mu}_{m,n}-\mu_{m}^{*}\|_{P,2}\overset{p}{\to}0

as
n\to\infty
and
\|\mu_{m}^{*}-\mu\|_{P,2}\to 0
as
m\to\infty

by Lemmas
C.7
and
C.8
,
respectively. The triangle inequality then gives

\|\hat{\mu}_{m,n}-\mu\|_{P,2}\leq\|\hat{\mu}_{m,n}-\mu_{m}^{*}\|_{P,2}+\|\mu_{m}^{*}-\mu\|_{P,2}\overset{p}{\to}0,

as
m,n\to\infty
. An identical argument establishes the convergence
of
\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{m,n}
.

C.3.3.
Rate of Convergence

Next, derive explicit rates of convergence for the linear sieve estimators
\hat{\mu}_{m,n}
and
\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{m,n}
. We discipline this exercise by assuming that the functions
\mu(\cdot)
and
\overline{\mu}(\cdot)
are elements of Hölder classes with known parameterizations.

To this end, we fix the following notation. If
\mathcal{X}
is a compact set in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
0<\gamma\leq 1
is a constant, then a real-valued function
h
on
\mathcal{X}
is said to satisfy a Hölder condition with exponent
\gamma
if there is a positive number
c
such that
|h\left(x\right)-h\left(y\right)|\leq c\|x-y\|_{2}^{\gamma}
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
. For a
d
-dimensional multi-index
\beta
, let
D^{\beta}
denote the associated Differential operator and let
|\beta|=\beta_{1}+\cdots+\beta_{d}
. A real-valued function
h
on
\mathcal{X}
is said to be
p
-smooth if it is
m
times continuously differentiable on
\mathcal{X}
,
D^{\beta}h
satisfies a Hölder condition with exponent
\gamma
for all
|\beta|=m
, and
m+\gamma=p
. The Hölder class
\Lambda^{p}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
denotes the space of all
p
-smooth functions on
\mathcal{X}
. Let
C^{m}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
denote the space of all
m
-times continuously differentiable functions on
\mathcal{X}
. The Hölder ball on
\mathcal{X}
with width
c
and smoothness
p
is given by

\displaystyle\Lambda_{c}^{p}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{h\in C^{m}\left(\mathcal{X}\right):\sup_{|\beta|\leq m}\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}|D^{\beta}h\left(x\right)|\leq c,\sup_{|\beta|=m}\sup_{x,y\in\mathcal{X},x\neq y}\frac{|D^{\beta}h\left(x\right)-D^{\beta}h\left(y\right)|}{|x-y|_{2}^{\gamma}}|\leq c\right\}.

We make the following assumptions on the regularity of the data, smoothness of the target functions, the quality of their approximation by the chosen linear sieve spaces.

Assumption C.2.

(i)
The moment bounds

(C.26)

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\text{ }\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\left(Y-\mu\left(z\right)\right)^{2}\mid Z=z,G=1\right]

\displaystyle<C\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\text{ }\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\left(\mu\left(S,x\right)-\overline{\mu}\left(x\right)\right)^{2}\mid X=x,G=0\right]

\displaystyle<\overline{C}

hold for
\lambda
-almost every
z\in\mathcal{Z}
and
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
respectively.

(ii)
The densities of
X_{i}
and
Z_{i}
are
uniformly bounded away from zero and infinity.

(iii)
The function classes
\Theta
and
\overline{\Theta}

are given by
\Lambda_{c}^{p}\left(\mathcal{Z}\right)
and
\overline{\mu}\in\Lambda_{\overline{c}}^{\overline{p}}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
,
respectively.

(iv)
The sieve spaces
\Theta_{n}
and
\overline{\Theta}_{n}

are linear and satisfy

\mathsf{dim}\left(\Theta_{n}\right)=J_{n}^{d+k}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{dim}\left(\overline{\Theta}_{n}\right)=J_{n}^{k}

as well as

\inf_{h\in\Theta_{n}}\|h-\mu\|=O\left(J_{n}^{-p}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\inf_{h\in\Theta_{n}}\|h-\overline{\mu}\|=O\left(\overline{J}_{n}^{-\overline{p}}\right)

for some sequences
J_{n}
and
\overline{J}_{n}
, respectively.

The following Theorem, stating rates of convergence for the estimators
\hat{\mu}_{m,n}
and
\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{m,n}
, is a consequence of Theorem 3.2 of
Chen, 2007
. The proof uses Lemma 2 of
Chen and Shen, 1998
, in addition to
Lemmas

C.10
and
C.12
, which are stated and proved in
Section

C.3.4
.

Theorem C.5.

Suppose that
C.1
, (i), and
C.2
hold, and that the sieve spaces
\Theta_{n}
and
\overline{\Theta}_{n}

are chosen such that

(C.27)

J_{n}\asymp\left(\frac{n}{\log n}\right)^{\frac{1}{2p+d+k}}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{J}_{n}\asymp\left(\frac{n}{\log n}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\overline{p}+k}}.

(i)
The estimator
\hat{\mu}_{n,n}
satisfies

(C.28)

\displaystyle\|\hat{\mu}_{n,n}-\mu\|_{P,2}

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{\frac{p}{2p+d+k}}\right)

as
n\to\infty
.

(ii)
If

\frac{\overline{p}}{2\overline{p}+k}\leq\frac{1}{2}\frac{p}{2p+d+k},

then the estimator
\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}
satisfies

(C.29)

\|\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}-\overline{\mu}\|_{P,2}=O_{P}\left(\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{\frac{p}{2p+d+k}}\right)

as
n\to\infty
.

Remark C.1
.

Assumption
C.2
, (iv), will hold if
\Theta_{n}

is the
d+k
tensor product of the space of polynomials of degree

J_{n}
or less. Similar statements hold for different choices of
sieve spaces, e.g., tensor products of univariate splines or orthogonal
wavelets. See e.g., Section 2.3 of
Chen, 2007
for further discussion.

\blacksquare

Proof.

First, we verify the conditions of Theorem 3.2 of
Chen, 2007
, re-stated here as Lemma
C.9
, for the loss functions
L_{P}\left(\cdot,A\right)
and
\overline{L}_{P}\left(\cdot,A\right)
, defined in (
C.21
). These conditions will allow for the application of
Lemma

C.12
in both cases. For this task, we will make frequent use of the functions
K(y,z)
and
\overline{K}(s,x)
, defined in
Lemma

C.10
.

In both cases, Condition (i) follows immediately from the smoothness
and convexity of the population risk functions
R_{P}\left(\cdot\right)

and
\overline{R}_{P}\left(\cdot\right)
about their minimizers.

To verify Condition (ii), fix
h\in\Theta_{n}
such that
\|h-\mu\|_{P,2}\leq\eta
,
and observe that

\displaystyle\Var_{P}\left(L\left(h,A\right)-L\left(\mu,A\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\left(L\left(h,A\right)-L\left(\mu,A\right)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\left(\left[K\left(Y,Z\right)\left(h\left(Z\right)-\mu\left(Z\right)\right)\right]\right)^{2}\right]\lesssim\|h-\mu\|_{p,2}^{2}\lesssim\eta^{2},

as required. Here, the second and third inequalities follow from
Lemma
C.10
. An identical argument verifies Condition
(ii) for the loss
\overline{L}_{P}\left(\cdot,A\right)
.

To verify Condition (iii) of Lemma

C.9
, again fix
h\in\Theta_{n}
with
\|h-\mu\|_{P,2}\leq\eta
,
and observe that

\displaystyle|L\left(h,A\right)-L\left(\mu,A\right)|

\displaystyle\leq K\left(Y,Z\right)\|h-\mu\|_{\infty},

\displaystyle\lesssim K\left(Y,Z\right)\|h-\mu\|_{\lambda,2}^{\frac{2p}{2p+d+k}}\lesssim K\left(Y,Z\right)\|h-\mu\|_{P,2}^{\frac{2p}{2p+d+k}}\penalty\ ,

where the first inequality follows from Lemma
C.10
,
the second inequality follows from Lemma
C.11
,
and the third inequality follows from
C.2
,
Part (ii), which implies that
\|\cdot\|_{\lambda,2}
and
\|\cdot\|_{P,2}

are equivalent norms on
\mathcal{Z}
. Similarly, if
\overline{h}\in\overline{\Theta}_{n}

with
\|\overline{h}-\overline{\mu}\|_{P,2}\leq\eta
, then

\displaystyle|\overline{L}\left(\overline{h},A\right)-\overline{L}\left(\overline{\mu},A\right)|

\displaystyle\leq\overline{K}\left(S,X\right)\|\overline{h}-\overline{\mu}\|_{\infty},

\displaystyle\lesssim\overline{K}\left(S,X\right)\|\overline{h}-\overline{\mu}\|_{\lambda,2}^{\frac{2\overline{p}}{2\overline{p}+k}}\lesssim\overline{K}\left(S,X\right)\|\overline{h}-\overline{\mu}\|_{P,2}^{\frac{2\overline{p}}{2\overline{p}+k}}\penalty\ ,

again by Lemmas
C.10
and
C.11

and
C.2
, Part (ii).

Thus, in the case of
L_{P}\left(\cdot,A\right)
, Condition (iii)
is verified by setting
U\left(A\right)=C\cdot K\left(Y,Z\right)

for some constant
C
and
s=\frac{2p}{2p+d+k}
. Similarly, in the
case of
\overline{L}_{P}\left(\cdot,A\right)
, Condition (iii) is verified
by setting
U\left(A\right)=\overline{C}\cdot\overline{K}\left(S,X\right)

for some constant
\overline{C}
and
s=\frac{2\overline{p}}{2\overline{p}+k}
.
In both cases, the required bound deterministic on
\mathbb{E}\left[U\left(A\right)^{2}\right]

follows from Assumption
C.2
,
Part (i), and the definitions of
K\left(Y,Z\right)
and
\overline{K}\left(S,X\right)
.

Now, we apply
Lemma

C.12
to verify the rate (
C.28
) for the estimator
\hat{\mu}_{n,n}
. Observe that
4.2
, Part (iv) implies
C.1
, Part (ii).
Consequently, we have that
\|\hat{\mu}_{n,n}-\mu\|=o_{P}\left(1\right)
as

n\to\infty
by Theorem
C.4
. Thus, as
\hat{\mu}_{n,m}
is a sequence of estimators that satisfy

\frac{1}{n}R_{n}\left(\hat{\mu}_{n,n}\right)=\sup_{h\in\Theta_{n}}\frac{1}{n}R_{n}\left(h\right)\penalty\ ,

the rate (
C.28
) follows from
4.2
, Part (iv), and Lemma
C.12
.

It remains to verify the rate (
C.29
) for the estimator
\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}
. This result is achieved through an inductive application of
Lemma

C.12
.
First note that,
4.2
, Part (iv) again implies that
\|\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}-\overline{\mu}\|=o_{P}\left(1\right)
as
n\to\infty
by Theorem
C.4
.

Observe that the rate (
C.28
) implies that for any
\overline{h}\in\overline{\Theta}_{n}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\overline{R}_{n}\left(\overline{h}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(1-G_{i}\right)\left(\left(\mu\left(S_{i},X_{i}\right)-\hat{\mu}_{n,n}\left(S_{i},X_{i}\right)\right)+\left(\hat{\mu}_{n,n}\left(S_{i},X_{i}\right)-\overline{h}\left(X_{i}\right)\right)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\overline{R}_{n,n}\left(\overline{h}\right)+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(1-G_{i}\right)\left(\mu\left(S_{i},X_{i}\right)-\hat{\mu}_{n,n}\left(S_{i},X_{i}\right)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(1-G_{i}\right)\left(\mu\left(S_{i},X_{i}\right)-\hat{\mu}_{n,n}\left(S_{i},X_{i}\right)\right)\left(\hat{\mu}_{n,n}\left(S_{i},X_{i}\right)-\overline{h}\left(X_{i}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\overline{R}_{n,n}\left(\overline{h}\right)+O_{P}\left(\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{\frac{2p}{2p+d+k}}\right)

(C.30)

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(1-G_{i}\right)\left(\mu\left(S_{i},X_{i}\right)-\hat{\mu}_{n,n}\left(S_{i},X_{i}\right)\right)\left(\hat{\mu}_{n,n}\left(S_{i},X_{i}\right)-\overline{h}\left(X_{i}\right)\right).

The term (
C.30
) can be written
C_{n}+D_{n}(\overline{h})
, where

\displaystyle C_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(1-G_{i}\right)\left(\mu\left(S_{i},X_{i}\right)-\hat{\mu}_{n,n}\left(S_{i},X_{i}\right)\right)\cdot\left(\hat{\mu}_{n,n}\left(S_{i},X_{i}\right)-\overline{\mu}\left(X_{i}\right)\right)\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle D_{n}(\overline{h})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(1-G_{i}\right)\left(\mu\left(S_{i},X_{i}\right)-\hat{\mu}_{n,n}\left(S_{i},X_{i}\right)\right)\cdot\left(\overline{\mu}\left(X_{i}\right)-\overline{h}\left(X_{i}\right)\right)\penalty\ .

Observe that the term
C_{n}
does not depend on
\overline{h}
. Consequently, if we define the auxillary loss function

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{n}(\overline{b})=\overline{R}_{n}\left(\overline{h}\right)-nC_{n}\penalty\ ,

then the minimization problem is unchanged, in the sense that

\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{m,n}^{*}=\underset{h\in\Theta_{m}}{\arg\min\text{ }}\tilde{R}_{n}\left(\overline{h}\right)\penalty\ ,

as in (
C.24
), and we obtain the expansion

(C.31)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\tilde{R}_{n}\left(\overline{h}\right)=\frac{1}{n}\overline{R}_{n,n}\left(\overline{h}\right)+D_{n}(\overline{h})+O_{P}\left(\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{\frac{2p}{2p+d+k}}\right)\penalty\ ,

from the expression (
C.30
).

Observe that the relations

(C.32)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\overline{R}_{n,n}\left(\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\tilde{R}_{n}\left(\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}\right)-D_{n}(\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n})+O_{P}\left(\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{\frac{2p}{2p+d+k}}\right),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\overline{R}_{n,n}\left(\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{n}\overline{R}_{n,n}\left(\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}^{*}\right),\quad\text{and}

(C.33)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\overline{R}_{n,n}\left(\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\tilde{R}_{n}\left(\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}^{*}\right)-D_{n}(\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}^{*})+O_{P}\left(\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{\frac{2p}{2p+d+k}}\right),

where
\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}
is defined in (
C.24
),
imply the inequality

(C.34)

\frac{1}{n}\overline{R}_{n}\left(\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}\right)\geq\frac{1}{n}\overline{R}_{n}\left(\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}^{*}\right)+\left(D_{n}(\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n})-D_{n}(\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}^{*})\right)+O_{P}\left(\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{\frac{2p}{2p+d+k}}\right)\penalty\ .

Moreover, the term
D_{n}(\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}^{*}))
is bounded from above by

(C.35)

\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\mu\left(S_{i},X_{i}\right)-\hat{\mu}_{n,n}\left(S_{i},X_{i}\right)\|_{2}\|\overline{\mu}\left(X_{i}\right)-\overline{h}\left(X_{i}\right)\|_{2}=O_{P}\left(\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{\frac{p}{2p+d+k}+\frac{\overline{p}}{2\overline{p}+k}}\right)\penalty\ ,

by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, the rate (
C.28
), and the choice (
C.27
). Plugging this bound into (
C.34
) implies

(C.36)

\frac{1}{n}\overline{R}_{n}\left(\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}\right)\geq\frac{1}{n}\overline{R}_{n}\left(\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}^{*}\right)+D_{n}(\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n})+O_{P}\left(\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{\frac{p}{2p+d+k}+\min\big\{\frac{p}{2p+d+k},\frac{\overline{p}}{2\overline{p}+k}\big\}}\right)\penalty\ .

Thus, in order to apply
Lemma

C.12
, must must control the term
D_{n}(\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n})
.

To this end, suppose that it were known that

(C.37)

\displaystyle\|\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}-\overline{\mu}\|_{2,P}=O_{P}\left(\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{s}\right)

for some constant
0\leq s<1
. In this case, the term
D_{n}(\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n})
is similarly bounded from above by

(C.38)

\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\mu\left(S_{i},X_{i}\right)-\hat{\mu}_{n,n}\left(S_{i},X_{i}\right)\|_{2}\|\overline{\mu}\left(X_{i}\right)-\overline{h}\left(X_{i}\right)\|_{2}=O_{P}\left(\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{\frac{p}{2p+d+k}+s}\right)\penalty\ ,

by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the rate (
C.28
), which again implies

(C.39)

\frac{1}{n}\overline{R}_{n}\left(\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}\right)\geq\frac{1}{n}\overline{R}_{n}\left(\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}^{*}\right)+O_{P}\left(\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{\frac{p}{2p+d+k}+\min\big\{s,\frac{p}{2p+d+k},\frac{\overline{p}}{2\overline{p}+k}\big\}}\right)\penalty\ .

Hence, by
C.2
, Part (iv), Lemma
C.12
implies that

(C.40)

\displaystyle\|\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}-\overline{\mu}\|_{2,P}=O_{P}\left(\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{r(s)}\right)\penalty\ ,

where

(C.41)

\displaystyle r(s)=\max\bigg\{\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{p}{2p+d+k}+\min\bigg\{s,\frac{p}{2p+d+k},\frac{\overline{p}}{2\overline{p}+k}\bigg\}\right),\frac{\overline{p}}{2\overline{p}+k}\bigg\}\penalty\ .

Observe that we have shown
\overline{\mu}_{n,n}
converges to
\overline{\mu}
in
\|\cdot\|_{2,P}
at rate
s
, then it also converges at the rate
r(s)
.

To conclude the proof, observe that (
C.37
) must be satisfied by
s=0
, as
\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{n,n}
and
\overline{\mu}
are subsets of
\Lambda^{\overline{p}}_{\overline{c}(\mathcal{X})}
. Thus, if we define the sequence
(s_{i})_{i=0}^{\infty}
by
s_{i}=r(s_{i-1})
, with initial condition
s_{0}=1
, we find that

\lim_{i\to\infty}s_{i}=\frac{p}{2p+d+k}\quad\text{if}\quad\frac{\overline{p}}{2\overline{p}+k}\leq\frac{1}{2}\frac{p}{2p+d+k}\penalty\ ,

and so the rate of covergence (
C.29
) is obtained by induction.

C.3.4.
Auxiliary Lemmas

Lemma C.6
(
Newey and McFadden, 1994
, Theorem 2.7,
)
.

If there is a function
Q_{0}\left(\theta\right)

defined on a convex set
\Theta
such that (i)
Q_{0}\left(\theta\right)

is uniquely minimized at
\theta_{0}
, (ii)
\theta_{0}
is an element
of the interior of
\Theta
, (ii) the function
\hat{Q}_{n}\left(\theta\right)

defined on
\Theta
is concave, and (iv)
\hat{Q}_{n}\left(\theta\right)\to Q_{0}\left(\theta\right)

for all
\theta\in\Theta
, then

\hat{\theta}_{n}=\arg\max_{\theta\in\Theta}\text{ }\hat{Q}_{n}\left(\theta\right)

exists with probability one and
\hat{\theta}_{n}\overset{p}{\to}\theta_{0}

as
n\to\infty
.

Lemma C.7.

Under
C.1
, the estimators
\hat{\mu}_{m,n}
and
\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{m,n}
satisfy

\|\hat{\mu}_{m,n}-\mu_{m}^{*}\|_{P,2}\overset{p}{\to}0\quad\text{and}\quad\|\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{m,n}-\overline{\mu}_{m}^{*}\|_{P,2}\overset{p}{\to}0

as
n\to\infty
and
m
is sufficiently large, respectively.

Proof.

It will suffice to verify the conditions of Lemma
C.6
.
Note first that both
\Theta_{m}
and
\overline{\Theta}_{m}
are convex.
The population criteria
R_{P}\left(\cdot\right)
and
\overline{R}_{P}\left(\cdot\right)

are both uniquely minimized at
\mu_{m}^{*}
and
\overline{\mu}_{m}^{*}

over
\Theta_{m}
and
\overline{\Theta}_{m}
, respectively, by convexity.
Both
\mu_{m}^{*}
and
\overline{\mu}_{m}^{*}
are elements of the interiors
of
\Theta_{m}
and
\overline{\Theta}_{m}
for sufficiently large
m

by Lemma
C.8
. It is clear that the
functions
R_{n}\left(h\right)
and
\overline{R}_{m,n}\left(\overline{h}\right)

are strictly convex.

The pointwise convergence

R_{n}\left(h\right)\overset{\text{p}}{\to}R_{p}\left(h\right)

as
n\to\infty
for
m
sufficiently large follows from the weak
law of large numbers, and so

(C.42)

\|\hat{\mu}_{m,n}-\mu_{m}^{*}\|_{P,2}\overset{p}{\to}0

as
n\to\infty
for
m
sufficiently large follows from Lemma
C.6
.
Consequently, the pointwise convergence

\overline{R}_{n}\left(\overline{h}\right)\overset{\text{p}}{\to}\overline{R}_{p}\left(\overline{h}\right)

follows from (
C.42
) and the weak law of large numbers,
again giving that
\|\hat{\overline{\mu}}_{m,n}-\overline{\mu}_{m}^{*}\|_{P,2}\overset{p}{\to}0

as
n\to\infty
for
m
sufficiently large by Lemma
C.6
.

Lemma C.8.

Under
C.1
, the unique minimizers
\mu_{m}^{*}
and
\overline{\mu}_{n}^{*}
of
R_{P}\left(h\right)
and
\overline{R}_{P}\left(h\right)

over
\Theta_{m}
and
\overline{\Theta}_{m}
satisfy

\|\mu_{m}^{*}-\mu\|_{P,2}\to 0\quad\text{and}\quad\|\overline{\mu}_{n}^{*}-\overline{\mu}\|_{P,2}\to 0

as
m\to\infty
, respectively.

Proof.

We prove only
\|\mu_{m}^{*}-\mu\|_{P,2}\to 0
as
m\to\infty
, as
the argument for
\|\overline{\mu}_{n}^{*}-\overline{\mu}\|_{P,2}\to 0
as
m\to\infty

is identical. Assume, by the way of contradiction, that there exists
some
\delta>0
such that
\|\mu_{m}^{*}-\mu\|_{P,2}>\delta
for
all
m
greater than some integer
m_{1}
. As
R_{P}\left(\cdot\right)

is strictly convex on
\Theta
, we have that

(C.43)

R_{P}\left(\mu_{m}^{*}\right)>R_{P}\left(\mu\right)+\varepsilon

for all
m\geq\max\left\{m_{0},m_{1}\right\}
for some
\varepsilon>0
.
By the continuity of
R_{P}\left(\cdot\right)
, there exists some

\delta^{\prime}>0
such that
\|\mu^{\prime}-\mu\|_{P,2}<\delta^{\prime}

implies

(C.44)

R_{P}\left(\mu^{\prime}\right)<R_{P}\left(\mu\right)+\varepsilon.

By
C.1
, (ii), there exists some integer
m_{1}

such that
\|\Pi_{m}\left(\mu\right)-\mu\|_{P,2}<\delta^{\prime}

for all
m\geq m_{1}
. Thus, by (
C.44
) and (
C.43
),
we have that

R_{P}\left(\Pi_{m}\left(\mu\right)\right)<R_{P}\left(\mu\right)+\varepsilon<R_{P}\left(\mu_{m}^{*}\right),

for all
m\geq\max\left\{m_{0},m_{1}\right\}
, giving a contradiction,
as
\mu_{m}^{*}
minimizes
R_{P}\left(\cdot\right)
over
\Theta_{m}
.

Lemma C.9
(
Chen, 2007
, Theorem 3.2,
)
.

Let
\left\{X_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
be
an independent sequence of random variables with an identical distribution

P
. Fix a sieve
\Theta_{1}\subseteq\Theta_{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\Theta
.
For some loss function
\ell\left(x,\theta\right)
, let
\theta^{*}\in\Theta

be the population risk minimizer

\theta^{*}=\underset{\theta\in\Theta}{\arg\min}\text{ }\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\ell\left(\theta,X_{i}\right)\right]

and let
\|\cdot\|
be some norm on
\Theta
. Define the function
class

\mathcal{F}_{n}=\left\{\ell\left(\theta,\cdot\right)-\ell\left(\theta^{*},\cdot\right):\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|\leq\delta,\theta\in\Theta_{n}\right\}.

For some constant
b>0
, let

\delta_{n}=\underset{\delta\in\left(0,1\right)}{\inf}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\delta^{2}}\int_{b\delta^{2}}^{\delta}\sqrt{H_{[]}\left(w,\mathcal{F}_{n},\|\cdot\|\right)}\text{d}w\leq 1,

where
H_{[]}\left(w,\mathcal{F}_{n},\|\cdot\|\right)
is the
L^{r}\left(P\right)

metric entropy with bracketing of the class
\mathcal{F}_{n}
. Assume
that the following conditions hold:

(i)
There exists constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such
that

c_{1}\mathbb{E}\left[\ell\left(\theta,X_{i}\right)-\ell\left(\theta^{*},X_{i}\right)\right]\leq\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|^{2}\leq c_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\ell\left(\theta,X_{i}\right)-\ell\left(\theta^{*},X_{i}\right)\right]

for any
\theta\in\Theta
.

(ii)
There is a constant
C_{1}>0
such that for
sufficiently small
\eta>0
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{n}:\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|\leq\eta}\Var\left(\ell\left(\theta,X_{i}\right)-\ell\left(\theta^{*},X_{i}\right)\right)\leq C_{1}\eta^{2}.

(iii)
For any
\eta>0
, there exists a constant

s\in\left(0,2\right)
such that

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{n}:\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|\leq\eta}|\ell\left(\theta,X_{i}\right)-\ell\left(\theta^{*},X_{i}\right)|\leq\eta^{s}U\left(X_{i}\right)

with
\mathbb{E}\left[U\left(Z_{i}\right)^{\gamma}\right]\leq C_{2}

for some
\gamma\geq 2
.

If
\hat{\theta}_{n}
is a sequence of estimators satisfying

(C.45)

\displaystyle\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta^{*}\|

\displaystyle=o_{P}\left(1\right),

(C.46)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell\left(\hat{\theta}_{n},X_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{n}}\frac{1}{n}\sum\ell\left(\theta,X_{i}\right)+O_{P}\left(\varepsilon_{n}^{2}\right),\quad and

(C.47)

\displaystyle\max\left\{\delta_{n},\inf_{\theta\in\Theta_{n}}\|\theta^{*}-\theta\|\right\}

\displaystyle=O\left(\varepsilon_{n}\right),

as
n\to\infty
for some sequence
\varepsilon_{n}
, then
\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta^{*}\|=O_{P}\left(\varepsilon_{n}\right)

as
n\to\infty
.

Lemma C.10.

Suppose that
C.2
holds.

(i)
There exists a function
K\left(Y,Z\right)

and a constant
C
such that such that

|L\left(h,a\right)-L\left(\mu,a\right)|\leq K\left(y,z\right)|h\left(z\right)-\mu\left(z\right)|,

for each
a=\left(y,z,g\right)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{S}\times\left\{0,1\right\}
,
where

\sup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\text{ }\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[K\left(Y,Z\right)^{2}\mid Z=z\right]\leq C.

(ii)
There exists a function
\overline{K}\left(S,Z\right)

and a constant
\overline{C}
such that such that

|\overline{L}\left(\overline{h},a\right)-\overline{L}\left(\overline{\mu},a\right)|\leq\overline{K}\left(s,z\right)|\overline{h}\left(x\right)-\overline{\mu}\left(x\right)|,

for each
a=\left(y,z,g\right)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{S}\times\left\{0,1\right\}
,
where

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\text{ }\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\overline{K}\left(S,X\right)^{2}\mid X=x\right]\leq\overline{C}.

Proof.

We provide the details of the verification of the first claim, as
verification of the second claim follows by an identical argument.
For any
h\in\Lambda_{c}^{p}\left(\mathcal{Z}\right)
and
z\in\mathcal{Z}
,
we have that

\displaystyle|L\left(h,a\right)-L\left(\mu,a\right)|

\displaystyle=g|\left(y-h\left(z\right)\right)^{2}-\left(y-\mu\left(z\right)\right)^{2}|

\displaystyle=g\cdot|2\left(y-\lambda\left(z\right)h\left(z\right)+\left(1-\lambda\left(z\right)\right)\mu\left(z\right)\right)|\cdot|h\left(z\right)-\mu\left(z\right)|

for some function
\lambda\left(z\right)\in\left[0,1\right]
, by
the Mean Value Theorem. Thus, we can set

K\left(y,z\right)=g\cdot|2\left(y-\lambda\left(z\right)h\left(z\right)+\left(1-\lambda\left(z\right)\right)\mu\left(z\right)\right)|.

We can verify that, for some constant
C
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[K\left(Y,Z\right)^{2}\mid Z=z\right]

\displaystyle=4\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[G\left(Y-\lambda\left(Z\right)h\left(Z\right)+\left(1-\lambda\left(Z\right)\right)\mu\left(Z\right)\right)^{2}\mid Z=z\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[G\left(\left(Y-\mu\left(Z\right)\right)-\lambda\left(Z\right)\left(h\left(Z\right)-\mu\left(Z\right)\right)\right)^{2}\mid Z=z\right]\leq C

for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, by the bound (
C.26
)
in Assumption
C.2
and the fact
that both
h\left(z\right)
and
\mu\left(z\right)
are in
\Lambda_{c}^{p}\left(\mathcal{Z}\right)
,
and are therefore bounded on
\mathcal{Z}
.

Lemma C.11
(
Chen and Shen, 1998
, Lemma 2,
)
.

If
\mathcal{X}
is a compact subset
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
h\in\Lambda_{c}^{p}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
,
then

\|h\|_{\infty}\leq 2\|h\|_{2}^{\frac{2p}{2p+d}}c^{1-\frac{2p}{2p+d}},

where
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
denotes the
L_{\infty}
norm and
\|\cdot\|_{2}

denotes the
L_{2}
norm under the Lebesgue measure.

Lemma C.12.

Continue the notation of Lemma
C.9
.
Assume that the conditions (i)-(iii) of Lemma
C.9

are satisfied. Suppose that the function class
\Theta=\Lambda_{c}^{p}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
,
where
\mathcal{X}
is some compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
that the sieve spaces
\Theta_{n}
are linear and satisfy

(C.48)

\mathsf{dim}\left(\Theta_{n}\right)\asymp\left(\frac{n}{\log n}\right)^{\frac{d}{2p+d}}\quad\text{and}\quad\inf_{h\in\Theta_{n}}\|h-\mu\|\asymp\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{\frac{p}{2p+d}}.

If
\hat{\theta}_{n}
is a sequence of estimators satisfying the
consistency condition (
C.45
) and optimization condition
(
C.46
) for the sequence
\varepsilon_{n}
,
then

(C.49)

\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta^{*}\|_{P,2}=O_{P}\left(\max\left\{\varepsilon_{n},\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{\frac{p}{2p+d}}\right\}\right)

as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

As the conditions of Lemma
C.9
are satisfied, we
have that

\displaystyle\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta^{*}\|_{P,2}

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\max\left\{\varepsilon_{n},\delta_{n},\inf_{h\in\Theta_{n}}\|h-\mu\|\right\}\right)

(C.50)

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\max\left\{\varepsilon_{n},\delta_{n},\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{\frac{p}{2p+d}}\|\right\}\right),

where

\delta_{n}=\underset{\delta\in\left(0,1\right)}{\inf}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\delta^{2}}\int_{b\delta^{2}}^{\delta}\sqrt{H_{[]}\left(w,\mathcal{F}_{n},\|\cdot\|_{2}\right)}\text{d}w\leq 1

and
H_{[]}\left(w,\mathcal{F}_{n},\|\cdot\|_{2}\right)
is the
L^{2}\left(P\right)

metric entropy with bracketing of the function class

\mathcal{F}_{n}=\left\{L\left(h,\cdot\right)-L\left(\mu,\cdot\right):\|h-\mu\|_{P,2}\leq\delta,h\in\Theta_{n}\right\}.

Now, Condition (iii) of
Lemma

C.9
implies that

H_{[]}\left(w,\mathcal{F}_{n},\|\cdot\|_{2}\right)\leq\log N\left(w^{1+\frac{d}{2p}},\Theta_{n},\|\cdot\|_{P,2}\right),

where
N\left(w,\mathcal{F}_{n},\|\cdot\|_{2}\right)
is the covering
number of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
. Moreover, we have that

\log N\left(w^{1+\frac{d}{2p}},\mathcal{F}_{n},\|\cdot\|_{2}\right)\lesssim\mathsf{dim}\left(\Theta_{n}\right)\log\left(\frac{1}{w}\right)

as
\Theta_{n}
is an element of a finite dimensional linear sieve
(see e.g., Section 3.2 of
Chen, 2007
). Thus, we have
that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\delta^{2}}\int_{b\delta^{2}}^{\delta}\sqrt{H_{[]}\left(w,\mathcal{F}_{n},\|\cdot\|_{2}\right)}\text{d}w

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\delta^{2}}\int_{b\delta^{2}}^{\delta}\sqrt{\log N\left(w^{1+\frac{d}{2p}},\Theta_{n},\|\cdot\|_{P,2}\right)}\text{d}w

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta}\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{dim}\left(\Theta_{n}\right)}{n}\log\frac{1}{\delta}}

and that therefore

(C.51)

\delta_{n}=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{dim}\left(\Theta_{n}\right)\log n}{n}}\right)=O\left(\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{\frac{p}{2p+d}}\right)

by Assumption
C.2
and the choice (
C.48
). The proof is then complete by plugging the rate (
C.51
) into the bound (
C.50
).

Appendix D
Simulation

D.1.
Data

The data from
Banerjee et al., 2015
were acquired from
https://dataverse.harvard.edu/dataset.xhtml?persistentId=doi:10.7910/DVN/NHIXNT
on September 10, 2021. We restrict attention to data from the program evaluation in Pakistan. Prior to cleaning, there are
1299
households in this sample. We omit data from
399
households who attrited from the sample prior to either of the post-treatment outcome measurements. We also omit data from 46 households who were missing measurements of all consumption variables for either of the post-treatment outcome measurements.
Consequently, we obtain a cleaned sample of 854 households. Of these households, 446 were randomly assigned to participate in the program. There was perfect compliance with treatment assignment.

Some of the individuals are missing measurements for some of the variables. There are at most
14
individuals missing a measurement of a given variable. For most continuously valued variables, we impute missing values with the mean value of that outcome. Five of the continuously valued variables are heavily censored, meaning that more than 50% of the observations are equal to zero.
20
20

20

These variables are total informal loans outstanding, total formal loans outstanding, savings deposit amount, income from agriculture, income from business, and revenue from animals
We impute missing values with zero for these variables. For categorical or binary valued variables, we impute missing values with the modal outcome of that variable.

We include variables belonging in five categories: consumption, food security, assets, finance, and income and revenue. The consumption variables are the total monthly consumption and the total monthly consumptions on food, non-food, and durable commodities. The food-security variables are an index for overall food-security and five binary variables indicating different aspects of food security (e.g., did a child in the household skip a meal). The assets variables are value of assets, the value of productive assets, and value of the household’s assets, each aggregated in two ways. The financial variables are the total amount of formal and informal loans outstanding and the total value of the households savings. The income and revenue variables are the income from agriculture, the income from business, the income from paid labor, the revenue from animals, and the self-assessed perception of economic status. We do not include the total income from business as a baseline covariate, as it is equal to zero for all individuals in the sample. We refer the reader to the appendix of
Banerjee et al., 2015
for further information on the construction of these variables.

D.2.
Simulation Calibration

We calibrate our simulation to the
Banerjee et al., 2015
data with GANs. A GAN is composed of a pair of competing neural networks
(
Goodfellow et al., 2014
)
. The objective of one neural network is to generate data similar to the training data. The objective of the other neural network is to distinguish between the true and generated data. These networks iteratively compete until the true and generated data are difficult to distinguish.

There are three GANs that contribute to generating a data point
A_{i}
. The first GAN is fit to the marginal distribution of the pre-treatment covariates (i.e.,
X_{i}
). The second GAN is fit to the distribution of the short-term outcomes conditioned on pre-treatment covariates and treatment (i.e.,
S_{i}|X_{i},W_{i}
). The third GAN takes two forms, depending on whether we are generating data designed to satisfy the assumptions in the Latent Unconfounded Treatment or Statistical Surrogacy Models. For the Latent Unconfounded Treatment Model, the third GAN is fit to the distribution of the long-term outcome conditioned on the short-term outcomes, the pre-treatment covariates, and treatment (i.e.,
Y_{i}|S_{i},X_{i},W_{i}
). For the Statistical Surrogacy Model, the analogous distribution does not condition on treatment. In the Statistical Surrogacy Model treatment is assumed to be independent of
Y_{i}
, conditional on
S_{i}
and
X_{i}
, by
2.5
. By contrast, in the Latent Unconfounded Treatment Model, treatment is only independent of the potential outcomes
Y_{i}(w)
, conditional on
S_{i}
and
X_{i}
, by
2.4
.

We estimate the GANs needed to calibrate our simulation with the “wgan” python package associated with
Athey et al., 2021
. This package is available at
https://github.com/gsbDBI/ds-wgan
. Table
1
displays the choices of tuning parameters used for this calibration. All other tuning parameter choices are set to the wgan defaults.

Tuning Parameter Choices in GAN Estimation

Notes: Table
1
displays tuning parameter choices for the GAN estimation using the wgan package associated with
Athey et al., 2021
. Any tuning parameter choices not listed are set to the wgan defaults.

Figure

3
compares the moments of the data from
Banerjee et al., 2015
to the GAN generated simulation data. The first two rows compare the means and variances of these data sets, and are displayed in log-scale. The second two rows compare correlations within and across variable types. For example, the third row compares the correlations of the pre-treatment covariates in the true and generated data, and the fourth row compares the correlations of the pre-treatment covariates and short-term outcomes. Note that in this comparison the long-term outcomes
Y_{i}
includes both total assets, chosen as the long-term outcome in the main text, and total consumption, chosen as the long-term outcome in
Section

D.5
.

Notes:
Figure

D.4
displays scatterplots comparing the moments of the data from
Banerjee et al., 2015
to the GAN generated simulation data. Columns differentiate between different types of variables. In the first three rows, the columns correspond to the pre-treatment covariates, the short-term outcomes, and the long-term outcomes, respectively. The columns of the fourth row correspond to the three pairs of these three variable types. Rows differentiate between different types of moments. The first two rows compare the means and variances of the the true and generated data. The second two rows compare correlations of the true and generated data, within and across variable types, respectively. The x-axis of each sub-panel measures the moments of the true data. The y-axis of each sub-panel measures the moments of the generated data. The x and y axes in the first two rows are displayed in log-scale. A forty-five degree line is displayed in all sub-panels. Triangular and square dots correspond to data generated the Latent Unconfounded Treatment and Statistical Surrogacy Models, respectively. Circular dots correspond to data shared by both models. Blue and green dots denote moments conditioned on treatment being set to one and zero, respectively. Black dots denote unconditioned moments.

The joint distributions of the true and generated data match remarkably closely. This match holds both for data generated for the contexts in which treatment is and is not observed in the observational sample, and continues to hold if moments are conditioned on the treatment assignment being set to one and zero, respectively.

D.5
displays histograms comparing the true and generated distributions for the long-term outcomes, conditioned on treatment assignment. These distributions again align remarkably closely. In each case, the distributions have similarly shaped supports and right tails.

D.3.
Design

To generate an observation
A_{i}
, we first generate a value of the pre-treatment covariates
X_{i}
from the first GAN. This generated value of
X_{i}
is then input into the second GAN to generate values of the treated and untreated short-term potential outcomes, i.e., generating
S_{i}(1)
and
S_{i}(0)
by taking draws from the GAN approximating the distributions of
S_{i}|X_{i},W_{i}=1
and
S_{i}|X_{i},W_{i}=0
. We repeat this step, using the generated values
(X_{i},S(1))
and
(X_{i},S_{i}(0))
as inputs into the third GAN to generate the long-term treated and untreated potential outcomes
Y_{i}(1)
and
Y_{i}(0)
, respectively. Each observation is assigned to being either “experimental” or “observational” uniformly at random, i.e., we draw each
G_{i}
from a Bernoulli distribution with parameter
1/2
. Treatment is always assigned uniformly at random for experimental observations.

We consider two procedures for assigning treatment for the observational units. First, as a baseline, we assign treatment uniformly at random, leaving treatment assignment unconfounded. Second, we induce confounding. Specifically, we assign treatment with probability

\frac{1}{1+\exp(-(\tilde{\tau}_{c,i}+\tilde{\tau}_{a,i})\cdot\phi)}

where
\tilde{\tau}_{c,i}
and
\tilde{\tau}_{a,i}
denote the true treatment effects on short-term total consumption and total assets for unit
i
, normalized across observational units, and
\phi
is a parameter indexing the degree of confounding. Written differently, the probability of being assigned treatment increases logistically with the true treatment effects on short-term outcomes. Large values of
\phi
induce more confounding.

By repeating this process, we generate ten million simulation draws. We take the empirical distribution of this sample as the population distribution. In this sample, we observe both treated and untreated potential outcomes, and, thereby, the true treatment effects for each individual. It is worth noting the significant computational scale of the experiment reported in this section. The simulation requires parallelization on a large-scale computing cluster and uses roughly 60 years of cpu time.

Panel A of
Figure

D.6
plots the probability that an observational unit is assigned to treatment as a function of
\tilde{\tau}_{c,i}
and
\tilde{\tau}_{a,i}
, the true treatment effects on short-term total consumption and total assets for unit
i
, normalized across observational units. This curve is displayed for
\phi
equal to
0.50
and
0.66
, which are the parameter values that we consider in out simulation analysis. Panels B and C display histograms of the treatment probabilities for all of the simulation draws for
\phi
equal to
0.50
and
0.66
. Observe that increasing
\phi
increases the confounding in the data, in the sense that there are more simulation draws with propensity scores very different from one half.

Figure D.6.
Visualization of Confounding Parameterization

Panel A: Parameterization of Confounding

Panel B: Histogram of Propensities,
\phi=0.5

Panel C: Histogram of Propensities,
\phi=0.66

Figure

displays curves giving the
probability of a simulation draw being assigned to treatment as a function
of
\tilde{\tau}_{c,i}
and
\tilde{\tau}_{a,i}
, the true treatment effects on short-term total consumption and total assets for unit
i
, normalized across observational units. Panels B and C display histograms of the treatment probabilities for all simulation draws for
\phi
equal to
0.50
and
0.66
, respectively.

D.4.
Estimation Details

Cross-fitting:

Following the choice made in
Chernozhukov et al., 2018
, we set
k
equal to five for the
k
-fold cross-fitting construction. We expect that alternative choices for this hyper-parameter will not impact the results of the simulation substantially, and so do not report results for different choices of
k
.

Propensity Score Estimation:

For the sake of numerical stability across simulations, we threshold all estimates of propensity scores to lie in
[0.05,0.95]
, which amounts to enforcing a value of
\varepsilon
chosen for the strict overlap
2.2
in the estimation of propensity scores.
21
21

21

When estimating the propensity score
\rho_{w}(s,x)
, we trim estimates of each of the components in (
3.9
) in addition to the final estimate
\hat{\rho}_{w}(s,x)
constructed by combining these estimates according to (
3.9
).
The choice of this hyper-parameter may substantively impact results in cases, e.g., in which the covariate distributions of the experimental and observational distributions have limited overlap. We do not report results for alternative choices of these hyperparameters, but view the adaptation of the methods developed in
Section

4
to setting in which there is limited overlap along one or more direction as a useful direction for further research.

Linear Regression:

We use ordinary linear regression and a generalized linear model with a binomial response type when estimating outcome mean and propensity score type nuisance functions, respectively.

Lasso:

We implement cross-validated Lasso
(
Tibshirani, 1996
)
with the R package “glmnet’
(
Hastie and Qian, 2014
)
. In each cross-fitting fold, we use
5
-fold cross-validation to choose the regularization parameter that gives the minimum mean cross-validation error. We use the binomial response type when estimating propensity scores.

GRF:

We implement generalized random forests
(
Athey et al., 2019
)
with the R package “grf”. When
h\in\{1,3\}
, we set the “honesty” hyper-parameter equal to false. In each cross-fitting fold, we choose the hyper-parameters “sample.fraction”, “mtry”, and “min.node.size” by setting the grf hyper-parameter “tune.parameters” equal to “all”. This option chooses
1000
hyper-parameter values randomly, then chooses the hyper-parameter values with the minimum 5-fold cross-validation error. When
h=10
, we set “sample.fraction” equal to 0.1 in order to optimize memory usage, but tuned the other parameters in the same manner. We use regression forests to predict both long-term outcome means and propensity scores.

XGBoost:

We implement XGboost
(
Chen and Guestrin, 2016
)
with the R package “xgboost”
(
Chen et al., 2015
)
. We set the learning rate
\eta
and max depth equal to
0.1
and
2
, respectively. We choose the number of rounds with 5-fold cross validation, with the maximum number of rounds set to
100
. We use the root mean squared error and log-loss as the evaluation metrics for the estimation of long-term outcome means and propensity scores, respectively.

D.7

Tables
2
and
3
present estimates of coverage probabilities of confidence intervals constructed around the estimators formulated in
4.1
. Wald intervals constructed around the difference in means estimator severely undercover when there is confounding in the observational data set. The confidence intervals (
A.42
) tend to undercover, although the under-coverage is not as severe. Coverage is worse under the Statistical Surrogacy Model and is very poor when generalized random forests are used to estimate nuisance parameters. This is a result of the bias exhibited in
Figure

3
and
Figure

D.8
. By contrast, in the same situation, the coverage probabilities are much closer to the nominal level when nuisance parameters are estimated with the Lasso or XGBoost. As an exception, these intervals over cover if nuisance parameters are estimated with linear regression.
